#5090. DeFi Explained

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Understanding How Decentralized Finance Protocols Work

DeFi, or decentralized finance, works through smart contracts that coordinate financial activity on-chain. Instead of relying on a centralized intermediary, DeFi protocols use liquidity pools, automated market makers, collateral rules, oracles, governance systems, and composable protocol integrations to support trading, lending, yield strategies, derivatives, and other financial functions.

DeFi 201 is intended for readers who already understand crypto basics and want to understand how DeFi protocols actually work. It moves beyond beginner definitions and into the systems that keep decentralized finance running: exchanges, lending markets, yield strategies, oracle networks, derivatives, governance, and composability.

DeFi is best understood as a connected protocol system rather than a list of isolated apps. Its main components include decentralized exchanges, lending protocols, yield aggregators, oracles, decentralized derivatives, governance, and composability.

how decentralized exchanges enable on-chain trading through order books, AMMs, liquidity pools, and intent-based execution;

how DeFi lending markets use collateral, pooled liquidity, utilization, interest rates, and liquidations;

how yield aggregators use vaults, strategy routing, managers, and keepers to automate DeFi strategies;

why oracles are critical for prices, collateral values, liquidations, derivatives, and synthetic assets;

how decentralized derivatives create synthetic exposure through collateral, margin, funding, and liquidation mechanics;

how DAOs and governance tokens coordinate proposals, voting, delegation, treasuries, and protocol upgrades;

how composability lets protocols build on each other while creating dependency and systemic-risk trade-offs.

Explain how DEX architectures route trades and allocate liquidity.

Describe the mechanics and risks of DeFi lending protocols.

Explain the role of vaults and automated yield strategies.

Identify oracle dependencies and manipulation risk.

Describe how DeFi protocols package derivative exposure without centralized intermediaries.

Explain how DAOs coordinate protocol upgrades and incentives.

Analyze both the benefits and risks of DeFi composability.

DeFi means decentralized finance. It refers to financial protocols that run through blockchains and smart contracts rather than traditional intermediaries. In DeFi, users can trade, lend, borrow, supply liquidity, manage collateral, vote on protocol changes, or build products by connecting multiple protocols together.

The important idea is not that DeFi removes every kind of intermediation. It changes where coordination happens. Rules that would usually be enforced by a platform, broker, exchange, or bank can be encoded into smart contracts, governance systems, oracle feeds, and liquidity mechanisms.

Most DeFi protocols combine a few common building blocks: users, smart contracts, liquidity providers, traders, borrowers, lenders, governance participants, and external data providers. The exact mix depends on the protocol type.

Smart contracts: Encode the rules for trading, lending, collateral, fees, voting, or settlement.

Liquidity pools: Hold assets that traders or borrowers interact with.

Liquidity providers: Supply assets to pools and take protocol-specific risks.

Oracles: Bring external data, such as prices, into smart contracts.

Governance: Coordinates upgrades, parameters, incentives, and treasury decisions.

Composability: Lets protocols plug into each other and create new products.

Decentralized exchanges allow users to trade on-chain through smart contracts. Early exchange design borrowed from order books, where buyers and sellers post bids and asks. But on-chain order books can be expensive and capital-inefficient because each order update consumes gas and liquidity may sit idle.

Automated market makers changed that model. In an AMM, traders do not need to match with another person’s order. They trade against a shared liquidity pool supplied by liquidity providers. A liquidity pool is a smart contract that holds tokens, and an algorithmic formula determines prices based on the pool’s reserves.

The constant product AMM model uses the formula x times y equals k. It keeps the product of the two token reserves constant, so prices move automatically as traders buy one asset and add another. This model is simple and resilient, but it spreads liquidity across all possible prices, which can make capital inefficient.

Core idea: Matches bids and asks directly.

Trade-off: Costly and capital-inefficient on-chain.

Strength: Simple, always provides liquidity.

Trade-off: Capital may sit unused far from market price.

Strength: Higher capital efficiency and tighter spreads.

Trade-off: Requires more active liquidity management.

Core idea: Keeps exchange rate near fixed parity.

Trade-off: Can break if one side of the pool is drained.

Core idea: Blends product and sum curves.

Strength: Useful for stable or pegged assets.

Trade-off: More complex and less intuitive.

Core idea: Users express desired outcomes; solvers compete to fulfill them.

Strength: Can improve routing and user experience.

Trade-off: Depends on solver competition and execution quality.

Liquidity providers deposit assets into pools so other users can trade. In return, LPs may earn a share of trading fees. But providing liquidity is not passive income without risk. LP returns depend on trading volume, fee tiers, price movement, pool design, and impermanent loss.

LP profitability can be expressed as trading fees earned minus impermanent loss, if any.

DeFi lending protocols let users supply assets to earn yield and borrow against collateral without a traditional bank. Smart contracts replace bilateral lending relationships with pooled markets that set rules, manage collateral, and enforce liquidations automatically.

A typical lending protocol uses overcollateralization. A borrower deposits collateral, borrows a smaller value against it, and must keep the position healthy. If collateral value falls too far, the protocol can liquidate the position to protect the lending pool.

Collateral: Assets pledged to secure a loan or leveraged position.

Utilization: How much of a lending pool is currently borrowed.

Supply APY: The rate suppliers may earn for lending assets.

Borrow APY: The rate borrowers pay to use borrowed assets.

Health factor: A measure of whether a position is safely collateralized.

Liquidation: Forced sale or closure when a position becomes unsafe.

Yield aggregators simplify DeFi yield generation by routing deposits across strategies and automating parts of the process. Instead of manually moving funds between pools or protocols, users deposit into a vault. The aggregator then follows a strategy, compounds returns, and abstracts away some of the operational work.

This does not remove risk. Aggregators add another layer of smart-contract exposure, strategy risk, fee policy, governance risk, and dependence on keepers or automation agents. Yield optimization is a mechanism to understand, not a recommendation.

DeFi protocols often need information that does not naturally live on-chain. Lending markets need asset prices to value collateral. Derivatives need reference prices. Synthetic assets need external data. Liquidations depend on timely and accurate price information.

Oracles provide this data to smart contracts. Because smart contracts can execute automatically based on oracle inputs, oracle design is a core security issue. Bad data, delayed data, manipulation, or concentrated data sources can create losses across trading, lending, derivatives, and liquidation systems.

More resilient designs combine multiple sources and aggregate observations before publishing a value on-chain. That can reduce dependence on one provider, but it does not eliminate latency, source correlation, or governance risk.

Decentralized derivatives create exposure to assets or market outcomes without necessarily requiring users to hold the underlying asset directly. Common examples include perpetual futures, options, synthetic assets, and other collateralized instruments.

The key mechanics include collateral, margin, funding rates, liquidation thresholds, and protocol risk controls. Perpetual futures have no expiry and use funding payments to help keep contract prices aligned with spot prices. Like other DeFi products, derivatives can increase capital efficiency but also introduce leverage, liquidation, oracle, and systemic-risk concerns.

Many DeFi protocols are governed through DAOs, governance tokens, proposals, voting systems, delegation, and treasuries. Governance determines how protocols update parameters, allocate incentives, manage upgrades, and respond to risk.

Proposals may need a quorum or other participation threshold before a vote can take effect. The exact rules determine how voting power is counted, how delegates act, and how quickly a protocol can respond to changing conditions.

Governance can make protocols more community-driven, but it can also create concentration and coordination problems. Token-weighted voting may give large holders outsized influence. Low voter participation can make decisions fragile. Delegation can improve expertise but may also centralize practical control.

Composability is the ability for DeFi protocols to plug into and build on top of one another. This is why DeFi is often described as money legos. A lending protocol can use an oracle, a yield aggregator can route deposits across lending markets, and a derivatives protocol can depend on multiple liquidity and data systems.

Composability accelerates innovation because developers can reuse existing liquidity, infrastructure, and protocol primitives. But it also creates dependency risk. If one widely used protocol, oracle, bridge, collateral asset, or governance decision fails, the effects can cascade across many connected applications.

What it does: Enables on-chain trading through pools, curves, routing, or intents.

Main risk: Slippage, LP risk, smart-contract risk, solver/routing risk.

What it does: Lets users supply assets and borrow against collateral.

Main risk: Liquidation, collateral volatility, oracle risk.

What it does: Automates deposits, routing, strategies, and compounding.

Main risk: Strategy risk, contract risk, fee/governance risk.

What it does: Supplies external data to smart contracts.

Main risk: Data manipulation, latency, source concentration.

What it does: Creates synthetic or leveraged exposure.

Main risk: Funding, liquidation, margin, oracle, and leverage risk.

What it does: Coordinates protocol decisions and upgrades.

Main risk: Voter concentration, low participation, governance capture.

What it does: Connects multiple protocols into new products.

Main risk: Dependency and cascading failure risk.

DeFi replaces some intermediaries with code, liquidity systems, and governance. That does not remove risk. It changes the form of risk.

Smart contract risk: code can contain bugs or be exploited.

Oracle risk: bad or delayed data can trigger wrong pricing, lending, or liquidation outcomes.

Liquidity risk: pools can become shallow, imbalanced, or expensive to trade against.

Liquidation risk: collateralized positions can be closed when markets move quickly.

Governance risk: token voting can be captured, delayed, or dominated by large holders.

Composability risk: protocols that depend on one another can transmit failures across the system.

Regulatory risk: DeFi protocols, interfaces, and participants may face changing legal treatment.

This page is for educational purposes and explains protocol mechanics. It does not provide investment, trading, tax, or legal advice.

A user wants to trade ETH for USDC through a decentralized exchange. Instead of matching with a specific seller, the user trades against an ETH/USDC liquidity pool. Liquidity providers have deposited both assets into the pool. The smart contract calculates the trade based on the pool’s pricing formula, updates the token reserves, charges a trading fee, and sends the output token to the user.

The user gets an on-chain swap. LPs earn fees. The pool’s price changes because its reserves changed. If the trade is large relative to pool liquidity, the user may experience slippage. If the pool is part of a larger aggregator or intent-based route, solvers or routers may search across multiple liquidity sources to improve execution.

DeFi protocols use smart contracts to coordinate financial activity without a traditional centralized intermediary.

DEXs and AMMs use liquidity pools and algorithmic pricing to support on-chain trading.

DeFi lending protocols use collateral, utilization, rates, and liquidations to manage risk.

Yield aggregators automate strategy routing and compounding, but they add another layer of protocol risk.

Oracles are critical because many DeFi systems depend on accurate external data.

Decentralized derivatives introduce collateral, margin, funding, leverage, and liquidation dynamics.

Governance and composability are major strengths of DeFi, but they also create coordination and systemic-risk trade-offs.

This is an intermediate topic. Helpful background knowledge includes blockchain basics, Ethereum basics, wallets, tokens, smart contracts, basic trading terms, and basic lending vocabulary.

Related Campus topics include Ethereum & Layer 1s 201 for smart contracts, gas, and token standards; Market Structure 201 for liquidity, order books, derivatives, and institutions; Scaling & Layer 2s 201 for cheaper execution environments and rollup context; and stablecoins, RWAs, and glossary pages for collateral, settlement, and liquidity context.

DEX: A decentralized exchange that lets users trade through smart contracts rather than a centralized intermediary.

AMM: An automated market maker that prices trades algorithmically using pooled liquidity.

Liquidity pool: A smart contract holding tokens supplied by liquidity providers for traders to swap against.

Liquidity provider: A user or entity that deposits assets into a pool and earns fees while taking pool risk.

Constant product AMM: An AMM model that preserves the invariant x times y equals k.

Concentrated liquidity: A liquidity design that lets LPs provide capital inside selected price ranges.

Intent-based DEX: A DEX model where users express desired outcomes and solvers compete to execute them.

Collateral: Assets pledged to secure a DeFi loan or leveraged position.

Liquidation: The forced closing or sale of collateral when a position becomes unsafe.

Yield aggregator: A protocol that routes funds across strategies to optimize yield.

Oracle: A service that provides external data, such as prices, to smart contracts.

Perpetual futures: Derivative contracts with no expiry that use funding rates to track spot prices.

DAO: A decentralized organization governed through proposals, votes, and smart-contract rules.

Governance token: A token used to vote on protocol decisions or delegate voting power.

Composability: The ability for protocols to plug into and build on top of one another.

DeFi works through smart contracts that coordinate activity such as trading, lending, collateral management, yield strategies, derivatives, and governance on-chain.

An AMM is an automated market maker. It lets traders swap tokens against a liquidity pool, with prices set by an algorithmic formula rather than a traditional order book.

DeFi lending protocols let users supply assets to earn yield or borrow against collateral through smart-contract-based lending markets.

Yield aggregators are protocols that route deposits across strategies and automate parts of yield generation, such as strategy selection and compounding.

DeFi protocols need oracles because many smart contracts depend on external data, especially asset prices used for collateral values, derivatives, and liquidations.

DAO governance uses proposals, voting, delegation, tokens, and treasuries to coordinate protocol decisions and upgrades.

Composability means protocols can connect and build on each other, creating new products while also introducing dependency and systemic-risk trade-offs.

#5088. BitGo launches institutional trading and financial solutions business led by former NYDIG executive

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Pete Janney will lead the new BitGo Global Markets division, following the firm's acquisition of NYDIG’s institutional and trading business in August.

Digital asset infrastructure firm BitGo Holdings BitGo Holdings, Inc. (BTGO) announced the launch of BitGo Global Markets on Wednesday, its new institutional trading and financial solutions business bringing together global liquidity, execution, financing, derivatives, and settlement services.

The division will be headed up by former NYDIG executive Pete Janney following BitGo's acquisition of NYDIG's institutional and trading business on Aug. 27, according to a statement shared exclusively with The Block.

About 30 employees joined BitGo following the NYDIG acquisition, bringing experience in derivatives, financing, collateral servicing, and structured products. Janney initially came aboard as head of financial infrastructure and brings extensive experience in trading and capital markets.

BitGo has been offering institutional trading through BitGo Prime since 2020, with that business becoming part of Global Markets alongside the expanded derivatives, collateral management, and structured finance solutions from NYDIG.

"BitGo has built a strong foundation across custody, liquidity, trading, financing, and settlement," BitGo CEO Mike Belshe said in the statement. "Global Markets brings these capabilities together with deeper capital markets expertise, allowing us to support more complex institutional strategies and serve a broader range of our clients' financial needs. Pete understands how institutions trade and manage risk, and I'm excited to have him leading Global Markets.”

Under the new unit, BitGo's Global Liquidity Layer will provide access to spot trading and large over-the-counter transactions, drawing liquidity from exchanges, market makers, and other counterparties, the firm said. Clients can also borrow against their crypto holdings to fund trading positions, with BitGo keeping the collateral in qualified custody, monitoring margins, and handling collateral calls or liquidations when needed.

Additionally, BitGo Global Markets will offer options, swaps, and custom structured products for eligible counterparties to hedge exposure, express market views, and manage portfolio risk, the firm said.

Global Markets will also provide managed investment strategies, private funds, and research, while BitGo's Go Network will handle clearing and settlement.

"Clients who own digital assets need sophisticated solutions that go beyond buy and hold," Janney said. "I look forward to accelerating that work and growing the Global Markets franchise at BitGo."

BTGO was trading up 3.4% in pre-market trading on Wednesday, according to TradingView, following a 3.7% rise on Tuesday.

#5085. Circle reports $865 million in Q3 revenue and reserve income, discloses 5% workforce reduction

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Circle booked $182 million in ARC token presale revenue during the quarter, while income from its USDC reserves fell.

Stablecoin issuer Circle Internet Group Circle Internet Group, Inc. (CRCL) reported $865 million in total revenue and reserve income for the third quarter, up 17% from a year earlier, according to its earnings release on Wednesday.

Net income from continuing operations came in at $226 million, compared with $214.4 million in the same quarter last year. Adjusted EBITDA was $317 million, up from $171.5 million in Q3 2025. Circle previously reported $701 million in revenue and reserve income for the second quarter of this year, naming BlackRock, DTCC, and Visa among founding validators for its Arc blockchain.

Operating expenses rose 16% year over year to $245 million in the third quarter, the firm said, citing higher administrative, IT infrastructure, and marketing costs. Distribution, transaction, and other costs fell 8% to $412 million, mostly due to lower distribution payments, it added.

A footnote to the earnings tables also disclosed that Circle had reduced its workforce by approximately 5%, with the company booking $9.1 million in associated restructuring expenses, mainly for notice period and severance payments, employee benefits, and related costs. Circle said the reduction was designed to "better align our organizational structure with our operating model and strategic priorities," but did not give a headcount for the cuts. Circle had approximately 1,100 employees worldwide at the end of 2025, according to its annual report.

Of the $207 million Circle reported in other revenue, $182 million was recognized in Q3 from its ARC token presale. Other revenue had totaled $29 million in the third quarter of 2025. Circle agreed to sell a total of $242.2 million worth of ARC tokens in the presale at a purchase price of $0.30 per token and a $3 billion fully diluted valuation. Circle attributed the 85% increase in adjusted EBITDA to the presale revenue and lower distribution costs, partly offset by spending on new products.

Circle also maintained its full-year 2026 guidance, including $310 million to $330 million in other revenue and $570 million to $585 million in adjusted operating expenses.

Reserve income fell 8% year over year to $658 million as its reserve return rate declined by 61 basis points, the firm said. Circle's USDC stablecoin averaged $73.3 billion in circulation during Q3, an 8% increase from a year earlier. Onchain USDC transaction volume reached $13.9 trillion for the quarter, up 49% year over year. USDC circulation stood at $73.9 billion at quarter-end, unchanged from a year earlier, while Circle's stablecoin market share fell to 27%.

Circle opened Arc's public mainnet on Sept. 16, with more than 100 institutional and ecosystem builders on the network at launch. It also minted the initial supply of 10 billion ARC tokens, which the firm said was a technical milestone, not a commitment to launch them publicly. It expects Arc to move from proof of authority to proof of stake in 2027.

In September, Circle also agreed to acquire Tazapay, a Singapore-based payments firm with more than $25 billion in annualized payment volume. The firm expects the Tazapay acquisition to close in 2027, subject to regulatory approvals. Circle Ventures had led Tazapay's $36 million Series B extension in March. Binance also bought $100 million worth of Circle shares in September and signed a new five-year USDC agreement.

#5081. Schroders launches tokenized emerging markets debt fund on Solana with Centrifuge

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The $1 trillion-plus asset manager brings its emerging markets bond strategy to Solana, expanding the range of actively managed funds available onchain.

Centrifuge has launched a tokenized emerging markets debt fund managed by Schroders on the Solana blockchain, marking the $1 trillion-plus asset manager's first deployment on the network.

The fund, Schroder International Selection Fund Emerging Market Bond, provides onchain exposure to Schroders' actively managed emerging markets debt strategy, which spans sovereign, corporate, and local-currency investments.

The launch expands tokenized real-world assets beyond cash and short-term government securities into more complex, actively managed investment strategies.

Schroders' operations chief Marita McGinley called the launch "further evidence of our commitment to developing world-class investment solutions that meet the evolving needs of investors in the digital age."

This marks the latest institutional asset manager moving traditional investment products onchain. New York Life Investment Management partnered with Centrifuge to launch a tokenized high-yield corporate bond fund. Coinbase called on Centrifuge to tokenize ETFs, credit funds, and structured products on Base, building on prior strategic investment with the exchange.

"Emerging markets debt is an asset class where experience, local insight and active management are particularly important," said John Mensack, Senior Investment Director, Fixed Income, Schroders. "As the digital financial ecosystem develops, we also recognise the importance of bringing relevant emerging markets debt opportunities onchain, helping to make the asset class more accessible and fit for the future."

#5078. Coinbase says 99% of customer assets sit in cold storage after Binance’s $6.3 billion breach

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

"The advancement of [AI] capabilities is rapidly changing the security landscape, and, in response, Coinbase will continue to invest in best in class security innovation," the excahnge said.

Coinbase said it keeps more than 99% of customer assets in cold storage and uses distributed private keys, withdrawal limits, and anomaly detection to protect funds from unauthorized transfers.

In a blog post published Wednesday, the U.S. crypto exchange said it was not affected by the Binance breach and that customer assets are held 1:1, remain customer property, and are not lent or used without customer authorization.

On Oct. 29, the world's largest crypto exchange was exploited to the tune of $6.3 billion. Binance said Tuesday that hackers used a fake job interview and an internal AI tool to assist their breach.

"Our responsibility is to keep improving safeguards and continuously monitor for threats so Coinbase can be the best place for customers to grow, use and move their money," the blog reads. "The advancement of artificial intelligence capabilities is rapidly changing the security landscape, and, in response, Coinbase will continue to invest in best in class security innovation."

Binance cofounder and former CEO Changpeng "CZ" Zhao said crypto companies need AI-based defenses and an industry alliance to share lessons and freeze stolen funds faster. "The only way to fight a monster is to kind of become that monster," he said Tuesday.

For its part, Binance said Wednesday it has replenished approximately 98% of the assets affected by its security breach and expanded its Proof of Reserves coverage to 44 tokens, up from 10 previously.

The exchange said it is acquiring the remaining 2% of assets through market and over-the-counter channels, with purchases taking longer due to lower liquidity, while the new zero-knowledge proof is expected to take 50 to 60 hours to generate.

#5075. Ethereum Privacy Network Aztec Rolls Out Major Security and Speed Upgrade

The Aztec Foundation said Tuesday that the upgrade, called Aztec Tierra and designated Alpha V6, delivers stronger security, formal verification of its proving system and faster deposits from Ethereum, alongside a refreshed brand.

Aztec is a decentralized layer-2 with more than 3,000 validators and fully programmable private smart contracts, and it pitches itself as having no privileged viewing keys or centralized sequencer, meaning no single operator can peer into or control user activity.

The upgrade resolves known vulnerabilities from earlier versions, including a critical issue discovered in V5, and adds extensive hardening. Notably, that includes a new AI-powered security testing harness and formal verification of core cryptographic components, a mathematical process for proving code behaves as intended.

The move reflects a broader scramble across crypto to harden code as AI tools make vulnerabilities easier to find.

On performance, Aztec said private transactions now generate client-side proofs in about one second on a standard device, twice as fast as before, while a new architecture cuts the time for Ethereum-to-Aztec messages from an average of two minutes to as little as 12 seconds, speeding up deposits and cross-chain activity.

The upgrade arrives with a crop of private applications spanning payments, trading, AI and social.

Leading them is zk.money, a private payments and savings app built by Aztec Labs that the team revived this year. With Tierra’s faster messaging, users can deposit directly from any Ethereum wallet without complex bridging.

Others in the lineup include Galactica for private AI inference, Nemi for private perpetuals trading, a private browser wallet called Nulo, and Rolling Chat, a private message board based on Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin’s own Aztec experiments.

#5073. French Hill’s race still too close to call as House flips, changing the direction of crypto legislation

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Rep. Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill.

As of Wednesday afternoon, House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill's race for reelection is too close to call as a Democratic takeover in the House is set to usher in a new slate of leaders and reshape the future of crypto legislation in Congress.

On Tuesday night into Wednesday, Hill has been neck and neck with Democratic candidate Chris Jones in Arkansas, according to the Associated Press, with 99% of votes already reported. Hill held a slim lead at publication time.

Hill has led the House Financial Services Committee for the past two years, during which members passed the first federal framework for stablecoins as well as a sweeping crypto bill. The latter stalled in the Senate in September. Crypto political action committee Fairshake and other PACs spent $1 million on Hill's campaign, according to reporting from the Arkansas Democrat Gazette.

Other crypto-friendly lawmakers also secured reelection, including Josh Gottheimer, D-N.J., Rep. Warren Davidson, R-Ohio, Rep. Ritchie Torres, D-N.Y., and current House Agriculture Committee Chair Glenn "GT" Thompson, R-Pa. Later in the day on Wednesday, Rep. Bill Huizenga, R-MI., who is viewed favorably by the crypto industry, lost his seat to Democratic candidate Sean McCann, according to the Associated Press.

Candidate Shomari Figures beat Republican candidate Rhett Marques in Alabama. Figures was endorsed by crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto, given his vote on sweeping digital asset legislation called the Clarity Act.

With the House flipping control, Democrats will usher in the agenda for key congressional committees that have jurisdiction over crypto and prediction markets. Top Democrat Maxine Waters will likely take back her spot as House Financial Services Committee chair, leaving Hill out of his top spot, but likely to be the top Republican on that committee.

Waters is still expected to take up the spot again as chair, but has since been challenged by Democratic Reps. Sean Casten and Bill Foster. Some Democrats have signaled that they want "generational change," given that Waters is 88 years old and Casten is 54, according to reporting from Politico last month.

Regarding crypto, Waters worked on the stablecoin framework dubbed the GENIUS Act and has shown an openness to writing rules for the industry. However, she has also criticized the industry following the collapse of crypto exchange FTX and, later, President Donald Trump's crypto interests and how that can influence regulation.

#5069. UK Says AI-Powered Ransomware Attack on Critical Infrastructure 'Highly Likely' by 2028

UK Says AI-Powered Ransomware Attack on Critical Infrastructure 'Highly Likely' by 2028

Britain's critical national infrastructure should be braced for a ransomware attack involving AI-powered malware by 2028, the UK's National Cyber Security Centre warned on Tuesday.

Within the next two years, hostile actors' use of AI is "highly likely" to increase the scale and efficiency of their operations, including ransomware, social engineering and the exploitation of software vulnerabilities, according to the NCSC's annual State of the Cyber Nation report. The agency is part of GCHQ, the UK's signals intelligence service.

Today, we've published our 2026 annual review: The State of the Cyber Nation.

The UK faces a moment of choice. The future of cyber resilience is not set.

Read the report now:https://t.co/TzqzYedqXQ@GCHQ @DanJarvisMBE pic.twitter.com/mIFWrBB5Yt

The threat stems less from new attack techniques than from old weaknesses, NCSC chief executive Richard Horne said in prepared remarks for a speech launching the report.

"In the coming months, we face a real prospect of an unprecedented rise in the number of cyber incidents," Horne said. "But we must be clear that is not because AI has invented new ways to attack us. But because vulnerabilities that have been tolerated for far too long can now be discovered and exploited at a scale that was once unimaginable."

"For decades, we have collectively got away with doing tech on the cheap," he added.

Horne urged organizations to invest properly in their technology, stop connecting systems to the internet unnecessarily, and treat the government's Cyber Essentials certification as the starting point for their security rather than the end goal. Leaders who fail to act could have to explain to investors, staff and customers why "food isn't on shelves, lights aren't on, businesses have gone under," he warned.

The government last week announced £60 million (about $80 million) for the first phase of a national Cyber Shield, £70 million ($93 million) for a new National Exposure and Real-Time Vulnerability Centre to identify weaknesses across critical infrastructure, and a new AI Cyber Security Centre of Excellence in Manchester, run by the NCSC.

The warning follows growing evidence that attackers are already putting AI to work. In May, Google's Threat Intelligence Group said cybercriminals had used an AI model to find and weaponize a previously unknown flaw that let them bypass two-factor authentication in a popular open-source administration tool, the first time it had confirmed AI-assisted zero-day development in the wild. A Cambridge University study of more than 90,000 cybercrime forum threads published the same month, however, found that most criminals were using AI for spam and phishing rather than sophisticated attacks.

UK officials have sounded the alarm before. In July, Foreign Secretary Yvette Cooper warned that governments must agree on AI safeguards before frontier systems transform warfare, crime and society. "We cannot afford to wait for an AI equivalent of Hiroshima before we act," she wrote.

#5068. Bitcoin rebounds past $80,000 as markets shrug off midterms and Binance hack

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Ether jumped nearly 6% and crypto stocks rallied, while Bitwise says the market’s muted reaction to the Binance hack shows growing maturity.

Bitcoin has recovered from its post-Binance hack losses, surging toward $81,000 following Tuesday's U.S. midterm elections, which saw Democrats regain control of both chambers of Congress.

"Markets took the Democratic win in stride," Nexo analysts wrote in their daily dispatch. "Futures fell as much as 2.5% on early counts and then recovered fully, which suggests investors saw the outcome as adding clarity and not as changing the growth story."

Bitcoin (BTC) was up 3.5% to $80,500 on Wednesday afternoon, while Ethereum jumped nearly 6% to $2,433. Solana and XRP were up a more muted 2.5% on the day.

Futures data suggests traders are adjusting existing positions rather than taking on additional leverage, according to Nexo. Crypto futures volume climbed 27%, while open interest rose just 3%, and Bitcoin open interest grew more slowly than its price. Roughly $469 million in leveraged positions were also flushed out.

Nexo said market orders have shifted to a slight net buying bias after favoring sellers the previous day, while the most actively traded Bitcoin options are predominantly calls.

Crypto-related stocks also rallied, with Strategy and Coinbase gaining about 6% each; Strategy's variable-rate perpetual preferred stock STRC hit $100 for the first time since May.

"The case for holding Bitcoin crosses party lines," Executive Chairman Michael Saylor said earlier Wednesday.

Block Inc. jumped 15% following its earnings report, while bitcoin treasury firm Strive climbed roughly 12%. Meanwhile, the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin fell 17%.

Bitwise CIO Matt Hougan argued that the market's relatively muted reaction to the Binance hack reflects the crypto industry's growing maturity and improved security infrastructure.

In his weekly blog memo, Hougan examined the 10 largest crypto-related hacks in history and found that Bitcoin finished higher in the following 30 days in five cases and lower in five, suggesting no consistent relationship between major security incidents and Bitcoin's subsequent performance.

He said Bitcoin's roughly 4% decline following the Binance breach was largely in line with broader risk assets, emphasizing that Binance was hacked, not the Bitcoin network.

"Hacks would impact the market if they suggested there is no safer way to hold bitcoin, but there is. In this case, the stolen crypto was held in online hot wallets at a large, integrated crypto exchange," Hougan wrote.

He pointed to institutional custodians, cold storage and spot Bitcoin ETFs as increasingly secure alternatives for investors, highlighting ETFs in particular because their underlying assets are held in offline cold storage and subject to multiple withdrawal approvals and restrictions.

Hougan's assessment was echoed by Matt Mena, crypto research strategist at 21Shares, who said the market's resilience following the Binance breach was encouraging.

"What we find most encouraging is how the market handled the worst of it," Mena wrote in a Tuesday report. "The largest hack in crypto’s history caused a far smaller drawdown than past crises; the protocols kept running, and none of the structural drivers broke: banks, payment networks, and asset managers kept shipping onchain products through the drawdown, and regulators kept writing rules."

Mena is watching four things into November: whether Bitcoin holds $76,000 to $78,000 and sustains its move back above $80,000 to $82,000; Binance's progress in completing withdrawals and proving its reserves; credit spreads, oil prices, and long-end Treasury yields; and whether ETF inflows return.

#5063. Democratic sweep of Congress forces crypto industry to rethink Washington strategy

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

As results rolled in, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate.

After two years of a pro-crypto, pro-innovation climate in Washington, the digital asset industry faces a sharp political jolt as Democrats take control of both chambers of Congress, forcing the sector to seek new allies within a party that has been more divided on crypto.

As results rolled in on Tuesday, crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto said 19 pro-crypto lawmakers in the Senate had been elected and 226 in the House as Democrats took control of the House and Senate. Eight anti-crypto Senators were also elected alongside 106 in the House.

Following the results, some in the crypto industry have taken the position that crypto is bipartisan and that lawmakers on both sides of the aisle are pro-digital assets. But a Democratic sweep means more oversight, and there is close to no chance of the crypto industry getting legislation it wants on the table.

"Nobody has their head in the sand," said Kara Calvert, vice president of U.S. policy at Coinbase, in a post on X. "There will be headwinds for any bipartisan legislation next year, but crypto is 100% bipartisan."

Many of the new members in Congress are pro-crypto, said Kevin Wysocki, head of policy at Anchorage Digital.

"The younger members are more used to this technology, are more open to new technology that maybe rivals some of the existing technology," he said in an interview with The Block on Wednesday. "It's very bullish, I think, for crypto overall, so I think that's one of the great things from what happened yesterday, and I think it's only going to get better over time, regardless of who's in power, Republicans or Democrats."

However, one of the biggest races the crypto industry had its eyes on, and money on, was the Ohio Senate race between former Democratic Sen. Sherrod Brown and SWC-endorsed Republican Sen. Jon Husted. The crypto political action committee spent tens of millions of dollars on that race against Brown, but ultimately lost when Brown defeated Husted later in the night.

Brown was previously Senate Banking Committee Chair, which has jurisdiction over digital assets and has been viewed as critical of crypto. He could have a leading role in oversight and regulation of the industry. And since he won in a special election to fill Vice President JD Vance's Senate term, that means Brown could return to the Senate as early as Dec. 15.

Brown's return is significant, Wysocki said. When he was chair of the Senate Banking Committee, Brown and his Senate staff were open to constructive conversations about crypto, he said.

Pro-crypto political action committees reportedly spent over $60 million alone in the Senate race in Ohio against Brown. On its own, crypto PAC Fairshake raised $106.2 million, according to Americans for Financial Reform. And crypto PACs overall spent $170 million on the midterms, according to Fortune, with millions going to Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts in Nebraska.

Fairshake spent $154.5 million in both primaries and the general election, with over $13.5 million going to Ricketts.

"Fairshake played a meaningful role in winning competitive races across the country in 2026 and we will do it again in 2028," said Geoff Vetter, spokesperson for Fairshake, in an emailed statement to The Block. "Newly elected and current members will have a choice: protect consumers and American jobs or fight progress. We will be watching and '28 is a new map."

One crypto industry source alluded that PACs and trade associations will have to "recalibrate" and need to "double down on being bipartisan."

Specifically on Fairshake, the person criticized funds being used on Brown's race.

"What did we ultimately get out of that?" they said. "Where could that money have been better spent? To be perfectly honest with you, I think it could have been better spent on shoe-leather lobbying."

That money could have been spent to lobby lawmakers on the Clarity Act, broad crypto legislation that passed the House, but got stuck in the Senate in September, they said.

"You spend a fraction of it, and you could have effectively hired all of DC to lobby on Clarity," they added.

Focus will turn to taxes and security and hacks, Anchorage's Wysocki said. The election, too, comes just days after the largest crypto hack on record of Binance for over $6 billion.

"Congress is going to be asking those questions, like how do we make security at our financial institutions stronger?" Wysocki said.

The Clarity Act that passed out of the House last year and stalled in the Senate a few months ago is unlikely to advance over the next two years given Brown's win, said investment bank TD Cowen.

Oversight will also come into view, including investigations into Trump-family-run World Liberty Financial, said TD Cowen’s Washington Research Group, led by managing director Jaret Seiberg.

"Democrats will investigate Trump's crypto entanglements, including World Liberty Financial," Seiberg wrote in a note on Wednesday. "We believe the resulting political environment may make it impossible to advance a version of Clarity that the crypto sector could back."

#5061. Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Epidemic of Hacked Freezers Spreads Across Europe

Europe's freezers have gone rogue, with supermarkets in nine more countries reporting refrigeration systems that heated up overnight on Monday.

Stores in Belgium, Estonia, Finland, Ireland, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Portugal and Sweden reported problems, and Estonian units were pushed to 50°C (122 °F).

No group has claimed responsibility, so the only ones taking the heat are the freezers.

The outbreak has a patient zero in France, where Finance Minister Roland Lescure told Parliament on Oct. 13 that about 700 of the country's 10,000 supermarkets were affected.

According to France's CERT-FR, hackers forced a continuous defrost cycle, which spoiled food and sparked fires at some stores. CERT-FR said it was aware of numerous compromises of remote control modules made by Danfoss and Copeland.

Groupement Mousquetaires, which owns Intermarché and Netto, counted 300 stores affected, while Cooperative U said 80 of its 2,000 shops were hit. Lescure said food supplies were not at risk but acknowledged the attack "touches the heart of our food security." Some Mousquetaires display cases were still out of service that Tuesday.

Monday's wave hit several chains in Belgium, where Carrefour reported disruption at about 15 of its 700 stores. Sabcobel, a Belgian refrigeration contractor, said evaporators had been switched into defrost mode, and federal prosecutors opened an investigation. Four Delhaize outlets in Luxembourg also reported problems.

Italy's Radenza Group, which runs Coop-branded stores in Sicily, said the attack began at 4 a.m local time. and targeted the remote-control system for frozen food sections. Ireland's Musgrave counted 13 SuperValu stores affected, and Mosqueteiros confirmed incidents at seven Intermarché stores in Portugal.

At 50°C (122 °F), a freezer is well past hot-tub territory and turns basically into a low-powered steam oven or, worse, a bacteria incubator where germs grow quickly if food is left there too long.

In Sweden, Coop said six of its roughly 800 stores were hit and that they ran on 4G connections, making them "more exposed." Ica said a limited number of stores had problems and that the affected shops used an external Danfoss control system. Neither chain has confirmed a cyberattack, and Finland's Kesko says network interference has not been ruled out.

EU Agriculture Commissioner Christophe Hansen called the incidents a "deliberate attempt" to disrupt the food supply chain. Investigators have not said whether Monday's wave is linked to the French attack.

Estonian refrigeration firm Kitman Coldtech said the affected units were older Danfoss models similar to those attacked in France. CERT-FR warns that leaving an end-of-life device without security patches exposed to the internet carries a very high risk of compromise. Researchers at Claroty published flaws in Danfoss AK-SM 800A controllers in August, including an authentication bypass.

Claroty found 2,765 of those devices exposed online, and Danfoss fixed the flaws in firmware R4.3.1. It remains unclear which weaknesses the attackers used, and CERT-FR says the campaigns are probably still ongoing, while Italy's government was set to hold a cybersecurity meeting on Tuesday.

For now, CERT-FR advises operators to take the controllers off the public internet, and Sabcobel's CEO says many older units need physical modifications that require investment. Until those arrive, there is no real fix for the oldest units, which makes your 1998 chest freezer the most secure device on the continent.

#5058. Crédit Mutuel Arkéa confirms cyberattack after ZachXBT flags $133M in suspected losses

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Crédit Mutuel Arkéa did not confirm the exact amount of losses but validated the order of magnitude of the $133 million figure flagged by ZachXBT.

French bank Crédit Mutuel Arkéa has confirmed that a cyberattack enabled fraudulent transfers from its account, which were converted into crypto via specialized platforms.

In a statement released Wednesday, the bank said that client assets were unaffected and the incident has been contained.

"As soon as this attack was identified, our teams immediately activated the necessary security measures to contain the incident and prevent any further fraudulent movements against Crédit Mutuel Arkéa," the bank said.

"We wish to reassure all of our customers: this incident has had no impact on their assets," the bank added. "It only caused a delay in the execution of certain transfers."

Onchain sleuth ZachXBT, who first publicly flagged the exploit on Nov. 2, estimated the losses at more than $133 million. He noted that the figure could rise, as the attack unfolded over several days and may have involved additional over-the-counter transactions.

While Crédit Mutuel Arkéa did not confirm the exact amount of losses, it validated the order of magnitude. The bank said some of the stolen funds had been blocked.

"While it is therefore still too early to communicate a final amount, we can already state that the amounts involved are in no way of a nature to undermine the financial strength and soundness of Crédit Mutuel Arkéa," the bank said.

ZachXBT previously reported that the attacker had been moving stolen funds through a series of transactions, including swapping USDC for ETH, routing assets through instant exchanges such as WellWallet and Kyrrex, converting funds into XMR via Wagyu, and bridging assets across multiple chains.

He also said he has a "high degree of confidence" that the attacker was not associated with North Korea's Lazarus Group.

The bank has over 12,000 employees and more than 5.8 million members and customers. Its reported total assets are €211.5 billion ($238.1 billion), according to the statement.

#5056. Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Five Ways North Korea Breaks Into Crypto Firms—and How Binance Was Hacked

Hackers from North Korea are notoriously effective at stealing crypto, with one estimate attributing 60% of all losses last year to DPRK-linked groups. These hackers enlist several different vectors of attack, making it all the more difficult to spot them in the act.

Last week, it took just 20 minutes for alleged North Korean hackers to drain $6.3 billion from Binance, in what is the biggest crypto hack on record. The attackers compromised a Binance engineer's work computer through a fake job interview, used an internal AI tool to gain control of other engineers' computers, and then used stolen credentials to hijack an internal wallet signing service, the exchange said.

In the past, North Korean hackers have been known to get hired at crypto companies under fake identities, attack through third-party infrastructure, and poison widely used code libraries. More recently, they've started using AI to make all of it more convincing.

Here are some examples of the methods that North Korean hackers are known to use in crypto attacks.

Binance chief security officer Jimmy Su told Decrypt in August 2025 that the exchange throws out suspected North Korean resumes every day, and that "almost all the large DPRK hacks," at the time, had involved a fake employee helping facilitate the attack.

Su said there were tell-tale signs in resumes that indicate the applicant was from North Korea, but the exchange was not willing to reveal the specifics on the red flags. Sometimes, though, malicious actors get through to the interview stage, where the attackers use deepfakes to change their face and voice.

"The only real good detection is that they almost always have a slow internet connection," he told Decrypt. "What's happening is that the translation and the voice changer are working during the call … that's why they are always delayed."

Those who do get hired tend to be suspiciously good at the job. Su said North Korean workers are often among a company's top performers, likely because several people may be sharing one role across time zones.

The threat has not let up. Binance said its security team detected and prevented 57 suspected North Korea-linked cyber threat cases in 2026, using techniques ranging from fraudulent job interviews to fake video conferencing apps.

Attackers also flip the scam around and pose as recruiters to target people who already work at high-profile crypto companies. Often, the attacker will claim there's a video or audio issue during the interview and send the victim a link to update Zoom, which infects their device with malware

"The DPRK group will [also] try to schedule calls with the external-facing employees," Su told Decrypt. "Either as a DeFi project or investment firm. Worst yet, they'll be recruiting them for a high-level job, paying twice, three times as much, just to get them onto an interview."

This is how the Binance attack began, according to the exchange. In September, a Binance software engineer was approached through a legitimate job-hunting platform by someone posing as a project director, who set up two interviews. In the first, a purported HR representative built trust over a live video call by sharing a social media account full of personal photos and videos, an approach Binance called "more advanced and deceptive than previously seen."

In the second, held on a chat app, the engineer was told to download an "assignment" onto his work computer. The code repository contained previously unknown malware that ran during setup, swapped a legitimate developer tool for a malicious version and connected to an attacker-controlled server, evading Binance's security tools.

One North Korea-linked group infected at least 30,000 devices in more than 100 countries between roughly December 2025 and July 2026 using similar methods. It took funds or credentials from more than 7,000 crypto wallets and moved around $10.71 million to North Korea.

The group, known as WaterPlum or Contagious Interview, posed as AI, crypto, or NFT companies and set candidates a technical interview or coding task that required downloading malware-filled files. Some used AI face-swapping software during interviews before cutting their video and asking the candidate to do the same, blaming a bad connection.

Binance's Su also told Decrypt that North Korean hackers poison public code libraries, ranking it as the second most common method of attack—behind fake employees.

Node Package Manager (NPM) packages are collections of reusable, open-source code that developers frequently use. Attackers can clone these packages and slip in a small line of malicious code while keeping its original function. That means that a victim could use this poisoned code library and not notice, with the malicious code embedding itself deeper over time as more is built on top of it.

In September 2025, software security firm ReversingLabs found two NPM packages that used Ethereum smart contracts to download malware, which the firm said it hadn't seen before. The packages were tied to a wider network of GitHub repositories, mostly branded as crypto trading bots, with thousands of commits and stars that ReversingLabs thinks were mostly faked.

The tactic formed part of a “sophisticated campaign” of malicious actors attempting to hack users via poisoned blockchain-related public code libraries, a vector of attack that Binance has previously attributed to North Korean hackers.

To counter the threat, Su said, Binance makes employees go through NPM libraries with a fine-tooth comb.

Tampered developer tools also played a part in the Binance hack. In June, a member of Binance's wallet DevOps team downloaded a tampered installation of Homebrew, a popular software installer, while trying to install an AI coding tool he had found through a search engine, the exchange said. Its security team caught the problem, replaced the device and rotated his credentials, but the incident exposed the engineer and his colleagues on the wallet infrastructure team to the attackers.

Before Binance, the unwanted record for the largest crypto hack belonged to Bybit, which lost $1.4 billion in February 2025. Cybersecurity firms revealed that North Korean hackers pulled off the attack by planting malicious code into the infrastructure of Safe—a crypto wallet provider used by the exchange.

The security firms said malicious JavaScript code was directly injected into Safe's online infrastructure. Though, it was unclear how the attackers managed to do this.

Bybit's infrastructure "was not compromised," the exchange told Decrypt, and the hack came purely because a third party was exploited.

The Binance attack targeted the exchange's own systems. On October 29, the attacker used stolen credentials to deploy a container and gain unauthorized access to an internal wallet signing service, which let it initiate transactions outside Binance's normal withdrawal process. On Ethereum, it used the signing service to exploit EIP-7702, a mechanism that lets an account delegate execution rights to a smart contract, giving an attacker-controlled contract partial control over a Binance hot wallet.

Those on-chain authorizations kept working even after Binance suspended withdrawals. Because the hot wallet used multi-party computation, there was no single private key Binance could use to revoke them quickly, the exchange said.

Every single one of the above mentioned attack vectors can be turbo-charged with artificial intelligence tools.

Early this year, Google warned of an AI-powered malware campaign targeting crypto companies coming from North Korea. Similarly, Binance's Su also mentioned that AI tools can be used to increase the effectiveness of attacks.

Concerns around AI-powered hacks have risen in recent months, with Ethereum co-founder Vitalik Buterin saying in October that it's "plausible" AI could break encryption within the next two years. It came amid an explosion in AI-driven maths, with OpenAI claiming to have cracked a 90-year-old maths problem in a matter of days. Experts urged the industry to prepare for AI-powered attacks on encryption.

There are no confirmed reports of North Korean hackers using AI to break encryption. But given how quickly they've adopted AI elsewhere, the industry may have to prepare for that next.

#5054. The Digital Chamber calls Sherrod Brown’s win ‘most consequential unfavorable result’ for crypto

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Digital Chamber said Sherrod Brown’s priorities in financial stability, consumer protection, banking oversight, and illicit-finance concerns could affect the party's overall approach to crypto.

The Digital Chamber called Sherrod Brown's win the "most consequential unfavorable result for crypto," and said his priorities in financial stability, consumer protection, banking oversight, and illicit-finance concerns could affect the Democratic Party's overall approach to crypto.

"His influence could extend beyond committee votes to the Democratic caucus's overall approach to crypto," the industry group said. "Engagement should be substantive and focused on consumer protections and accountability."

Brown, a longtime crypto skeptic, won the U.S. Senate special election in Ohio on Nov. 3, defeating Republican Jon Husted. Crypto political action committees (PACs) spent more than $63 million supporting Husted against Brown, according to The Digital Chamber.

Outside of Brown's win, Democrats are set to control both chambers of Congress after a larger-than-expected victory on Nov. 3. The Senate stood at 54 Democrats to 44 Republicans with two races uncalled, while Democrats held 240 House seats with 48 races yet to be called, according to AP.

The Digital Chamber stated that the results shift the policy debate from whether Washington should embrace technological innovation to what protections, accountability, and economic benefits should accompany it.

Furthermore, Democratic committee chairs will hold subpoena power and can investigate regulators, cryptocurrency exchanges, AI developers, and data-center development. Digital asset legislation will depend more heavily on Democratic priorities including consumer protection, financial stability, illicit finance, and market integrity, the group said.

On Senate Banking, the Digital Chamber identified Elizabeth Warren as the clear favorite to become chair. It described the committee as the largest potential obstacle to the Clarity Act and other market-structure legal frameworks for the crypto industry. Clarity failed to pass a procedural vote in the Senate in September.

"A Democratic-controlled Congress does not necessarily mean comprehensive crypto legislation is permanently dead," the group said. "It does, however, mean the industry cannot simply continue the same legislative strategy that neglects working with moderate Democrats."

The group said that there are still viable legislative openings around issues including: tax reporting, custody, stablecoin implementation, and consumer asset segregation.

Meanwhile, the Digital Chamber also highlighted that among other Democratic winners, James Talarico of Texas is supportive of clear cryptocurrency rules while advancing a strong AI-accountability agenda that includes an AI dividend and requirements that data centers cover infrastructure costs.

Josh Turek, who won a Senate seat in Iowa, is supportive of market-structure legislation, self-custody rights, and ending improper debanking, the group said. Mark Warner of Virginia, who won reelection, is also identified as an existing Democratic bridge on digital asset issues because of his seats on Banking and the Digital Assets Subcommittee.

"This is an important reminder that a Democratic congressional victory does not automatically translate into opposition to crypto," the group said.

#5052. Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Elon Musk’s Tesla and SpaceX Block Their Stock From OKX-ICE Tokenized Venue

Two of Elon Musk’s companies are now keeping their shares off OKX and ICE’s planned tokenized stock venue.

Tesla and SpaceX are among six new objecting issuers listed in an updated notice from OKXICE TSV dated Monday. Amazon, Netflix, Lam Research, and SoFi Technologies are the others. The additions bring the total to 56 companies.

The list stood at 50 in the venue’s Oct. 30 notice, when Nvidia, Costco, and Nike led the biggest batch yet. That followed 38 on Oct. 28, after Walmart joined, and nine on Oct. 15, when the first objectors appeared.

The new names deepen the roster of megacap holdouts. Amazon, Tesla, and Netflix join Microsoft, Alphabet, and Nvidia, while SoFi, a fintech company that offers crypto trading, adds a company with its own stake in digital assets. SpaceX, which trades under the ticker SPCX, rounds out the batch.

Under the SEC’s innovation exemption, which the agency unveiled in September after the Clarity Act stalled in the Senate, a venue must notify a company before offering a version of its shares tokenized by an unaffiliated third party.

If the company objects in writing within 30 days, the venue can’t make that stock available for trading, according to the SEC’s order. The objection applies only to that venue.

OKXICE TSV, a 50-50 joint venture between NYSE owner Intercontinental Exchange and OKX’s U.S. holding company, plans to offer tokenized U.S. stocks 24/7 through permissioned Uniswap v4 pools on OKX’s XLayer blockchain, paired with stablecoins such as USDC.

To trade, users must pass identity and sanctions checks and hold a nontransferable “soulbound” token that the venue’s smart contracts verify before every transaction.

Apple is now one of the few megacap names still missing from the list. And while objections bar OKXICE from listing third-party tokenized versions of a company’s stock, they don’t close every door.

The notice says the venue can also list shares tokenized by the issuers themselves, though it doesn’t identify any, and that tokenized stocks it offers aren’t restricted to trading on OKXICE alone.

#5050. Arkansas sues Kalshi, says its alleged illegal gambling operations cause mental harm

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The lawsuit adds Arkansas to a growing list of states taking legal action against the prediction market platform.

Arkansas Attorney General Tim Griffin has filed a lawsuit against Kalshi for allegedly operating an illegal gambling platform in the state.

Griffin said in a statement on Wednesday that Kalshi has built a global business that allows users to wager on the outcome of "just about anything."

"It doesn't take a genius to understand that wagering money on whether the Razorbacks win or lose is gambling," Griffin said, adding that such practice is "squarely prohibited" by Arkansas law.

"Although Kalshi insists that its products are not gambling, its actions speak louder than its labels," Griffin added. "Kalshi is not blind that its platform presents real, tangible risks to its users, including excessive wagering, financial losses, depression, and other mental health harms."

Kalshi has previously argued in other lawsuits that its event contracts are derivatives regulated by the Commodity Futures Trading Commission and therefore not subject to state gambling laws.

The Block has reached out to Kalshi for comment on the Arkansas lawsuit.

The lawsuit adds Arkansas to a growing list of states taking legal action against the prediction market platform amid a dispute over whether prediction markets fall under federal derivatives regulations or state gambling laws.

Last week, Utah filed criminal charges against Kalshi, CEO Tarek Mansour, and chief operating officer Luana Lopes Lara, accusing them of illegally promoting online gambling and letting an undercover agent place wagers. The criminal charges came after Utah sued Kalshi and two Susquehanna trading firms, alleging that they operated an illegal gambling business in the state.

Meanwhile, the CFTC has sued at least 10 states, arguing that the states were intruding on its jurisdiction over prediction markets.

"Congress entrusted the CFTC to be the federal regulator with sole responsibility to oversee these markets," CFTC Chair Michael Selig said in a statement. "As I've said before, the CFTC has the expertise and responsibility to defend its exclusive jurisdiction over commodity derivatives, and that’s exactly what we'll do."

#5048. Brazil's Biggest Bitcoin Exchange Says It Will Refund Users After a $40 Million Hack

Brazil's Biggest Bitcoin Exchange Says It Will Refund Users After a $40 Million Hack

Brazil's largest crypto exchange says its users won't pay for its hack. Mercado Bitcoin, which lost tens of millions of dollars on Monday, says the losses are its problem, not its customers'.

An exchange holds your coins the way a bank holds your cash, and when one gets robbed, the customers are often the ones left waiting to see what they get back.

The exchange spotted a breach early on November 2 and froze deposits and withdrawals. “We identified and quickly contained a security incident in one of our systems early this morning,” Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language statement on X. “As a preventive protection measure, deposits and withdrawals are temporarily suspended.”

It said no customer lost money and no data leaked. It has not said how the attackers got in.

How much was taken depends on who is counting. Security firm Hypernative flagged more than $40 million in suspicious transfers across at least 10 blockchains, including Bitcoin, Ethereum, Solana, and Tron.

Primeiro, o mais importante: os ativos dos clientes estão garantidos . Os saldos continuam registrados nas respectivas contas.

Ontem (2/11), identificamos um incidente de segurança. Acionamos na hora nossos protocolos de segurança e governança e informamos as autoridades.…

— Mercado Bitcoin (@MercadoBitcoin) November 3, 2026

The exchange's own number is lower. “Right now the value of the assets involved is about $38 million,” Mercado Bitcoin said in a Portuguese-language update on X. “After a quick action with the crypto community and strategic partners, including Tether, Circle, Paxos, BRL1 and Chainalysis, we have already blocked $11 million.”

Others put it higher. Blockchain analysts at Arkham traced at least $46 million, while Chainalysis says about $49 million, with nearly $10 million frozen. The exchange has not said whether its $38 million includes the frozen amount.

Tether, which issues the USDT stablecoin (a token pegged to the dollar), froze more than $4.5 million in USDT tied to the incident, CEO Paolo Ardoino said on X.

A freeze only stops the tokens from moving. It doesn't return them to their owner, which is why the exchange's promise to cover losses carries the weight here.

On October 29, hackers drained more than $6 billion from Binance, the world's biggest exchange, and the FBI blamed North Korea's TraderTraitor group. Mercado Bitcoin has not linked its incident to that attack.

Binance made the same pledge as Mercado Bitcoin. “Binance will absorb the financial impact of the stolen funds, not our users,” CEO Richard Teng wrote on X, adding that about 95% of the affected assets had been replenished.

Neither promise can be checked from the outside. A guarantee to cover losses is only as good as the money behind it, and exchanges don't have to show customers their books.

For now, Mercado Bitcoin deposits and withdrawals remain suspended with no restart date. The exchange says it will post updates on X and resume service once full security is restored.

#5045. Eric Trump’s American Bitcoin swings to $126 million profit in Q3 on digital asset gains

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The Q3 profit swing was driven primarily by a $197.34 million gain on digital assets as bitcoin holdings rose to 8,427 BTC.

American Bitcoin swung to profit in the third quarter after recording a $197.3 million gain on digital assets, offsetting weaker mining revenue and higher production costs.

The bitcoin miner, co-founded by Eric Trump, reported net income of $126 million for the three months ended Sept. 30, compared with a $57.2 million net loss in the previous quarter, according to a statement. The company booked a $71.2 million loss on its digital assets in the previous quarter.

American Bitcoin said adjusted EBITDA also turned positive at $220.8 million, from a loss of $45 million in Q2. Mining revenue, however, fell 5.4% quarter over quarter to $63.4 million from $67 million, while bitcoin production declined 4.4% to approximately 891 BTC from 932 BTC.

"These are significant results, particularly in a challenging digital asset market, and they reflect the discipline and long-term conviction behind this business," Eric Trump said in the statement. "But our opportunity extends beyond any single quarter."

On production costs, the company said its expenditure rose 15.3% to approximately $42,100 from $36,500. Gross margin fell to 41% from 49%, while gross profit declined to $25.8 million from $33 million.

American Bitcoin sold a portion of its quarterly bitcoin production to cover operating costs, the company said, while adding about 425 BTC to its reserve and making no purchases during the period. This brought its total holdings to 8,427 BTC at the end of September.

The company's shares closed down 5.6% on Wednesday but climbed 2.4% in extended trading following the earnings release. The stock was down 76% year-to-date.

Meanwhile, other miners also reported substantial digital asset gains. Hut 8 posted $189.5 million in Q3 net income, including $413.8 million in primarily unrealized digital asset gains.

Riot Platforms reported $181.4 million in quarterly revenue, including $116 million from bitcoin mining.

#5043. CFTC Lets Traders Bet on Stocks With Crypto-Style Perpetual Futures

CFTC Lets Traders Bet on Stocks With Crypto-Style Perpetual Futures

The U.S. derivatives regulator has opened the door to perpetual futures on individual stocks, importing one of crypto’s most popular trading instruments into traditional equity markets for the first time.

The Commodity Futures Trading Commission said Tuesday that “equity perpetual futures,” or perpetual contracts tied to individual stocks, can now begin trading on CFTC-registered exchanges.

Perpetual futures, long a staple of crypto markets, are derivatives that track an asset’s price but carry no expiration date, letting traders hold leveraged positions indefinitely as long as they meet margin and funding requirements. Until now, they’ve been largely off-limits to U.S. traders.

Chairman Michael Selig framed the move as the latest step in a campaign to bring perpetual products onshore.

“In my inaugural remarks, I directed the CFTC to use the tools at its disposal to onshore perpetual derivative products so that they can flourish in the U.S., subject to appropriate safeguards,” he said, calling the stock contracts an extension of the agency’s earlier approval of the first true Bitcoin perpetual futures on a regulated U.S. exchange.

That milestone, as Decrypt reported, saw the CFTC clear Bitcoin perpetual futures for Kalshi earlier this year.

Selig returned to a familiar argument: that regulated U.S. venues are safer than the offshore platforms traders have otherwise had to use.

Bringing these products into regulated markets “ensures that American investors and traders can access these products within the protections of the CFTC’s proven regulatory framework, rather than being forced to turn to offshore, and often unregulated, venues,” he said.

It’s a theme he pressed just last week when he used the record Binance hack to argue offshore exchanges need U.S. oversight.

The equity perps were deemed approved as security futures products under the Commodity Futures Modernization Act of 2000, placing them under the joint oversight of the CFTC and the Securities and Exchange Commission.

Selig credited a historic memorandum of understanding the two agencies signed in March for ending the jurisdictional limbo that had, in SEC Chairman Paul Atkins’s words, stranded such products in a regulatory “no man’s land.”

The approval arrives amid a rush to bring perps onshore. The CFTC has floated plans to bring crypto exchanges under federal oversight, dismissed a CME lawsuit challenging crypto perpetual futures, and cleared the way for Coinbase, which recently filed to list single-stock perps on names like Apple, Tesla and Nvidia.

For Selig, the goal is explicit: ensuring “the new frontier of finance is built in the United States of America.”

#5040. Samsung Wallet launches USDC accounts on Solana for US Galaxy users

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

Samsung Wallet's Stablecoin Account lets eligible U.S. Galaxy users buy, hold, send and receive USDC on Solana via the app.

Samsung's Stablecoin Account is now live in Samsung Wallet, allowing eligible Galaxy users in the United States to buy, hold and transfer USDC on Solana, according to a statement released Thursday.

Samsung first announced the feature on Oct. 7, with the launch originally expected in the last week of that month.

Around 82 million Galaxy devices in the U.S. are compatible with the service, according to the release. Users must be at least 18 years old, have a Samsung Account and own a compatible device running Android 13 or later. New York residents are excluded.

"For a decade, stablecoins have proved the technology works," Solana Foundation President Lily Liu said. "What they lacked was a way to reach the mainstream."

Users can send USDC to another Samsung Wallet user or a compatible external crypto wallet without paying a Samsung transfer fee. Eligible users can also send money to qualifying bank accounts in more than 60 countries, where recipients may receive local currency, with fees varying by destination.

Licensed stablecoin custodian and infrastructure provider Bastion handles the stablecoin accounts and transfer infrastructure, while Coinbase holds the USDC as its sub-custodian through Coinbase Prime Vault.

Coinbase was already working with Samsung before this launch. In October 2025, Samsung expanded its Coinbase partnership to let U.S. Galaxy users fund Coinbase accounts with Samsung Pay.

Samsung's new Stablecoin Account feature lets users access USDC without installing a separate crypto app or managing private keys themselves. The firm said it also expects to explore online and in-store stablecoin payments, and expansion to additional markets, subject to local regulation.

"Stablecoins have the potential to make moving money around the world faster and easier, and we want Galaxy users to be able to take advantage of that without having to navigate the complexity of traditional crypto tools," said Woncheol Chai, EVP and Head of the Digital Wallet Team at the Mobile eXperience (MX) Business at Samsung Electronics.
