#5038. Crypto Docs Host GitBook Hit by AI Agent Security Breach, Urges Users to Rotate Keys

Crypto Docs Host GitBook Hit by AI Agent Security Breach, Urges Users to Rotate Keys

GitBook, a documentation platform widely used by cryptocurrency projects, has disclosed a security breach involving AI agents that allowed an attacker to gain access to some of its infrastructure credentials, forcing the company to shut down services and revoke customer access tokens.

In a security disclosure published today, the company described the incident as “a sophisticated, highly targeted attack using AI agents.” The attacker gained unauthorized access at 7 p.m. Central European Time on November 2, according to GitBook, which said it detected the intrusion immediately and moved to contain it.

The reference to AI agents is significant. Unlike traditional chatbots that simply respond to questions, AI agents can carry out sequences of actions, interact with software, and perform tasks with limited human supervision, making them potentially useful to attackers trying to automate parts of a cyberattack. Some are even capable of acting autonomously and even against their guidelines, as OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s experiments have proven.

GitBook has not explained exactly how the attacker used these tools, which AI systems were involved, or how the credentials were obtained. Its disclosure also does not establish that GitBook's own AI products were responsible for the intrusion.

The company took its application and documentation sites requiring authentication offline while securing its infrastructure. It also kept publicly available documentation accessible through cached copies for as long as possible, allowing visitors to continue reading previously saved versions of pages even while the underlying systems were unavailable.

A separate status notice acknowledged that GitBook was experiencing downtime affecting its published documentation sites and editor. By Tuesday afternoon, GitBook said its application, API, and published documentation were accessible again, although some services could remain degraded during recovery.

GitBook is effectively an online instruction manual for software products. Cryptocurrency projects use platforms like it to publish technical explanations, developer instructions, and guides that help users understand everything from connecting software to interacting with decentralized financial applications.

The documents themselves are often public, but the systems that maintain them can connect to private accounts and external services. Those connections depend on credentials that are supposed to remain secret, and GitBook says some of them may have been exposed.

Its investigation identified several potentially affected categories, including integration tokens, Git Sync secrets used to connect documentation with code repositories, and API tokens that allow software to communicate with GitBook automatically.

The incident has already prompted a response from the cryptocurrency industry, where documentation websites often serve as an important source of official instructions.

Decentralized exchange PancakeSwap temporarily disabled its documentation following news of the incident, although it said its own systems had not been compromised.

In a public statement, PancakeSwap said its “docs are not compromised and [the] PancakeSwap app is not affected.” The exchange nevertheless took its documentation offline as a precaution and redirected visitors to its homepage.

PancakeSwap reminded users that its documentation does not require them to perform wallet transactions, warning that “docs never ask you to connect your wallet or approve tokens.”

PancakeSwap subsequently announced that its documentation was back online after being moved from GitBook to infrastructure under its own control, with existing documentation links continuing to work.

GitBook says it blocked the attacker, addressed the vulnerabilities involved, replaced its own infrastructure credentials, and invalidated customer API tokens. But customers using GitBook alongside other services still have work to do.

The company's instructions begin with “rotate any existing GitBook API tokens,” meaning customers should replace their old digital access keys with newly generated ones.

GitBook also instructed customers to replace third-party API tokens and secrets used to connect external tools to its platform, including credentials associated with published integrations. Users of its Model Context Protocol, or MCP, server must authenticate again; MCP is a system that allows AI assistants and other software tools to connect to services such as GitBook.

#5036. The Best Epilators for At-Home Hair Removal in 2026, According to Experts

Here's why they're better than waxing, shaving, or a laser.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Fall’s signature cool, crisp temperatures have me reevaluating more than just my wardrobe. My skincare routine is due for a seasonal refresh—but one beauty ritual that I won’t skip is body hair removal. There’s nothing wrong with body hair, of course. But if you’re like me and prefer smooth, dolphin-like skin brushing against your matching workout sets and cozy sweater dresses, then chances are that you’ve tried a slew of hair removal methods.

Shaving is cheap and easy, but it requires daily maintenance. Depilatory hair removal creams are convenient, but they can easily irritate sensitive skin. Waxing results last up to six weeks, but the discomfort can also be unpleasant for those with sensitive skin. Some of our editors adore laser hair removal devices, but they’ll set you back at least $300. Many aren’t designed with Brown and Black skin tones like mine.

However, we’re all in on one hair removal technique: epilators. They resemble electric razors, but instead of trimming hairs at the surface, they pluck them from the root for long-lasting, smooth skin. Because the hair-free results last for two to four weeks, epilators are a great alternative to shaving and hair removal creams. Repeated sessions slow down hair growth, and your unwanted strands will be finer when they regrow, says dermatologist Hadley King, MD. They’re also gentler than laser hair removal devices, which is why Dr. King recommends them for anyone with sensitive skin or who isn't a good fit for laser hair removal.

My favorites start at just $9, so you can enjoy hair-free skin without putting a major dent in your beauty budget ahead of the holiday season. Plus, many of our recommendations are sold on Amazon, meaning you can take full advantage of the retailer’s signature two-day Prime shipping and enjoy hair-free legs before your upcoming weekend plans.

WH editors haven’t met a Braun device we don’t love—and this top-rated epilator is no exception. I love that it’s equipped with a wide, flexible head that flawlessly bends along the curves of my kneecaps, underarms, and my bikini line. Even better? It features an impressive number of micro-grip tweezers, which are much slimmer than traditional tweezers in the motor to ensure I get the closest hair removal possible. While it sounds intimidating, the sheer number of tweezers sped up my hair removal process.

This wet-dry device has an impressive 50-minute runtime which allows me to remove unwanted body hair over several sessions without worrying about it dying mid-epilating. It also has a relatively quick charging time of just an hour, which is shorter than other epilators I’ve come across in my research and testing process.

Despite the lightweight feel, I like how sturdy this device is and that it doesn’t easily slip out of my grip. Plus, it's waterproof so you can use your new epilator in the shower without worrying about it cranking out mid-shave.

And while a small detail, this device has a built-in “smartlight” that illuminates your skin while epilating, solidifying it as a must-have for women who like dimmer lighting while showering but still want to see what they're doing.

Truthfully, the only bummer about this device is that it’s the most expensive recommendation. However, we think its performance makes it worth it.

While it's not as effective as our best overall epilator, it gets the job done by removing all of our unwanted body hair in around 30 minutes. Most importantly, the pain is rather minimal. Not too shabby for a compact, travel-friendly pick!

Like our No. 1 pick, this more affordable tool is also equipped with a smartlight feature to illuminate your skin and spotlight inconspicuous body hairs. It’s also equipped with two speed settings, making it helpful for beginners and novices alike.

The biggest downside with this tool is that it’s not designed to remove unwanted facial hair. That said, you can pluck away rogue chin hairs or reach for the Amazon bestselling Kitsch dermaplaning tool that trims your brows and shaves your face to reveal smoother skin.

Epilators are unique because they’re equipped with built-in tweezers to pluck hairs from the root.

The smaller heads have fewer tweezers, but they're fantastic for targeted areas like the face, underarms, and bikini line.

Larger epilator heads are better for covering bigger areas like your legs. Having a lot of tweezers helps speed up your hair removal routine, but it also means you’ll have a more painful experience because it’s plucking dozens of hairs at a time. But like ripping a bandage off a hairy arm, it’s uncomfortable at first, but not too rough. And FWIW, Dr. King promises the pain level noticeably improves after multiple sessions. So be brave and patient, and it’ll all be over before you know it.

For the most effective hair removal, your body hair should be about the length of a sprinkle or grain of rice (about 0.25 inches or 6.35 mm). This standard for epilators is the same as waxing and sugaring. When your hair is too short, it's challenging for the epilator to grasp and remove the strands. You also don't want them to be longer than about two rice grains to help prevent hairs from getting tangled and minimize pain. Everyone's hair regrowth is different, but for most people, stubble typically occurs within one to two weeks.

It sounds like a small detail, but also consider the power source.

If you have minimal outlets in your home, then battery-powered makes sense. However, the hidden cost of replacing batteries can start to add up within the year. That said, all of the featured battery-operated devices in this story include a battery to help offset the long-term cost.

These devices are great because you won’t have to worry about them cranking out mid-epilating, but honestly the cords can get in the way. If you’ve got the extra funds, it’s best to invest in a cordless device so you can remove your unwanted body hair without stretching in odd positions or sitting on your bedroom floor. That said, there are some stellar corded options, including the relatively affordable $40 Philips Epilator Series 2000.

Water-resistant devices can be used wet or dry, so the cordless option can be used in your shower and the corded version works on your damp body if you prefer. Most aren’t waterproof, except the Braun Epilator Silk-épil 9 Flex 9-481 Beauty Set and the epilator head on the Colordiary Eyebrow Hair Remover.

As our beauty editor, I’m constantly on the hunt for the best products, and spend the vast majority of my time testing and reviewing the latest drops. I also regularly consult dermatologists about all things beauty, which is exactly what I did to kick off the selection process for our best epilators story. After talking with Dr. King (she’s one of my most trusted dermatological experts), I asked about her recommendations and insights into shopping for epilators. Then, our team tested several of her picks and compared them to some of the other epilators in our beauty closet.

We've been testing epilators since March 2021, and over time we've evaluated dozens of newer models with various features until we settled on these seven winners. In the years since, we've kept a close eye on the latest launches across all price points and have updated this story at least yearly to share our honest thoughts. For our most recent update, we tried 12 epilators and determined these seven are worthy of a place in our routine—and we're confident they'll equally impress you as well.

Over the last six weeks we've been trying a slew of options and kept the number of tweezers, power source, and water resistance in mind. Next, we paid attention to the number of attachments. We prioritized accessories that helped alleviate discomfort to ensure we have less intimidating options for beginners. Next, we considered each epilator's ease of use, noise level, grip strength, comfort, and efficacy at removing hair. Our team used these devices in our real lives and ahead of important events, like weddings, birthdays, and after exercise.

To narrow down the list even further, I recruited Women's Health colleagues, including Assistant Social Media Editor Isabel McMahon, to join the testing process. I also carefully vetted all of the top-rated picks across Amazon, Ulta, and Sephora. I also considered pricing. While yes, the top pick is over $250, it’s from Braun, one of the most-trusted names in the hair removal game. It also earned major snaps from McMahon and me. And FWIW, our other picks are $140 and under, with some in the sweet $10-$40 range.

If you’re interested in giving epilating a shot, the above are some highly reviewed options that will get you on your way to smooth, bare skin.

These devices look like chunky electric razors and are either manual like a traditional women’s razor or powered by batteries or an electrical outlet. When you run the device across your body, tiny tweezers pluck away unwanted body hair. So yes, the initial hair removal process will be a tad uncomfy, but the smooth results are so worth it, says Dr. King.

Like all other hair removal techniques, using an epilator is all about skin prep. Dr. King recommends a gentle physical exfoliation, such as a body scrub, gloves, mitt, loofah, or brush, before epilation. (Psst: I’ve been using Nécessaire The Body Exfoliant since 2019 and haven’t looked back since.) "This will help to expose hairs so they can be picked up by the epilator, and it also helps decrease the risk of ingrown hairs," says Dr. King.

#5034. Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun Accuses Trump-Backed World Liberty of Using UAE Ties to Escape Federal Court

Justin Sun’s legal fight with World Liberty Financial now hinges on a question of nationality.

The Tron founder said Tuesday on X that his lawyers have asked a federal court in California to sanction the Trump family-backed crypto venture over how long it took to disclose that its ownership chain gives it citizenship in the United Arab Emirates.

Sun says he is one of World Liberty’s earliest and largest investors. He agreed in November 2024 to buy $30 million of its WLFI tokens and later raised his total investment to $45 million.

In April, he sued the company, alleging it misled him into investing and then unlawfully froze his tokens. World Liberty has fired back in court, alleging that Sun shorted WLFI and drove down its price.

The current dispute turns on a technical point. Sun chose to sue in federal court, which can hear cases between parties from different places but generally can’t hear a foreign plaintiff’s case against a foreign defendant.

As Sun explained it, a company like World Liberty takes on the citizenship of each of its owners, so a single foreign owner makes the whole company foreign for those purposes.

According to Sun, World Liberty’s initial court disclosure listed only itself, with Florida as its sole citizenship. A judge signaled at an August hearing that some of Sun’s claims should stay in open court rather than go to arbitration.

Less than a week later, Sun says, the company amended the disclosure to list 22 members in its ownership chain, with citizenship in Florida, Delaware, Puerto Rico, and the UAE. World Liberty now argues that the federal court lacks jurisdiction over the case, according to Sun.

Sun argues the late disclosure was a tactic to move the case out of federal court, and his motion seeks to make World Liberty and its lawyers bear the cost. He contrasted the company’s position with its own marketing, posting excerpts from World Liberty’s “gold paper” that describe it as “an American, tax-paying, corporation.”

Once the sanctions motion is resolved, Sun said, he will refile his lawsuit in California state court. “I will keep fighting for the transparency this community deserves,” he wrote.

The feud has played out publicly for months. Sun has criticized how World Liberty treats its investors and alleged that the company threatened to burn his WLFI tokens.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD falling into Tuesday’s daily close as election results came in, with Democrats going on to win control of both the House and Senate. The pair dropped to a low of $75,618 on Bitstamp, a level last seen on Sept. 16, before rebounding as high as $78,039 over the next three hours.

BTC/USD one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

Data from CoinGlass showed the price dip liquidating late BTC long positions below $76,000, while a wall of asks kept upside contained below $78,000. Total crypto long liquidations over the past 24 hours stood at $288 million.

BTC liquidation heatmap. Source: CoinGlass

In comments sent to Cointelegraph, Kelly Ye, head of research at institutional digital asset trading platform Talos, said that markets had largely priced in the election outcome.

“Bitcoin moved about 1.7% after the polls closed, close to the roughly 1.8% options had priced, and there was no major leverage flush. The election was the main event, but markets had largely prepared for it,” she said.

US stock futures showed similar moves, initially falling before recouping some losses. S&P 500 and Nasdaq 100 futures were both down by about 0.3% at the time of writing.

S&P 500 futures one-hour chart. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

“I think we’ll work with the Democrats, and we’ll get along very well with them,” he said.

Bitcoin’s drop to $75,618 coincided with its 50-week simple moving average (SMA), a trend line that has held as support since mid-September.

“If this continues then these newfound 3-Day Lower Highs may guide price back towards the ~$75.5k level below over time,” he said on Wednesday.

#5030. CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike Launches AI Agent 'Kill Switch,' Plans to Acquire Fiddler AI

CrowdStrike has introduced an AI agent kill switch designed to stop unauthorized activity inside corporate networks, alongside plans to acquire AI observability startup Fiddler as businesses give increasingly autonomous software access to their systems.

The cybersecurity company announced the launch Tuesday at its Fal.Con Europe 2026 conference in Barcelona, expanding its Falcon Guardian security platform with controls that let administrators intervene when AI agents stray beyond their assigned tasks.

The move tackles a problem companies face as they deploy AI systems capable of executing code, accessing sensitive information, and interacting with business applications. Unlike conventional chatbots, these agents can perform sequences of actions with limited human oversight, potentially causing damage even when nobody has deliberately attacked them.

In practice, an agent authorized to organize corporate files might misinterpret its instructions, follow malicious directions embedded in a document, or execute a command its operator never intended. The same access that makes autonomous agents useful can also make their mistakes expensive.

The new AI Kill Switch gives security administrators a way to interrupt an agent's activity without waiting for it to complete its task. According to CrowdStrike's technical explanation, the currently available mechanism works through AI gateways, which can block an agent's access to the models powering its decisions.

The initial release does not yet provide every type of containment CrowdStrike has described. The company plans to expand the feature to terminate agent processes directly on endpoints and coordinate restrictions across cloud environments and other enterprise systems.

An authorized administrator triggers the kill switch, and the action is recorded in an audit trail. CrowdStrike also describes controls to prevent terminated agents from restarting and restrict their network access, with broader enforcement capabilities still under development.

The company is adding protections at other points where agents operate. Falcon Guardian can inspect agent prompts and apply policies to tools including Claude Code, OpenAI Codex, GitHub Copilot, Microsoft 365 Copilot, Cursor, and OpenCode.

The risk is already well documented. As Decrypt previously reported, Microsoft researchers identified a vulnerability in Anthropic's Claude Code GitHub Action that could have allowed attackers to manipulate the coding agent into exposing sensitive credentials through malicious repository content. Anthropic subsequently patched the flaw but Crowdstrike’s solution would be more extensive.

CrowdStrike also signed an agreement to acquire Fiddler AI, a company specializing in monitoring AI models and explaining their behavior.

The deal values Fiddler at roughly $400 million, according to The Wall Street Journal. CrowdStrike has not publicly disclosed the acquisition price, although it said the transaction would be paid primarily in cash, with some stock subject to vesting conditions.

Fiddler's technology addresses a related problem that a kill switch alone cannot solve: understanding what an AI agent was doing before something went wrong, and whether its actions were appropriate in the first place.

Its software evaluates agents before deployment, traces their decisions and tool calls, and monitors changes in behavior after they begin operating. These records can help security teams investigate unexpected actions or provide evidence to auditors.

CrowdStrike expects to complete the Fiddler acquisition during the fourth quarter of fiscal 2027, which ends January 31, 2027, subject to customary closing conditions. Its expanded endpoint kill-switch capabilities have not yet received a specific release date.

#5028. Democrats win US House to secure control of Congress in midterms

Democrats will take control of House committees in January 2027, giving them power to investigate President Donald Trump’s administration.

Update (Nov. 4, 2026, 11:24 am UTC): This article has been updated to include comments from CertiK’s Stefan Muehlbauer on the election results and the outlook for US crypto legislation.

Democrats have won control of both chambers of the US Congress in the midterm elections, giving the party greater influence over legislation and oversight of President Donald Trump’s administration.

The party won House seats in districts where Trump beat his Democratic opponent by as much as 18 percentage points in 2024. Democrats also flipped at least seven Senate seats in Kansas, Iowa, Texas, Ohio, Maine, North Carolina and South Carolina, according to the Associated Press.

The result could reshape the outlook for crypto legislation and bring greater congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked digital asset businesses. Democratic committee leaders will gain more influence over which bills advance and which industry activities face investigation.

Stefan Muehlbauer, head of US government affairs at CertiK, said the results could derail the CLARITY crypto market structure bill.

“The immediate read on this election result is that CLARITY bill is likely dead,” he said in comments shared with Cointelegraph, referring to both the upcoming lame-duck session and the next Congress.

Muehlbauer said he expected Sen. Elizabeth Warren and Rep. Maxine Waters, both outspoken crypto critics, to chair the Senate Banking Committee and House Financial Services Committee, respectively.

He also said Democrats had fallen short of the Senate’s 60-vote threshold for overcoming a filibuster, limiting their ability to advance legislation.

Democrats will take control of House committees in January 2027, allowing them to issue subpoenas and investigate the Trump administration, the president’s family and their business interests, according to NBC News.

Financial markets commentary publication The Kobeissi Letter pointed to affordability as the defining election issue, citing high energy costs and renewed interest rate increases.

Gokhshtein Media founder David Gokhshtein said Republicans had performed worse than he expected.

“Well, Republicans got their asses handed to them. I thought they’d do better. I was wrong,” he wrote on X Wednesday.

Prediction markets had favored a Democratic sweep. Traders on Kalshi and Polymarket put the probability of Democrats winning both chambers at roughly 75% on Tuesday, before the results were announced.

#5027. Anthropic's AI Is Getting Cheaper to Use. That's Becoming a Problem for Its Business

Anthropic's AI Is Getting Cheaper to Use. That's Becoming a Problem for Its Business

Anthropic is making Claude cheaper to use. Its computing bill, however, is moving in the other direction.

Anthropic said revenue growth from increased usage was "partially offset by a decrease of approximately $600 million from price reductions introduced as part of new model versions and contractual discounts," according to its amended SEC filing.

Meanwhile, compute and infrastructure expenses surged from $5.56 billion to $9.86 billion, an increase of roughly 77%.

That means Anthropic spent roughly 58 cents on compute and infrastructure for every dollar of revenue, versus 48 cents a quarter earlier. The measure includes computing used for research, training, and internal operations—not just serving paying Claude customers—so it isn't a per-prompt profit margin.

The $600 million figure isn't revenue that vanished from Anthropic's books. It's the amount the company says price reductions and discounts offset revenue growth driven by higher usage.

The cuts are not hypothetical. Claude Opus 5.5, which lowered token prices compared with Opus 5, was recently released, being more capable than any previous model, and sometimes even up to par against Fable. Anthropic also introduced Haiku 5.5 in October, pitching a much cheaper model for high-volume work.

Anthropic says Fable 5.1 reduced the cost of typical workloads by around 25%, while Opus 5.5 brought customers' cost per task down roughly 40% compared with Opus 5. The reductions reflect both lower prices and efficiency gains, rather than straight discounts across every task. Anthropic detailed Fable's lower cache-read pricing in its model announcement.

There is a business case for that strategy: cheaper, more capable AI can encourage customers to use more of it. Anthropic says users have historically expanded consumption after such releases.

But its latest quarter also shows how difficult it is to synchronize demand with supply. Anthropic typically reserves compute capacity 12 to 18 months before it becomes available, leaving much of that spending relatively fixed in the near term. It’s also infamous among AI enthusiasts because its quotas tend to deplete too easily.

The quarter included $5 billion of compute under short-term commitments that Anthropic says cost significantly more than comparable capacity secured through multiyear contracts. That expense helped push the company from a $974 million operating profit in the second quarter to a $567 million operating loss in the third.

The longer-term stakes are bigger. Anthropic's IPO filing, means the company is preparing to go public, and put the company in Wall Street’s bulls eye. Its updated disclosures put aggregate lease and purchasing commitments at roughly $895.4 billion, substantially all related to compute and infrastructure.

Reuters has also reported that the filing raised questions about whether infrastructure partners can secure the financing needed to deliver all that capacity. As of now, Google and Nvidia seem to be the most benefited from Anthropic’s growth as its core business depends on their infrastructure.

For now, demand for Claude is still growing rapidly. The harder question is whether Anthropic can keep making its models cheaper faster than the cost of building and running them comes down. Right now, OpenAI, which is also preparing its own IPO offers a competitive model, with many usage resets, and a more generous usage quota.

That’s the main issue keeping Anthropic from rising prices.

#5023. Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin, CEO Says—About 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

Tether Holds 150,000 Bitcoin, CEO Says—About 50,000 More Than Its Reserve Report Shows

Tether CEO Paolo Ardoino said the stablecoin giant now holds around 150,000 Bitcoin, worth roughly $11.5 billion at current prices, a figure that exceeds what the company’s own reserve disclosures imply by some 50,000 coins.

“We have give or take 150,000 bitcoin ourselves. It’s not much, but it’s honest work,” Ardoino said during a keynote at the Plan ₿ Forum in Lugano, Switzerland, the city where Tether has run a Bitcoin and stablecoin adoption program since 2022.

The claim is notable because it doesn’t square neatly with the paper trail. Tether’s third-quarter reserve attestation, which covers the assets backing its USDT stablecoin, lists $8.37 billion of Bitcoin valued at $83,621 per coin, implying roughly 100,060 BTC.

That leaves about 50,000 coins, worth close to $3.8 billion, that its public filings do not yet account for. Ardoino did not say where the additional Bitcoin is held or when it was acquired, so his comment doesn’t indicate a fresh purchase of that size, and Tether has long maintained that its corporate Bitcoin investments sit separately from the reserves supporting USDT.

The stablecoin issuer has been accumulating Bitcoin for years. In May 2023, Tether said it would funnel up to 15% of its net realized operating profits into Bitcoin, and that it would custody the coins itself rather than rely on third-party custodians.

The strategy has been fueled by staggering profits: the company reported $1.5 billion in net operating profit in the third quarter alone, and it has posted billion-dollar quarterly profits throughout the year while its USDT supply has kept growing.

The broader reserve picture, as laid out in the Q3 report, shows $191.45 billion in assets against $183.50 billion in liabilities, leaving an excess cushion of $7.95 billion, nearly double the $4.11 billion buffer it reported in June. U.S. Treasury bills remain the bulk of reserves at about $132.4 billion.

The transparency gap is a sensitive subject for Tether, which has faced years of scrutiny over whether USDT is fully backed. The company began its first full financial audit with KPMG this year, a long-awaited step toward addressing those doubts.

Ardoino also used the Lugano stage to tout Tether’s scale, saying the company now serves more than 730 million users worldwide and is adding roughly 30 million wallets a quarter, as he pitched a suite of open-source technologies funded by its profits.

#5020. Binance hackers move 81% of stolen Solana, route funds via THORChain

The Binance attackers moved most of the stolen SOL and continued laundering funds through THORChain after the protocol resumed swaps.

The attackers behind the $6.3 billion Binance hack have moved about 81% of the stolen Solana (SOL) as they continue laundering assets through the cross-chain swap protocol THORChain.

Wallets grouped under the Binance hacker entity held 182,443 SOL (worth $16.9 million), or roughly 18.8% of the total 970,522 SOL stolen during the exploit, according to blockchain data platform Arkham.

Lookonchain reported on Wednesday that the attackers split SOL across multiple wallets, selling it for USDC, bridging to Ethereum to buy Ether, and ultimately swapping ETH for Bitcoin (BTC) via THORChain.

Binance suffered a record-setting hack on Thursday after attackers compromised employee devices through fake job interviews and malicious software downloads, the exchange said. Binance said Tuesday it replenished 98% of affected tokens and would absorb losses.

The US Federal Bureau of Investigation attributed the attack to North Korea’s TraderTraitor hackers on Saturday and urged crypto service providers, including THORNode operators, to block transactions linked to stolen assets.

Kim Yo Jong, sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, dismissed the accusations as “absurd rhetoric.”

North Korea-linked hacking groups are suspected of using THORChain to launder proceeds from some of the largest cryptocurrency hacks, including the $1.4 billion Bybit hack.

THORChain restored trading on Bitcoin, Ethereum and several other chains on Wednesday. Services had been halted on Saturday after an Ethereum block reorganization exposed a refund bug. The halt inadvertently delayed Binance hackers’ laundering activity, according to Chainalysis.

Blockchain investigator SomaXBT estimated that about 54% of the attackers’ more than 47,600 transactions on Tron and Solana were automated.

Binance hackers expanded their laundering activity to Solana on Sunday after emptying most of their temporary holding wallets on Tron. Chainalysis estimated that about $500 million in total value moved through Tron, including repeated transfers between wallets.

#5019. OpenAI Wants to Rebrand AI Data Centers, Use Them to 'Protect the Grid'

OpenAI Wants to Rebrand AI Data Centers, Use Them to 'Protect the Grid'

OpenAI wants its massive AI data centers to help keep the lights on when electricity grids come under pressure. And, apparently, it would also prefer that people stop calling them AI data centers.

The ChatGPT developer announced its Grid Protectors Program on Monday, a national initiative designed to let future AI facilities reduce electricity consumption during emergencies and periods of high demand, potentially lowering costs for other customers.

Under the plan, OpenAI would coordinate with utility companies to determine how much electricity its facilities could temporarily stop using, how quickly they could respond, and for how long. The company is also exploring on-site batteries and power generation to help manage demand.

“Families and small businesses should not be asked to subsidize a compute campus’s electricity needs,” OpenAI said in its announcement.

The program addresses one of the biggest complaints surrounding the AI infrastructure boom: companies are building increasingly power-hungry facilities while residents worry about paying higher—and water—electricity bills to support them.

OpenAI is using a planned facility in Effingham County, Georgia, as its first example. The company has signed a 25-year agreement with Georgia Power covering approximately 3,200 megawatts of electricity demand, with up to 1,000 megawatts available for temporary reductions during periods of high demand.

Under the approved agreement, OpenAI must cover the full infrastructure costs required to serve its facility, rather than passing those expenses on to existing customers.

Georgia Power also projects approximately $950 million in annual customer savings beginning in 2029, equivalent to at least $15 per month for a typical residential customer. However, those estimates cover the utility's broader portfolio of large electricity customers and expected growth, not just OpenAI's project.

The Georgia facility is still under development, and the savings remain projections. OpenAI has not yet disclosed binding demand-reduction commitments for other facilities under its national program.

OpenAI's electricity initiative also arrives after reports of a massive PR campaign to change what people call the facilities in the first place to erase from collective memory the negative sentiment towards AI data centers.

According to Axios, OpenAI is preparing a post-midterm policy and PR campaign that includes rebranding data centers as "AI compute campuses," an effort to make the infrastructure sound more appealing to communities, effectively trying to solve a problem by changing the problem’s name.

The company has already started using the terminology. Its Grid Protectors announcement repeatedly refers to future facilities as "compute campuses," although it occasionally uses the more familiar term "data centers."

An OpenAI spokesperson confirmed the naming strategy to Business Insider, arguing that the term better reflects the facilities' broader role in computing, energy infrastructure, and skilled employment.

The reported rebranding comes as opposition to AI infrastructure projects has become a political issue across the United States. Pennsylvania introduced new restrictions on large data centers in August, including provisions designed to protect residents from higher electricity costs. Other states are following.

OpenAI and six other major technology companies also signed a White House pledge in March to pay for the electricity and grid upgrades their AI projects require.

The new program goes further by proposing that AI facilities could become useful to utilities rather than simply adding pressure to their systems. But the national framework is still largely an ambition, with individual agreements to be negotiated.

OpenAI's Georgia project will provide an early test of that approach. Electricity delivery to the planned 3.2-gigawatt facility is scheduled to begin in 2028 and expand in phases through 2032.

#5016. Judge Orders Bitcoin Wallet Returned to Family of Convicted Canadian Killer

Judge Orders Bitcoin Wallet Returned to Family of Convicted Canadian Killer

A Canadian judge has ordered that a laptop containing a Bitcoin wallet be returned to the family of William Sandeson, who is serving a life sentence for the 2015 murder of fellow university student Taylor Samson during a drugs deal, Global News reported.

Justice John Bodurtha of the Nova Scotia Supreme Court ruled on Tuesday that prosecutors had not proved the cryptocurrency was linked to the killing. Sandeson, who appeared virtually at the hearing in Halifax, had filed a motion in April seeking the return of personal items seized when police searched his apartment after his arrest in 2015.

"The Crown has not met its burden to demonstrate the cryptocurrency was used as part of the drug transaction, proceeds of crime, or otherwise tainted by criminality," Bodurtha said, adding that its potential use in the deal was "hypothetical."

The judge did rule that about $3,600 (C$5,000) in cash found in Sandeson's apartment after the murder was "offence-related property," meaning it will not be returned.

Sandeson has said the Bitcoin was worth between about $5,800 and $7,200 (C$8,000 to C$10,000), according to Global News. A lawyer representing Samson's family in a separate wrongful death lawsuit, in which the cryptocurrency had been at the center, has said it could now be worth more than $144,000 (C$200,000). Global News did not report how much Bitcoin the wallet holds.

Sandeson, then 23 and about to start medical school at Dalhousie University, arranged for Samson, 22, to come to his apartment on August 15, 2015, to sell him cannabis that Sandeson had arranged to buy for C$40,000, according to prosecutors at his 2023 trial. Sandeson shot Samson, kept the drugs and the money, and later admitted to dumping his body in a river that feeds the Bay of Fundy. Samson's remains have never been found.

Sandeson was convicted of first-degree murder in 2017, but an appeals court overturned the verdict in 2020. At a second trial in 2023, he admitted to shooting Samson but said he acted in self-defense. A jury found him guilty of second-degree murder, which carries an automatic life sentence. He is serving his sentence in Quebec and will be eligible for parole in 2030.

#5013. US midterm elections 2026 as it happened: Democrats win control of Congress

Democrats won control of Congress in the 2026 US midterms. Catch up on key races, crypto-backed candidates and the outlook for crypto regulation.

Democrats have won control of both chambers of the US Congress, reshaping the outlook for cryptocurrency legislation and giving the party greater power to scrutinize President Donald Trump’s crypto ventures during the final two years of his term.

Live coverage has ended. Read our updates on key congressional races, crypto-backed candidates and election-night reactions below. Results and race calls are based on Associated Press reporting. Entries appear in reverse chronological order.

Republican Sen. Pete Ricketts has defeated independent challenger Dan Osborn in Nebraska, where crypto political action committee Fairshake had reportedly spent nearly $11 million supporting Ricketts.

More recent spending data shows Fairshake-affiliated crypto super PAC Defend American Jobs spent approximately $13.5 million opposing Osborn, according to Federal Election Commission data compiled by Bankrolled as of Nov. 3.

Ricketts, a member of the Senate Banking Committee, has supported cryptocurrency legislation including the CLARITY Act and GENIUS Act. He voted to advance the CLARITY Act out of committee in May and supported an unsuccessful Senate procedural vote on the legislation in September, according to crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto.

Democrats have won control of the US House of Representatives, according to the Associated Press, flipping a chamber Republicans have held since 2023. Rep. Hakeem Jeffries is in line to become Speaker.

Control of the House gives Democrats the committee chairs and subpoena power. NBC says that includes oversight of Trump’s family and business ventures.

Democrat Troy Jackson defeated Republican Sen. Susan Collins in Maine, according to the Associated Press.

Collins was one of four Republicans to oppose advancing the CLARITY Act in September, expressing concerns over its impact on bank deposits.

Unlike Collins, Jackson has expressed support for clear cryptocurrency legislation in the run-up to the election. According to Stand with Crypto, which rates Jackson as “very pro crypto,” he called to end the debanking of “lawful crypto businesses, and ensure Americans’ right to self-custody digital assets.”

“My message is congratulations to the Democrats, and I look forward to working with you for the good of our country,” President Donald Trump told CBS News’ senior White House correspondent Weijia Jiang.

Democrats have won control of the US Senate, according to the Associated Press.

The majority was secured after Democrat Josh Turek won the Senate race in Iowa, bringing Democrats and independents who caucus with them to 51 seats.

The result reshapes the outlook for the stalled CLARITY Act and determines which lawmakers lead committees overseeing the industry.

Democratic control could also bring more congressional scrutiny of Trump-linked crypto businesses and regulators’ decisions. Control of the Senate could also affect confirmation of future presidential nominees to regulatory posts.

“It’s too early to say exactly what this means for crypto, but one thing is clear: nothing will pass without cross-partisan support,” Jerome de Tychey, president of Ethereum France and co-founder of EthCC, told Cointelegraph.

Democrats are now in control of 50 Senate seats — one short of a majority — after flipping five seats in GOP states, some that hadn’t seen a Democratic senator in power for decades.

Earlier, Decision Desk HQ projected the Democratic Party to win a majority in the US House of Representatives.

In Ohio, Democrat Sherrod Brown, a longtime crypto critic, beat incumbent Republican Senator Jon Husted in a race that has attracted approximately $63 million in crypto industry spending.

That includes about $56 million from Fairshake and $7 million from the Winklevoss-backed Digital Freedom Fund, with both groups spending to support Husted or oppose Brown.

Crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto characterizes Brown as strongly opposed to cryptocurrency, citing 17 statements and his 2024 vote against overturning the SEC’s SAB 121 crypto custody accounting guidance.

Brown was also targeted by crypto industry spending in 2024, when Fairshake affiliate Defend American Jobs spent more than $40 million supporting Republican Bernie Moreno, who defeated Brown in that year’s Senate election.

Democrat James Talarico has won Texas’ US Senate race against Republican Ken Paxton, according to the Associated Press, flipping another Republican-held seat.

Talarico becomes the first Democrat to win a statewide election in Texas since 1994. As of 11 pm ET, he led Paxton by approximately 1.09 million votes, with 56.1% to Paxton’s 42.2%, according to Decision Desk HQ. Counting was continuing.

Paxton received support from the Winklevoss-backed Digital Freedom Fund, although both candidates earned A ratings from crypto advocacy group Stand With Crypto.

Paxton has called for the passage of the CLARITY Act, arguing that clear regulations are necessary to maintain US competitiveness in digital assets. In a June statement, he said the country must become the “crypto capital of the world” and support innovation and growth.

Talarico has similarly called for clear regulations that protect consumers while allowing innovation. As a Texas state representative, he also voted in 2025 to establish a state strategic Bitcoin reserve, according to legislative records cited by Stand With Crypto.

Democrat Annie Andrews has defeated Republican Sen. Darline Graham in South Carolina, according to the Associated Press, giving Democrats another pickup.

Andrews’ victory ends more than two decades of Republican control of the seat. The last time South Carolina elected a Democrat to the Senate was in 1998.

Following their victory in North Carolina, Democrats would need two further Republican-held seats to reach a majority, provided they retain all their Democratic-held seats.

Decision Desk HQ has projected the Democratic Party to win a majority in the US House of Representatives.

Democrats and allied independents stood at 45 Senate seats as of 10:02 pm ET, six short of the 51 needed for a majority.

Their path includes retaining Democratic-held seats in Oregon, Michigan and Minnesota and flipping three additional Republican-held seats. Democrats have already picked up North Carolina.

NBC News has projected that Democrat Annie Andrews will defeat incumbent Republican Senator Darline Graham in South Carolina in a major upset. Associated Press has not yet called this race.

Republican Representative Andy Barr has won Kentucky’s US Senate race, sending a supporter of the CLARITY and GENIUS Acts to the upper chamber.

Fairshake-affiliated political action committees spent more than $7 million supporting Barr in the Republican primary. Barr has also introduced legislation aimed at protecting lawful businesses, including crypto firms, from debanking.

Democrat Juliana Stratton has won Illinois’ US Senate race, succeeding retiring Democratic Sen. Dick Durbin.

Stratton advanced through the Democratic primary despite substantial opposition from crypto-backed PAC Fairshake, which had spent more than $5.5 million against her by early March.

Republican Representative Harriet Hageman has won Wyoming’s US Senate race against Democrat James Byrd, succeeding retiring crypto advocate Senator Cynthia Lummis.

Lummis, who chairs the Senate Banking Subcommittee on Digital Assets, sponsored the BITCOIN Act of 2025 to establish a US strategic Bitcoin reserve and has supported the CLARITY and GENIUS Acts.

Hageman has also backed cryptocurrency legislation, voting to pass the CLARITY and GENIUS Acts in 2025 and the FIT21 market structure bill in 2024. She also cosponsored legislation seeking to prohibit the Federal Reserve from issuing a central bank digital currency, according to Stand With Crypto. The advocacy group lists no documented position on cryptocurrency for Byrd.

Kalshi and Polymarket traders now give Democrats a 99% chance of winning Senate control and price House control at nearly 100%. It came as Democrats flipped North Carolina’s Senate seat, while holding Georgia and New Hampshire.

Prediction markets increasingly favored Democrats taking control of both chambers of Congress on Tuesday evening.

As of 8:05 pm ET, the Blockchain Association’s election dashboard showed Democratic Senate-control odds at 94% on Kalshi and 93% on Polymarket, up from 75% and 75.5%, respectively, earlier in the day. Democratic House-control odds stood at 98% and 99%.

At the time, Democrats and allied independents have secured 42 Senate seats to Republicans’ 31.

Massachusetts Senator Ed Markey and Delaware Senator Chris Coons, both Democrats, win reelection, according to the Associated Press.

Markey voted against the GENIUS stablecoin bill in 2025, while Coons has criticized President Donald Trump’s crypto ventures, calling his latest memecoin-holder event “corruption.”

Democratic Sen. Jon Ossoff has won reelection in Georgia, beating Republican Representative Mike Collins.

Ossoff expressed support for impeaching Trump in April 2025 over plans to host a private dinner for top holders of the president’s TRUMP memecoin, arguing that the arrangement amounted to selling access to the presidency.

Maylea Ma, deputy general counsel at 1inch, said she expects federal agencies to drive the next phase of crypto regulation.

The election could reshape the path forward for the stalled CLARITY Act, but Ma said “real movement” in US digital-asset regulation over the next few years will come from the US Securities and Exchange Commission, Commodity Futures Trading Commission and Treasury, rather than Congress.

Ma said those agencies were increasingly engaging with crypto projects. However, agency engagement does not provide the stability the industry had hoped to secure through legislation.

“The long-term goal, of course, remains a statute that cannot be reversed every time a government changes,” said Ma.

Democratic Rep. Debbie Wasserman Schultz wins Florida’s 20th Congressional District. Wasserman Schultz voted against the CLARITY crypto market structure bill when it passed the House in July 2025.

Democratic Senator Mark Warner has won reelection in Virginia, defeating Republican challenger Bert Mizusawa. Warner was widely favored to win.

Warner voted against advancing the CLARITY Act in September, arguing that crypto legislation needs safeguards against President Donald Trump personally benefiting from the industry. Warner supports establishing federal rules for digital assets, with his opposition to the bill focused on its ethics protections.

The first polls in the US midterm elections closed at 6 pm ET in parts of Indiana and Kentucky. The remaining polls in both states close at 7 pm ET.

Polls also close at 7 pm ET in Georgia and Virginia, followed by Ohio at 7:30 pm ET, where Republican Sen. Jon Husted faces Democrat Sherrod Brown in a race drawing major crypto industry spending.

More than 57 million Americans had cast ballots as of Monday, with early voting surpassing 2022 levels in several closely watched states, according to the Associated Press.

Current balance of power in the US House and Senate. Source: Blockchain Association

#5012. Midterms Blue Wave Makes $917K for One Polymarket Trader, If Alaska Doesn’t Flip

Midterms Blue Wave Makes $917K for One Polymarket Trader, If Alaska Doesn’t Flip

A "blue wave” swept the U.S. midterms as the Democrats took control of both the House of Representatives and the Senate, in a crushing blow to President Donald Trump.

Polymarket predictor 0xA2 is the platform's most profitable trader over the past 24 hours, according to the site's daily leaderboard. They are currently $917,000 in profit, although most of that is still in an active position that has a real (ableit small) chance of upset.

The user wagered $609,644 on the Republicans not winning any Senate swing states when odds sat at just 36%, a market which has since surged to 87%. This turned his stake in to $1.48 million, putting him $860,000 in the green.

Its important to remember: these are unrealized gains and could drop at any moment as 0xA2 waits for Alaska's final verdict. Polymarket traders put a 14% chance on an upset—and while this may seem small, the site has seen similarly sized upsets recently—as in the case of Brazil's presidential election two weeks ago.

0xA2 also profited $106,115 by predicting that the Democrats would win the Texas Senate—profits that have been realized. Oddly, the user's second largest active position is $1.42 million on the U.S. not confirming that aliens exist before 2027.

"Election night showed how quickly round-the-clock markets move when traditional exchanges are closed. As polls began closing at 6pm ET, Kalshi’s odds of a Democratic sweep went from about 74% to 99%," Kelly Ye, Head of Research at digital asset management firm Talos, said in a note shared with Decrypt. "Prediction-market trading peaked between 8pm and 10pm ET, led by a small number of large trades."

It was a big day for Polymarket users profiting, with the top 10 users on the platform profiting more than $170,000, per the leaderboard. To put that into perspective, on a fairly random day in March this year, the top daily winner on Polymarket profited $154,000, according to the WayBackMachine, and the 10th biggest winner that day gained $29,700.

The midterms attracted major activity on leading prediction markets Polymarket and Kalshi. According to Precition.com data, midterm markets attracted more than $546 million in notional volume over the past two weeks.

"The 2024 election was the breakthrough moment for prediction markets. It was their first major real-world test and the event that really drove volume and open interest," Dash, the pseudonymous data lead at Prediction.com, told Decrypt. "Today, sports dominate trading volume, but Politics is still the key driving factor for open interest."

During the 2024 presidential race, Polymarket became what Bernstein analysts called the world’s largest election prediction market, with its flagship market topping $3.6 billion in volume. Maybe more importantly, predictors favored Trump for most of the campaign, while traditional polls kept him neck and neck with Kamala Harris. Prediction market users took this as a sign that prediction markets are more accurate than traditional political polling.

"Increasingly, people are looking for accurate forecasts on election results," Dash said, "and prediction markets are one place they can be found."

Those forecasts aren't always accurate, though. Just two weeks ago, Polymarket predictors whiffed Brazil's presidential election, with users giving Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva about a 20% chance of winning ahead of election day, before he ultimately beat Flávio Bolsonaro to take a fourth term.

Experts told Decrypt at the time that Brazil’s prediction market ban had left the odds in the hands of traders with little local knowledge to correct the odds. Plus, experts warned that a 20% chance of something happening is still a very real chance.

"Ultimately, something that has an 80% chance of happening does not happen 20% of the time," Luke Nolan, senior researcher at CoinShares, said.

So that 14% chance that 0xA2 loses his $860,000 in profit should be taken seriously.

#5009. USDC Issuer Circle Quietly Cuts 5% of Staff in Profitable Quarter

USDC Issuer Circle Quietly Cuts 5% of Staff in Profitable Quarter

Circle, the company behind the USDC stablecoin, has cut about 5% of its workforce, according to its third-quarter earnings release on Wednesday.

The reduction was "designed to better align our organizational structure with our operating model and strategic priorities," the company said in footnotes to its financial tables. Circle did not say how many employees were affected or which teams were cut.

It recorded $9.1 million in restructuring expenses in the quarter, mostly cash payments for notice periods and severance, employee benefits and related costs. Those were partly offset by a $5.7 million reversal of stock-based compensation, as unvested shares held by departing employees were forfeited.

The cuts came in a profitable quarter. Circle reported net income from continuing operations of $226 million, up 5% from a year earlier, on total revenue and reserve income of $865 million, up 17%. Adjusted EBITDA jumped 85% year-on-year to $317 million.

Much of that growth came from outside Circle's core business. Other revenue rose to $207 million, including $182 million from the presale of ARC, the token for its Arc blockchain. Reserve income, the interest Circle earns on the assets backing USDC and its largest source of revenue, fell 8% to $658 million as interest rates on those reserves declined.

The average amount of USDC in circulation rose 8% from a year earlier to $73.3 billion, but ended the quarter flat at $73.9 billion. USDC's share of the U.S. dollar stablecoin market slipped to 27%, according to the company.

CEO Jeremy Allaire did not mention the job cuts in his comments on the results, or in a tweet on Wednesday calling Q3 "a big quarter for Circle." He said USDC carried 79% of all stablecoin transaction volume, up from 66% a year earlier, and that Circle's payments network ended the quarter at $41 billion in annualized volume, up 15-fold. "In January, the GENIUS Act takes effect and regulated digital dollars go mainstream," Allaire wrote. "Every layer of our platform is live and ready for it."

→ USDC carried 79% of all stablecoin transaction volume, up from 66% a year ago, and $13.9T moved onchain (+49%)

→ A record $208B of USDC was minted and redeemed, about $2.3B every day

→ Arc mainnet went live, with 100+ institutions and apps… pic.twitter.com/euVfyHBUXX

— Jeremy Allaire (@jerallaire) November 4, 2026

The cuts come as Circle expands. In September, it launched the public mainnet of its Arc blockchain, with BlackRock, DTCC and Visa among its permissioned validators. Allaire said in October that Circle plans to enter about 40 emerging and developing markets, and the company has since teamed up with payments-technology firm ACI Worldwide to pilot USDC settlement on Colombia's instant-payments network.

Circle is the latest in a string of crypto companies to cut staff this year, citing weaker markets, artificial intelligence or both.

Coinbase cut 14% of its workforce in May, with CEO Brian Armstrong pointing to the crypto downturn and the company's adoption of AI. The Ethereum Foundation cut 20% of its staff in June as part of a reorganization, while custody firm BitGo cut 15% that month to focus on AI and stablecoins.

Exchange Luno cut a fifth of its global staff in July, citing automation, and crypto ETF issuer Bitwise cut 14% of its staff in August.

#5007. Bitcoin ETFs lose $1.16B as Bitwise calls selling ‘short-term’

Bitwise’s André Dragosch attributed Bitcoin ETF outflows to weaker market sentiment, saying institutions have largely maintained their exposure.

US spot Bitcoin exchange-traded funds (ETFs) recorded more than $1 billion in net outflows over three trading days, but crypto asset manager Bitwise says the withdrawals do not signal a lasting retreat by institutional investors.

Investors pulled another $116 million from the funds on Tuesday, bringing net outflows since Friday to $1.16 billion, according to SoSoValue data.

“I do think it’s related to a short-term risk-off environment rather than a structural change in institutional sentiment,” Bitwise’s head of research, André Dragosch, told Cointelegraph.

The withdrawals followed Binance’s $6.3 billion hack on Thursday, which raised concerns about investor confidence and ETF custody security.

Dragosch attributed the outflows to weaker market sentiment rather than fears over the safety of ETF holdings.

Dragosch said ETF flows reflect both long-term institutional adoption and shorter-term changes in investor sentiment.

He cited a recent Bitwise survey of 15 institutions, none of which had reduced their Bitcoin ETF exposure during the bear market. US regulatory filings also showed that most institutional investor categories, apart from hedge funds, had largely maintained their positions.

Related: Bitcoin ETF inflows leave institutional demand unclear: CoinShares

Retail investors still account for the majority of Bitcoin ETF holdings and tend to react more strongly to market volatility, according to Dragosch.

Bitwise’s analysis found that ETF inflows are closely tracked broader crypto market sentiment, which weakened around the Binance hack, he said.

Tuesday’s $116 million in outflows marked a slowdown from Monday’s $399 million and Friday’s $643 million, extending the funds’ withdrawal streak to three trading sessions.

The Crypto Fear & Greed Index rose to 34 from 26 on Monday, though it remained in the “Fear” category. Bitcoin gained about 1.6% over the past 24 hours, to trade near $78,000 at publication, according to CoinGecko.

The latest withdrawals brought the funds’ net outflows for 2026 to approximately $153 million.

Related: Binance traces $6.3B hack to fake job interviews, compromised AI tool

The Binance hack has raised concerns among some industry observers about crypto ETFs custody, particularly because major funds rely on a small number of custodians.

Dragosch pointed to differences between the security arrangements used by crypto exchanges and institutional custodians, noting that the Binance incident affected hot wallets, which are connected to the internet, rather than cold wallets.

“In contrast, institutional funds held at Coinbase custody are custodied in cold storage which, by definition, are not connected to the internet,” Dragosch said.

#5006. It Just Got Easier to Get Peptides in the US. Here's What Could Come Next.

Spoiler: More access doesn’t necessarily mean more safety.

ON WEDNESDAY, THE U.S. Food and Drug Administration announced a new policy that makes six experimental peptides more readily available. According to a document released by the HHS, the government will not penalize specialized pharmacies for making and supplying these popular substances to patients with a prescription. The peptides: BPC-157, epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500.

This does not mean these particular peptides have been approved for use by the FDA. In fact, none of them have gone through the rigorous human testing required for FDA drug approval. And the new policy is not intended to be permanent. Instead, it allows temporary access as the FDA decides on a longer-term plan for the six peptides. It also includes the caveat that compounders report “serious adverse events, and product quality issues to FDA and to the States where the products are compounded,” according to the document.

This topic has been red-hot since July, when the FDA held an advisory committee meeting to consider whether to add these peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply. These substances are already widely used and obtained on the gray market. If people are going to use them, advocates have argued, it would be safest if those substances came from licensed pharmacies and are used with physician oversight.

Critics, on the other hand—including FDA scientists—are concerned that the government may be opening the floodgates to under-studied and potentially harmful drugs. These substances, they note, have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness. And some of these peptides are already being promoted and sold on the basis of scientific claims that experts say are unsupported or questionable.

Here's what could come next, if you're thinking about taking one of these six compounded peptides.

Some parts of the current conversation played out at last month’s PepMed conference in Washington, D.C. A sign pointing guests to the registration table featured a stylized gold peptide overlaid on the white dome of the U.S. Capitol. PepMed’s organizer, the American Academy of Peptide Medicine (AAPM), founded just last year, has already become a major voice in this debate.

In advance of the FDA’s July meeting, AAPM submitted hundreds of pages to a crowded public docket, including information about each of the seven peptides then under review. Several of its advisory board members spoke during the meeting’s public comment sessions. And the group appears to have the ear of the administration. PepMed speakers included the assistant secretary for health, Admiral Brian Christine, and a host of prominent MAHA figures.

The September event opened with an on-the-record media hour, featuring a dozen speakers, including physicians and owners and CEOs of compounding pharmacies and telehealth companies. A recurring theme was the danger of the gray market, where peptide quality can be difficult or impossible to verify and consumers often make treatment decisions without medical oversight—“this is not a victory for patient safety,” said Ricardo Rossello, PhD, a U.S. congressional delegate for Puerto Rico and AAPM advisory board member who co-founded Beijing Prosperous Biopharm, a medical research company.

Many of the speakers also lamented the failure of the U.S. health care system to fully meet the needs of patients, and some of the day’s speakers, including Alex Tatem, MD, an Indiana-based urologist and men’s health specialist with a large YouTube following, presented experimental peptides as part of the solution. “Our established medical system is very much a sick care model,” said Tatem, who serves on the AAPM’s medical advisory board. It’s important to help patients in a health crisis, he told me in an interview. But people want to be proactive about disease prevention. In his view, the peptides under FDA’s consideration can play a role by expanding “the toolkit that’s available to providers.”

At the FDA’s July advisory committee meeting, the agency’s scientists advised against allowing broad access to these peptides, in part because they have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness in the kind of long-term, rigorously designed human studies typically required for drug approval.Even at the conference, some doctors acknowledged the evidence gaps. Abud Bakri, MD, works in California as a physician who specializes in caring for hospitalized patients. He also runs a separate medical practice focused on preventive and longevity medicine, and he serves on the Enhanced Games Independent Medical Commission. His interest in peptides has been long-standing enough to predate his medical career.

Bakri, now 33, has been a powerlifter since he was 17. He explained that he has a genetic condition that may increase his risk for ligament injuries. He first used BPC-157 almost a decade ago, and believes the peptide helped him recover faster, though he acknowledges that this is hard to prove definitively, given the absence of well-designed human studies.

If just a fraction of the rat and Petri dish research holds up in humans, BPC-157 could be an incredibly helpful drug in the ICU, he told me at the conference, during an interview at an outdoor dining area. But ideally, he says, its effectiveness should be proven before it’s used in this setting and potential side effects should be better understood. “If I’m going to have an honest conversation with a patient,” he said, “I need to be able to fully elucidate the risk, which I can’t for some of these compounds.”

After the conference, I spoke by Zoom with Thomas Kremen, MD, a physician at UCLA Health who works for several athletic teams, including the Los Angeles Chargers and Lakers. Kremen and his colleagues recently reviewed the evidence behind six popular experimental peptides. He and his co-authors seemed unimpressed. “It’s probably not a strong stance to be recommending these things,” he told me.

Would it be accurate for physicians to tell their patients that a peptide like BPC-157 heals tissue?

“Maybe if they're talking about a rat in some study,” he said. “But I think if you're making a claim, you better be able to cite the literature that supports that.”

Right now, not only is there a dearth of high-quality clinical evidence that peptides work and are safe, unsupported claims are being made about these compounds.

For instance, among the documents that AAPM submitted to the FDA’s public docket was a white paper claiming that two experimental peptides—epitalon and BPC-157—are endogenous, indicating they are naturally produced by the body. Similar descriptions can be found on websites for telehealth companies and online peptide vendors suggesting their products are synthetic versions of naturally derived peptides.

Epitalon is a lab-made peptide based on an extract that researchers in the Soviet Union obtained from cattle brains. Terry Roberts, PhD, a researcher who studies epitalon at Brunel University of London, said it’s unclear whether humans produce this peptide. As far as he’s aware, no studies have explicitly described its presence in humans.

And BPC-157? The peptide was discovered in gastric juice, said Vince Luca, PhD, a researcher at the Moffitt Cancer Center in Florida. Yet 35 years after its discovery, nobody has found a gene that encodes for it—it could have come from a microbe or from food. It’s not impossible that the body produces BPC-157, said Luca, but he thinks it’s unlikely. He also says that health care professionals “should know enough not to tell [people] that it's an endogenous peptide” when it is very unlikely to be one. And regardless, he continued, “there are plenty of endogenous peptides that, injected at high concentrations, would have a really negative effect on the body.” In other words, being endogenous alone doesn’t mean it’s safe.

(The American Academy of Peptide Medicine’s press team did not respond to queries asking how the group determined that BPC-157 and epitalon are endogenous.)

“My prediction is that those who are already using the gray market will continue to do so,” said Ryan Beattie, director of business development at Adaptog Research & Life Sciences, a UK-based company that sells experimental peptides. “So I think in the initial term, people will use compounding pharmacies in the US. What I then predict will happen is people will then get used to it, get more aware of it—and then switch to the gray market because of the cost.”

He may be right. For this story, we spoke with two peptide users who had been living with chronic pain, and after trying physical therapy as well as other treatment approaches, turned to the Wolverine stack, a combination of BPC-157 and TB-500. Both said they felt the peptides helped them—and neither was sure they’d switch to compounded versions, which are more expensive than the gray market and aren’t covered by insurance. One of them, Charles A., asked, “What are the hoops and what are the costs going to be?”

A third person, who asked to be referred to as James—the name he uses in online communities—tried an oral version of BPC-157 four years ago and said he hasn’t been the same since then. Shortly after taking the experimental compound, he felt anxiety followed by sensory and emotional numbness that still hasn’t gone away. He also developed an autoimmune condition. Online, he found others with some similar experiences. When he looked carefully at the published research, he concluded that it didn’t match the online hype. “I felt very foolish afterwards.”

Some scientists are continuing to investigate these peptides. At Moffitt, Luca is currently studying BPC-157 and he’s keeping an open mind.

Over the next few months, he and his colleagues plan to run a variety of very basic experiments in which they add the peptide to human cells and see how those cells respond. The results could provide additional insight into what, precisely, this molecule is doing in the body—and they may help explain why users have reported everything from striking benefits to lasting harm.

Perhaps one day, BPC-157 will prove to be an effective drug, said Luca. But for now, his advice is to wait on using it and other experimental peptides: “I just worry that people are a little too cavalier in how these are handled.”

Sara Talpos is a Michigan-based journalist covering biomedical research and public health. She’s written for publications including STAT, Science, and Undark, where she’s a contributing editor.

#5004. Bitcoin Shrugs Off Midterms as Experts Say Democratic Sweep Was 'Priced In'

Bitcoin Shrugs Off Midterms as Experts Say Democratic Sweep Was 'Priced In'

Democrats flipped both the House of Representatives and the Senate in the U.S. midterms, handing President Donald Trump a major political setback.

Experts say this was not the "preferred outcome" for crypto markets and that it has increased "uncertainty" for future pro-crypto policy. However, after a brief dip, Bitcoin is up 1.6% over the past 24 hours and now sits at $78,277, per Decrypt data. Broadly, almost every top 100 crypto token by market cap is in the green for the day.

"I think the Democratic sweep was largely priced in going into the midterms," André Dragosch, head of research at Bitwise Europe, told Decrypt. "The downside risks were fairly limited going into these midterms as sentiment was already very bearish and there were already signs of seller exhaustion again. So, it is no surprise that we have seen this kind of V-shaped recovery on an intraday basis during the midterms."

Polymarket put odds of the Democratic Party sweeping both the House and Senate during the midterms at 65.5% at the start of the week. Dragosch said these odds meant that markets weren't shocked by Tuesday's results.

James Butterfill, head of research at CoinShares, agreed. "My sense is that Bitcoin had largely priced in a Democratic sweep, leaving room for a relief rally as uncertainty clears," he said in comments shared with Decrypt. "Therefore, the anticipated result need not trigger another sell-off."

"Crypto policy did not decide the midterms. The economy, gas prices, the Iran situation, and Trump's unmet promises did," Heslin Kim, co-founder and CBO of Zenith, told Decrypt. "Crypto policy was irrelevant to the broader voting pool, but it now has the baggage of all the egregious activity that was stirred up in our ecosystem associated with the Trump family."

Tim Sun, senior analyst at HashKey, told Decrypt that the results do "increase the uncertainty surrounding the progress of crypto policy," but that Bitcoin's price action means that the market doesn't interpret it as a "major negative catalyst."

Specifically, this may be because the midterm results don't mean the Democrats can roll back existing pro-crypto guidelines set out by the SEC and CFTC, Dragosch said. However, it does mean that going forward, the market cannot rely as heavily on Trump's "political tailwinds for momentum," Sun added.

It also complicates the picture for the Clarity Act, which would establish rules for crypto markets. The bill has been in limbo since it was introduced in 2025, with many Democrats opposing it due to Trump's crypto conflicts of interest.

Dmitry Machikhin, CEO of BitOK, predicted in a note shared with Decrypt that the Clarity Act will remain in limbo well into 2027, thanks to the midterm results.

"The greater concern is slower crypto reform," Butterfill said. "CLARITY faces a harder path, while investigations into Trump-linked ventures could consume political capital and generate uncomfortable headlines across the sector. But the administration remains in place, and existing regulatory progress, including GENIUS, does not simply disappear."

He expects U.S. exchanges, token issuers and businesses that rely on new regulatory permissions to be more sensitive to the result than Bitcoin, and said established, compliant operators could even gain if scrutiny falls disproportionately on weaker competitors.

"A Democratic Congress may even be good for the industry in one way," Marcin Kaźmierczak, co-founder and COO of RedStone, told Decrypt. "Crypto shouldn't depend on one political camp. Infrastructure has to work for whoever is in power and bring most value to the ecosystem participants. Finance is changing, and politics will not stop it."

Despite the switch-up in the House and Senate, Dragosch expects little to change over the next two years.

"I personally expect more disputes, disagreements, and simply more of 'nothing stops this train,'" he said, pointing to a lack of significant progress to rein in fiscal deficits. "We will probably remain in a fiscally dominant environment with increasing fiscal risks, which I think is ultimately a tailwind for hard assets like Bitcoin."

Instead, he believes macro factors "remain in the driver's seat for now." Specifically, he points to a potential upcoming pivot from the Federal Reserve as a response to "increasing risk" around the AI trade.

"This will likely cause renewed interest in debasement-resistant assets like Bitcoin," Dragosch said, "and will continue to drive institutional adoption via ETPs and treasury companies as well."

Butterfill also pointed to macro factors. "For Bitcoin, real yields, dollar liquidity and ETP flows still carry more weight than the elections," he said. "I would temper expectations for the pace of U.S. reform, while remaining constructive on institutional adoption. How Trump responds to being constrained is the less predictable part."
