#4940. ‘Lawyer up’: Democrats target Trump, Big Tech after midterm sweep

Democrats wasted little time after their midterm victory, warning tech CEOs to prepare for subpoenas and signaling fresh investigations into Trump’s business dealings.

Democratic lawmakers said Big Tech executives should expect a wave of subpoenas, along with investigations into US President Donald Trump’s business circle when the new Congress takes office in January.

As Democrats secured control of Congress in a sweeping victory on Tuesday, Democratic lawmakers were already firing warning shots at the Trump family and tech executives close to the US president.

“They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we’ll be printing,” said US Senator Ed Markey, who sits on the Senate Commerce Committee, referring to Big Tech executives.

US Representative Robert Garcia, the expected incoming House Oversight Committee chairman, said he plans to take on “the Trump Crime Family, corporate corruption, DHS and ICE abuses, and the cover-up of the Epstein files,” adding a two-word warning: “Lawyer up.”

The warnings follow a mid-term election campaign in which Democrats challenged Big Tech’s growing political influence and the rapid expansion of AI, raising concerns about data centers’ demands on electricity, safeguards for AI products and tech executives’ ties to Trump.

Punchbowl News reported on Wednesday that technology executives close to Trump, including Elon Musk, Meta CEO Mark Zuckerberg and Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang, were expected to face scrutiny from House Oversight, Energy and Commerce and Senate Commerce.

The outlet also identified OpenAI CEO Sam Altman and Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei as potential witnesses facing questions about their companies’ AI products.

Trump’s crypto dealings also featured in mid-term Democratic campaign attacks, with candidates including Virginia Senator Mark Warner highlighting his memecoin earnings as part of a broader pattern of alleged corruption and self-enrichment.

“The amount of grift and corruption is unprecedented in American politics,” said Garcia during a press conference on Wednesday.

Related: Trump-linked tokens lead crypto losses after Democrats sweep in the US midterms

“The president himself has a crypto scheme where he’s making billions of dollars. This family is billions richer as the American public struggles.”

“What is being watched closely now is the lame-duck period,” said Andreja Cobeljic, head of derivatives trading at AMINA Bank in market commentary sent to Cointelegraph.

“Democrats have already told the Trump family and Republican leadership to lawyer up — a clear signal that corruption investigations are coming.”

House Judiciary Democrats have scrutinized Trump’s crypto businesses, publishing a November 2025 staff report alleging he used the presidency to enrich his family through crypto ventures tied to foreign interests while dismantling enforcement and anti-corruption safeguards.

House Judiciary Democrats have already started advertising positions for counsel and professional staff for the next Congress, including oversight and investigations roles.

#4939. A Reader’s Guide to the Many Sexy Roles of Alexander Skarsgård

Hollywood initially tried to make the 50-year-old star into a traditional hunk. He got weirder—and hotter—instead.

FAR TOO OFTEN, today’s Hollywood hunks deny their beauty to play a cavalcade of schmucks, freaks, and losers with funny accents, hiding in prosthetics because they think this will transform them into “serious” actors in the public eye. But this strategy doesn’t always pay off. Leo spent two hours and 42 minutes running around in a plaid bathrobe and messy man bun only to lose the Best Actor Oscar to Michael B. Jordan, who played a smokeshow vampire with a spit kink (and his identically sexy twin).

It’s best not to underestimate someone with undeniable sex appeal and unquenchable blood lust, as Alexander Skarsgård knows all too well from his years playing Eric Northman on True Blood. The role not only made Stockholm’s native son a household name in America, but an instantly recognizable sex symbol. Not that he needed much help from Hollywood. Skarsgård is 6'4" of muscle with anime bone structure. He’s 50, but doesn’t age in human years. He makes us mere mortal men all look, and feel, lesser than. The scion of Swedish acting royalty comes from a place where everyone kind of looks like that, yet he’s been named Sweden’s Sexiest Man Alive five times.

“After my first job, I was on a stupid ‘sexy hunky hot list’ and then people didn’t take me seriously,” Skarsgård told the The Sunday Times in 2022. “If you want characters with depth but have been labelled ‘a dude who takes his shirt off’, you’re not going to get those offers.” At the time, he was promoting The Northman, for which he’d packed on 25 additional pounds of muscle to play a Viking warrior prince, and spent most of his screen time shirtless, delivering the exact sort of performance that theoretically typecast him in the first place.

From scanning his filmography, it’s clear that at a certain point, Skarsgård decided to lean into his lot in life as an unapologetic sex symbol. Over the past two decades, he’s slowly gravitated toward roles that don’t attempt to soften or normalize his hunkiness, portraying a diverse range of characters who all just happen to be ridiculously hot in films and television shows that play with his beauty, what it means, and how it messes with the rest of us.

In his latest, Wicker, Skarsgård plays a literal object of desire, the purest distillation of his on-screen persona as a sweet and (still, somehow) sexy magical dildo who loves doing chores and adores his fishmonger wife (Olivia Colman), causing hysteria in a village full of dumbasses who have no idea how to treat or fuck a woman. In other words, he’s Alexander Skarsgård.

The Male Model: Zoolander & Zoolander 2 (2001 & 2016)

Ben Stiller’s comedy classic is built around the premise that Jerry Stiller’s son and Owen Wilson and Benedict Cumberbatch are male models. A fun piece of trivia is Skarsgård was inserted as one of Derek’s United Nations of conventionally attractive roommates. Baby faced Meekus is hilariously bad at bitchy teenage girl humor and loves orange mocha frappuccinos. He gets in a gasoline fight in the first installment that results in a fiery death, but returns for the sequel in a brief cameo, perfectly cast as the original man.

Skarsgård became a star when this show was a very good gothic soap opera people held in the same regard as many of the HBO classics that were on at the time, but it has faded from memory due to completely spinning off its axis in later seasons. But his role as Eric Northman, a sexy, menacing Viking prince who carries himself with regal arrogance when he first washes up in Louisiana, remains an indelible performance. He’s a kind of Magneto/Lestat figure, who believes in vampire superiority and humans primarily as food, then slowly reforms.

This remake of the 1971 Sam Peckinpah classic is a bad, hamfisted W. Bush era culture war movie (released three years late), in which James Marsden, as a pretentious, Harvard-educated lib Hollywood screenwriter, is pitted against Skarsgård, who plays a God-, guns-, country music and football-loving redneck. But the use of Skarsgård is symbolically correct: As a rugged man’s man who works with his hands, hulks over Marsden’s effete snob, and represents all Marsden’s shortcomings and insecurities.

The Charismatic Cult Leader: The East (2013) & The Stand (2020)

Two roles that trade on Skarsgård’s magnetism, casting him as cult leaders. One is Benji, an eco-terrorist ringleader in The East, which was overshadowed by Kelly Reichardt’s superior eco-terrorist anti-thriller Night Moves from the same year, but is also very good. Skarsgård begins the film as a hirsute, politically radical Jim Morrison. The role generally strips a lot of his raw tools away but he proves he still has the juice, running his operations with the certitude of a self-righteous zealot, taking full advantage of his implicit understanding that everyone will do what he says because they all want to fuck him.

That same energy is twisted evil in the less-than-stellar and largely forgotten Paramount+-produced adaptation of Stephen King’s The Stand from 2020. Skarsgård is having a blast here, clearly tapped in and comfortable with the cheap, fatty, made for TV vibes. He plays Randall Flagg, an infamous demonic anti-Christ figure in King lore, who also swaggers around making dark, grand pronouncements because he knows everyone is dreaming about him…and wants to fuck him.

The Mythological Figure: The Legend of Tarzan (2016) & The Northman (2022)

These are the two loin cloth roles that make best use of Skarsgård as physical clay. The first was a bid for a franchise at a more conventional phase of Skarsgard’s career. It’s a horribly problematic text about a Lawrence of Arabia raised by gorillas in the Congo, released at what was probably the latest moment in modern history anyone could’ve possibly thought this IP could still work in any context. And yet, Christoph Waltz is the bad guy, Margot Robbie is Jane, Samuel L. Jackson turns in a peak performance. It doesn’t really matter, because the movie doesn’t work. The one very funny, deeply horny beat, however, is Tarzan and Jane’s meet cute, when Robbie happens upon a still uncivilized, dirt and grime covered Skarsgård in the jungle, who emerges from the brush, towers over her, then sniffs her from head to toe, which is a shameless, incredibly erotic romantasy/soft porn scenario.

The Northman on the other hand, rules, and might just be the best movie in director Robert Eggers’s burgeoning and impressive filmography. I still wonder to this day how it wasn’t a bigger deal (likely the mid 2022 Covid-era release date, but still). Skarsgård leaves it all on screen in this folklore epic, near naked in the snow, in the mud, in the waves, shredded and extremely believable as a raider scaling ramparts with a battle axe, lurching through villages as they’re pillaged and burned.

The Alpha Scumbag: Big Little Lies (2017-2019)

“Biggest scumbag Alexander Skarsgård has ever played” is quite a distinction, but it has to be Perry Wright, a manipulative, abusive, domineering, womanizing monster who entwines magnetic, toxic sex with serial spousal abuse in David E. Kelly’s Shakespearean, rich, white wine-momsploitation thriller. He plays one of the few believable on-screen partners Nicole Kidman has ever had, arm candy who wears the fuck out of tailored suits. He’s a striking contrast to the show’s other husbands, all older boring, brunette, beta boys. He’s a perfect-from-a-distance husband and father, the on-paper exemplar of a certain anachronistic masculinity, and also the epitome of damaged male evil. Which is why Perry’s Orient Express style group murder is the climax of the first season of the show.

The Tech Demigod (The Odin of Codin): Succession (2018-2023)

A highly unlikely proposition. Harry Melling (who most probably still know best from his role as Dudley Dursley in the Harry Potter films) is Colin, a nebbishy mama’s boy with an aptitude for devotion in a barbershop quartet. Colin’s meetcute is delivering a back alley blowjob with little preamble to Skarsgård’s Ray, the hung final boss of leather daddies in the sweetest BDSM rom-com since Secretary. As Ray, Skarsgård communicates almost entirely via squinting, grunting, monosyllabic responses, and direct commands, yet is a completely alluring masculine ideal. His hobbies are waxing his motorcycle, watching shit on his phone while he eats, wrestling in assless unitards as foreplay, and reading Knausgaard. You can smell the sandalwood, cedar and tobacco wafting off the screen.

In which Skarsgård finally, inevitably plays a robot. This is a darkly comedic, but also occasionally saccharine, Apple TV+ series about a Wall-E war machine. His character, known only as Murderbot, has found a way to disable the guardrails that would otherwise force him to obey protocols programmed by the nefarious Weyland-Yutani style corporation who created him, and uses this freedom to consume our garbage TV shows. Murderbot expresses a professed disinterest in humanity, and hates eye contact; It’s a weird, flat, funny performance that ultimately lands halfway between Pedro Pascal in The Mandalorian and Peter Weller in Robocop. Skarsgård is a completely believable robot in a show that also serves as a metaphor for autism.

#4938. The Loneliness Trap

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Michelle Quirk

With unyielding inflation, economic uncertainty, and geopolitical friction sending collective anxiety levels higher and higher, many of us now consider genuine contentment an unaffordable luxury. Yet many decades of history serve as reminders that even amidst tremendous upheaval, human joy has never depended on what you can buy or external calm.

In this challenging environment, most of us want what we’ve always wanted: to be genuinely happy. Yet we are not willing to do what it takes to become happy.

A study from almost a century ago of the residents of the Northern English town of Bolton shows us the severe effects of not carving out time for our loved ones, a decision that most of us make every day. In the year my father was born, just over 2,000 kilometers (1,200 miles) to the east in Hungary, 1938—and then again in 2014—the residents of Bolton were asked what made them happy.

The most common ingredients of happiness among responses spanning 78 years were economic security and personal relationships, which were often tied together. Little mention was made of external events.

Joseph Roberts, a miner, despite answering the survey in 1938 when the threat of Hitler loomed large—the Nazi leader had already occupied Austria and parts of what was then Czechoslovakia—shared that what made him happy was returning from a day of hard work and spending time with his family.

“I see my kiddies and wife I am happy,” shared the miner. “When I am washed and romp with my kiddies, and when it is weekend and I tip up my humble wage and share with my wife in doling it out and we find we can manage another week we are happy. I am in regular work although it is hard and dangerous but I am happy because I have a contented mind.”

I am guessing you would agree that happiness is a seed you must grow in your life. Yet what most people do not understand is that you can’t grow your happiness alone; you can only do so with the help of others.

Since you cannot grow your happiness alone, it must also be true that happiness is a seed that grows in the gardens of others. How does this work?

We all seek happiness yet find it elusive. The reason is that most of us do not realize that one of the best ways to experience happiness is to help others experience it.

Plato understood this well. “Caring for the happiness of others,” observed the Greek philosopher over 2,500 years ago, “we find our own.”

Research also bears this out. Not only do people feel even more appreciated, happy, and connected to us than we expect them to when we give to them, but we also counterintuitively often benefit, as psychologists Sonja Lyubomirsky and Harry Reis note in their book How to Feel Loved, even more than they do.

I was reminded of this truth last month, when I spent nine days in a small town in rural Kenya where I had lived and worked as a Peace Corps volunteer 35 years earlier. Facilitating a pro bono leadership conference for rural primary and secondary school leaders and supporting five Kenyan women educators in teaching a women’s empowerment program for single mothers in one of Kenya’s most impoverished regions, I was struck by how happy I felt connecting with the people around me.

Returning to my comparatively more hermetic life of writing, researching, and teaching in Rome has been a jolt to my system; yet the memories remain and bring peace. In the end, I am like everyone else I’ve ever met in the world: traveling on an eternal search for connection and meaning.

Following the Italian way of expressing love, when you tell the other person “Ti voglio bene” (which means “I wish for your happiness”), you offer them the seed of happiness to plant in their own garden. You give them your support to grow in the way they desire, toward their own goals and values, or “to become,” as the former president of the American Psychological Association and the father of client-centered therapy, Carl Rogers, once wrote.

If the seed flourishes and becomes the flower of happiness, the other person may or may not bring you this flower one day. Sound risky? It may be; yet it’s the only way you will ever have the experience of seeing this flower in full bloom.

In other words, interconnectedness is the only path that will enable you to experience and sustain happiness throughout your life.

“Sounds good, Mr. Idealistic Author,” you may be thinking. “But how do I make daily decisions that will bring this interconnectedness into my life?”

If you want a more interconnected life, you will first have to ask yourself some tough questions about your daily decisions, right down to whether you should spend your time today ordering another gadget on Amazon or going hiking with a friend. Let’s consider this choice.

What do you think will bring you more happiness—an Amazon delivery or going on a hike with a friend? A study by Amit Kumar of the University of Texas has found that once you have a base level of financial resources, experiences rather than objects are the drivers of happiness.

Why? Experiences (“doing” something such as going to a meditation retreat or on a bicycle ride or to a restaurant or a concert) are more often shared, while objects (“owning” something such as a new shirt or book) are most often enjoyed alone.

In the end, the love we’ve shared with others is all we take with us. “All that we love deeply,” Helen Keller sagely observed, “becomes a part of us.” This is why experiences, which facilitate the sharing of love, produce much more long-term happiness than objects.

How, then, can you usher more happiness back into your life? Prioritize doing activities you enjoy in person with people you care about rather than buying objects to enjoy by your lonesome.

Just as you pursue objects to purchase and yet Prime shipping does not drop happiness at your doorstep, you also pursue social information about the people in your social network. As I write in my book Screened In, this search—like buying objects—does not bring the social connection that is the purveyor of genuine happiness into your life.

In sum, then, stop texting and call, go out with, see, visit, meet—in short, spend time with other, values-congruent people as often as you can.

In other words: Just. Connect. In person. In real time.

Spend time with the people you love, or could love if you were to get to know them better. Then happiness will cease to be a distant stranger and will become a part of your life again.

#4937. An Easy 3 Miles with On’s Cloudsurfer 3

After years of stubborn loyalty to another brand, could one city jog make a longtime runner reconsider his shoes?

After just a mile in On's Cloudsurfer 3, it dawned on me—I’ve been far too hard-headed when it comes to my choice of running shoes.

For years, I held steadfastly to a particular brand and style, year after year, run after run. When one pair wore out, leaving my shins aching and feet tender, I’d quickly order a replacement online without even a thought of pivoting. Different colors and patterns kept it interesting, but that was the extent of my athletic audacity.

Truthfully, it wasn’t about brand loyalty. I wouldn’t dare try another shoe by the same company. It was more a case of preferring what I had grown used to in the past decade over the potential thrill of experimentation.

When I was asked to take On’s Cloudsurfer 3 for a jaunt, I was skeptical. Not that the shoes couldn’t or wouldn’t be great—it’s On, after all, a company renowned for exceptional technology and an ultralightweight feel to their sneaks—but that they’d be able to win over someone like me.

Still, I was game. The assignment called for my honest opinion during a three-mile run, and I was ready to break out of my rut (or at least try to). So I laced up and headed out on a beautiful early-fall Sunday afternoon in New York City. Here’s how it went.

I was intrigued even before I took my first step. Putting on Cloudsurfer 3, it immediately felt lighter than my go-to shoes. That surprised me, considering the minimal weight of my faves is what originally attracted me to them. The knit-mesh on the outside of the Cloudsurfer 3 was light and airy, and the inside was plush in all the right places (including the generous ankle collar) without being overbearing.

Yet, I knew the bigger test was to come. As a long-time sand volleyball player, I’ve developed a harder-than-necessary plant of my foot when I run, so I tend to need soft cushioning from a shoe.

Heading outside, I took a breath and set off down the sidewalk. The first strides were cautious, as I focused on the new sensation. The initial feedback was encouraging. It all felt effortless and soft, which I later would learn was due to On’s proprietary Surreal™ Superfoam with CloudTec®. I knew of On’s “run on clouds” tagline, but now I was feeling it in action, and I liked the smooth, soft ride I was experiencing.

I was a half-mile in when I hit my first red light—an unavoidable hazard of city running—giving me a moment to stop and reflect. “That was easier than usual,” I thought. I appreciated the secure feel of my ankle without it being too constricting, and my foot didn’t shift at all in the propulsive phase of my gait. The toe box was nice and comfy.

Pleasantly surprised so far, I changed my route on the fly and headed into Central Park. I wanted to test out the Cloudsurfer 3 on the oval around the Great Lawn . . . and enjoy an uninterrupted 0.55-mile loop.

I was breathing a little heavier at this point, but I noticed something about the shoe: It gave me the sensation of running downhill, even on a flat surface. I also felt what I’d call a “rebound” effect as each foot touched down and lifted off. This energy return made me feel faster than usual, with a burst of extra power on my “swing” phase and increased confidence in my mechanics. Could the Cloudsurfer 3’s mere 10.8 ounces per shoe be the difference maker?

I don’t know if it was the refreshing chill in the air, the clearer path than I’m used to in the city, or an extra “oomph” that the shoes’ softness provided, but I did a doubletake when seeing my 10:02 split time at the end of the second mile—well ahead of my typically slow 11-minute pace.

After two treks around the Great Lawn, I was back on the paved trail that leads out of the park. The sweat was beading despite a comfortable temperature in the high 60s, but I was enjoying the workout.

By the last mile, my legs were feeling good thanks to the easy two miles prior. I was in “go” mode and barely noticed my shoes. I decided to switch up my routine and finish with a series of sprints on the city blocks—fast bursts between intersections, with short rests at red lights.

Everything was still flowing smoothly: an effortless “lift” on each step, a seamless heel-to-toe transition, and no reverberations from the hard sidewalk and street underfoot. The last third of the run seemed to fly by, and I was more than happy with yet another sub-11-minute mile. I also realized that a fresh scrape on my ankle from a volleyball match earlier in the week, which I had been worried about aggravating during the run, was completely pain free; there was no chafing whatsoever.

When I took this assignment, I knew I’d likely be impressed with On’s Cloudsurfer 3. This is no ordinary sneaker, after all, but one designed with Swiss precision and an array of features celebrated by regular Joes and top-tier marathoners alike.

What I wasn’t quite ready for was the thought that maybe, just maybe, it was time to move on from my old standbys. I do know one thing as I reflect on the experience: My daily runs will be in On’s Cloudsurfer 3 because its soft, smooth ride made this run feel effortless.

#4936. Binance, Polymarket seek Polygon hard fork to recover $6.8M in stolen crypto

Polygon block producers have already frozen the stolen tokens, but a proposed hard fork would allow the funds to be recovered and returned to their owners.

Binance and Polymarket have proposed a Polygon network hard fork to recover approximately 70.49 million POL tokens, worth about $6.8 million, stolen in separate security incidents involving the two platforms.

According to a Wednesday governance proposal, the recovery would cover 23.86 million POL, worth $2.31 million across four addresses associated with the Polymarket hack and 46.63 million POL, worth about $4.51 million, held in one address linked to the Binance hack.

The proposed hard fork would remove the stolen tokens from the five attacker-controlled addresses and transfer them to verified recovery wallets designated by Polymarket and Binance. The process would take place at a predetermined block without changing POL’s total supply.

Polygon block producers have already blocked transactions from the affected addresses, preventing the stolen funds from moving. However, the proposal said the restrictions are temporary measures that cannot return the funds to their owners.

The plan requires governance approval and testing on Polygon’s Amoy testnet before activation. It is scheduled for discussion on Nov. 5, with no confirmed date for the mainnet hard fork.

The proposal follows Binance’s record $6.3 billion hack on Oct. 29 and a separate incident in which Polymarket temporarily suspended trading after reporting that a third-party provider had been compromised.

#4935. Why It's Difficult to Be a Good Leader

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

When I conduct a workshop or extended program on leadership development, I often start by asking participants to identify the various characteristics of “good leaders.” Regardless of the audience, their lists are typically similar. In other words, the characteristics that define good leaders are not a secret, and there is general agreement as to what those characteristics are. I then follow with a question: “If this isn’t a secret, why don’t we see more actual leaders who exhibit these characteristics?”

Of course there are several viable answers to that question, but one important answer that gets overlooked is polarities that are inherent in leadership within an organization. What’s a polarity? Imagine a continuum, anchored at each end by a competing goal. As you invest more in reaching one of the goals, it’s like a pendulum swinging toward that end of the continuum, and you risk failing to reach the other goal. That is a polarity, and the trick is to continually and intentionally manage where you are on the continuum, recognizing that focusing more at one end tends to starve achievement of the goal at the other end.

As an example, a common polarity that many employed individuals face is the work-family polarity. The more time and energy devoted to work tends to result in negligence of one’s family, and vice versa. In a sense, “work-life balance” implies maintaining a midpoint position on the continuum. In reality, work or family often receives more than 50 percent of one’s time and energy due to what is needed in each domain at the time. Perhaps the ultimate goal of “life success” rests on not neglecting work or family for too long.

What about leaders in organizational settings? They commonly face a number of polarities, including these:

I imagine there are additional polarities, and perhaps some that are specific to each setting, but managing even this set listed here represents a monumental achievement. Leaders frequently notice a deficit in some area, perhaps followers regularly working in ways that do not align with the organization’s priorities. We might say that the leader’s pendulum had swung too far toward allowing autonomy. Because it’s now a problem, the unfortunate tendency is to swing too far in the other direction. In this case, the leader may take a heavy hands-on approach, which ends up frustrating followers as they complain of being micromanaged.

Just as leaders commonly focus on instances where a pendulum swung too far in one direction within the polarity, followers do as well. In the example of too much autonomy, at least some followers likely felt as though they did not receive enough direction or supervision. Just like Goldilocks and the Three Bears, the trick is finding and maintaining the area within the polarity that feels “just right.” Unfortunately, what constitutes “just right” varies across individuals.

So what can leaders do? Identify the prominent polarities they must navigate, and work on resisting the temptation to swing too far in one direction when trying to rectify a situation in which the other end of the continuum had been overly emphasized. I also suggest being explicit in explaining to followers the concept of polarities and the inherent tension the leader faces in each one. The hope is that followers begin to notice when a polarity is becoming out of balance and can alert leadership.

Perhaps you’re not a leader, and instead feel as though you are at the mercy of polarities over which you have little (if any) control. In that case, you now know about polarities and have that construct with which to better understand at least some of the problems you encounter at work. That knowledge doesn’t remove the negative effects of being too far toward one end of a continuum, but hopefully it provides the ability to recognize that leaders face difficult polarities. Because leaders frequently have not heard of the concept and the dynamic that polarities explain, it may be helpful for you to share this post with the leaders you think may benefit from it.

#4934. Crédit Mutuel Arkéa resumes operations after attack, assesses losses

The bank said customer funds were unaffected after attackers accessed its systems through a service provider’s platform.

French banking group Crédit Mutuel Arkéa said nearly all operations resumed Wednesday after a cyberattack linked to a suspected theft from the bank’s own account.

The bank said operations resumed following security checks and additional safeguards. It said customer funds were unaffected and the disruption only delayed certain transfers.

The bank also disclosed that attackers gained access through a service provider, not its online banking subsidiary Fortuneo. They transferred the bank’s money to other financial institutions, potentially allowing them to convert it into crypto.

Withdrawals from crypto exchanges first exposed the suspected theft. Blockchain analytics firm Bitquery traced withdrawals from accounts at Kraken, Crypto.com and OKX before the victim was publicly identified.

Onchain sleuth ZachXBT flagged more than $106 million in suspicious withdrawals on Monday and raised his estimate above $133 million on Tuesday after finding another $27 million. His Wednesday report identified Arkéa as the potential victim.

The bank then confirmed the attack and said it was still assessing its financial impact.

“We are not confirming these figures; we can only confirm the approximate scale,” Arkéa said, according to a machine translation.

Arkéa said some funds had been frozen and further recovery efforts were underway. Kraken and Crypto.com previously told Cointelegraph they had restricted accounts or blocked funds linked to the suspected theft. Both exchanges maintained that their platforms remained secure.

#4933. Can Finding Perfection Backfire?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Things were starting to feel rather boring at home—the same old places, the same old food, the same old sunsets. I’ve learned to be very wary of boredom, because it’s a close cousin to one of my least favorite symptoms of depression: anhedonia. Anhedonia is the inability to experience pleasure; things that used to give you pleasure (that amazing Malibu sunset, for example) no longer move you the same way.

It was clearly time to get away from my usual haunts. I needed a startle, an awakening, something that would reset my pleasure circuits so life didn’t feel so humdrum. Simply put, I needed a good healthy dose of awe.

Travel is one of the best ways I know to stoke the wonder inside me. Fortunately, my partner, Colin, was up for it. We thought and thought about where to go, and then, one day, I just knew. I wanted rejuvenation? I wanted rebirth? Then why not go to the very heart of it?

That meant, of course, heading to Florence. Historians credit Florence as the birthplace of Europe’s revivification after the Middle Ages, and it sounded simply ideal: museums overflowing with masterpieces, architecture so beautiful it could take one’s breath away. Literally. The so-called “Stendhal syndrome” originated there, when in 1817 French author Stendhal took one look at the Basilica of Santa Croce and fainted, overwhelmed by its magnificence.

I certainly wasn’t hoping to faint dead away, but to be so transported by beauty sounded heavenly. It sounded exactly like the cure I was seeking.

My anticipation grew with each passing day, until the trip was practically all I could think about. Colin and I were especially looking forward to seeing Michelangelo’s famous statue of David. At very long last, we found ourselves standing outside the Galleria dell’Accademia. There was a long line to get in, but what did it matter? David awaited.

When the guards started admitting people, there was some jostling, but eventually the rooms grew wider and the crowd, although still very much a crowd, grew thinner. I let go of Colin’s hand to search through my purse for my camera. When I found it and looked around for him, I was so astonished I nearly dropped my phone. There, illuminating Colin’s face, was the very sensation I had traveled so far for and hoped so much to find: awe.

I followed his gaze, and then I saw it, too: Michelangelo’s David. He dominated the room and dwarfed the tourists scrambling to capture his image. They encircled him, but he remained untouched, his smooth white marble immune to their adoration. I marveled at his potent form, every sinew of his body taut and ready for battle, his gaze resolute and unwavering. He stood forever on the edge of glory.

No, dear reader, I didn’t faint—although a pregnant woman next to me grew dizzy and had to be helped to a seat by a guard. The crowd, which had been so lively outside, was strangely quiet, as if in church. And it did feel like a sacred space. I knew, without being able to articulate it at the time, that I was in the presence of something holy, a talent so astounding that only a higher power could bestow it.

And contrary to everything I had expected, it didn’t instantly cure my discontent. I felt very strange—disconnected from my surroundings, and deeply disturbed. What was going on? I didn’t know how to explain my feelings to Colin, because I didn’t really understand them myself. All I knew was that compared to David, everything else seemed plebeian.

We went back to our hotel. There were frescoes on the walls and ceiling of our room that I’d delighted in just a few hours before. But now, I was only aware of their flaws. And worse yet, I was aware of mine. For all my artistic ambitions, I’d never create anything even remotely approaching a David.

I couldn’t help but wonder what other artists had felt in the face of incomparable beauty—and I wasn’t surprised by what I found out. Camus summed it up: “Beauty is unbearable, drives us to despair, offering us for a minute the glimpse of an eternity that we should like to stretch out over the whole of time.”

Exactly. So now what was I going to do, knowing that the rest of life would never reach the level of perfection I’d just witnessed?

“Let’s go to lunch,” Colin said, always the pragmatist. “You’ll feel better after you eat.”

A half-hour later, I sat in front of a large, steaming bowl of fusilli al pesto and toyed with my fork. I was sick of being so damned sensitive. Why couldn’t I just be moved by perfection, and let it go?

Then I took a bite of my fusilli. Oh my God, it was the most delicious thing I’d ever tasted. The pasta was just firm enough, the pesto fragrant and slightly sweet, with a teasing, subtle undertone of garlic. I looked up at Colin and smiled. He smiled back—relieved, no doubt, that I wasn’t moping anymore.

So maybe I couldn’t be perfect. Maybe I was meant to be like Salieri in Amadeus, fated to simply appreciate perfection. That didn’t mean I had to be miserable. That afternoon in Florence, sitting across from the love of my life, a perfect moment came and went, and I was there to know it.

#4931. Bitcoin, Ether ETFs draw $128M as crypto rebounds after midterms

Fidelity led Bitcoin ETF inflows, while BlackRock’s IBIT shed another $201 million, extending its outflow streak to four trading sessions.

US-listed spot Bitcoin and Ether exchange-traded funds (ETFs) snapped their outflow streaks on Wednesday as cryptocurrency markets rebounded following the US midterm elections.

Bitcoin (BTC) ETFs attracted $74.6 million on Wednesday, while Ether (ETH) funds added $53 million, according to SoSoValue data.

The turnaround ended three consecutive trading sessions of Bitcoin ETF outflows totaling $1.16 billion, while Ether ETFs snapped a seven-day outflow streak of $657 million.

Bitcoin rebounded above $80,000 and US stocks rallied following Democrats’ midterm election victories on Wednesday.

The latest batch of inflows followed a slowdown in withdrawals, from $399 million on Monday to $116 million on Tuesday.

Blackrock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust (IBIT) recorded another $201.2 million in outflows on Wednesday, extending its four-session outflow streak to $935.2 million, according to Farside Investors data. Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund (FBTC), meanwhile, led inflows with roughly $237 million.

Daily spot Bitcoin ETF flows by issuer since Oct. 30. Source: Farside Investors

Wednesday’s inflows narrowed Bitcoin’s 2026 net outflows to around $79 million.

James Butterfill, head of research at digital asset manager CoinShares, said Bitcoin had largely priced in the possibility of a Democratic sweep before the election, leaving room for a relief rally as uncertainty eased. He cautioned against attributing too much of Bitcoin’s performance to the election outcome.

CK Zheng, co-founder of crypto hedge fund ZX Squared Capital and former global head of risk at Credit Suisse, pointed to monetary liquidity, institutional investment flows and broader investor appetite for risk as more immediate drivers of Bitcoin’s price.

Related: Trump-linked tokens lead crypto losses after Democrats sweep in the US midterms

Zheng said spot Bitcoin ETFs and corporate treasury holdings could broaden demand while also exposing BTC to institutional selling pressure.

#4930. Seeking Spiritual Friendship on the Path to Enlightenment

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

People over the centuries have referred to spiritual companionship and assistance as a relationship with a “spiritual friend.” The Gaelic word anamchara refers to the spiritual friend as a “soul-friend.” A soul-friend is an assisting force on our journey. This is the reliable confidant with whom we can share our innermost spiritual conundrums, visions, and realizations.

As St. Aelred (1111-1167), the Cistercian abbot of Rievaulx Abbey in medieval England, wrote in On Spiritual Friendship: “It is no small consolation in this life to have someone who can unite with you in an intimate affection and the embrace of a holy love. Someone in whom your spirit can rest, to whom you can pour out your soul, to whose pleasant exchanges, as to soothing songs, you can fly in sorrow. To the dear breast of whose friendship, amidst the many troubles of the world, you can safely retire. A person who can shed tears with you in your worries, be happy with you when things go well, search out with you the answers to your problems, whom with the ties of charity you can lead into the depths of your heart. A person who, though absent in body, is yet present in spirit, where heart to heart you can talk to him, where the sweetness of the Spirit flows between you, where you so join yourself and cleave to him that soul mingles with soul and two become one.”

In Buddhism we find the idea of Kalyana-mittata, “virtuous friendship.” The spiritual friends support one another’s practice with candidness, wisdom, and trustworthiness. In the Upaddha Sutta, Buddha explained to Ananda, his disciple, cousin, and friend, that such spiritual companionship can be considered the whole of the Dharma as the “holy life.” Buddha went on to say that when we have Kalyana-mittata in place with friends, they cheer and encourage us on the path, and when our friend is Buddha, we are fully freed from suffering. In the Dighajanu Sutta, Buddha says that when a practitioner is committed to dialogue with a variety of upstanding people, he learns to imitate their commitment to the Dharma, their compassion, their discerning wisdom. They have become allies of enlightenment to one another.

Another text, the Anguttara Nikaya, lists the qualities of a monastic spiritual friend: he is amiable, venerable, able to give good counsel, patient in listening, respectful, capable of speaking in depth about the Dharma, and serious enough to share on a depth level. A teacher is a prime example of what is required in spiritual friendship. He, she, or they offer guidance, challenges, comfort, and cheer on the path to virtue and awakening—all the scaffolding of authentic support. A support group, a faith community, or a sangha, a coterie of practitioners of Buddhism, are examples.

Peer companionship is certainly valuable on the spiritual path. Our spiritual practice and our psychological health are enlivened when we have a trusted friend to whom we can speak openly about ourselves. This will not be the “friend” who supports and cheers our ego and its inflation—a narcissistic style of alliance. The ally of our higher Self supports our journey toward generosity, humility, enlightenment, and loving-kindness. This ally warns and saves us from what in Buddhism are called the three poisons: greed, hate, and the illusion of separateness. These are descriptors of the inflated ego at its worst. Spiritual friends will be the ones who show and lead us from greed to generosity, from hate to love, and from ignorance to wisdom.

“Ubuntu” is an African term meaning “humanity toward others.” It is also translated as “I am because you are” or “I am what I am because of who we all are.” Ubuntu tells us that all beings can be allies, contributing to our becoming what we can best be in this moment. Ubuntu is also a spiritual calling to reach out to and care about all beings as relatives in the family of humanity. Ubuntu transcends race and creed. All diversity becomes, or rather is, only one reality, just what the Buddhas and mystics in all traditions came to realize. We feel an intense, ineradicable sense of belonging and we pass on to others that same joyous sense. We remind ourselves that we are not stand-alone beings; we exist only with everyone else, the solidarity that Thich Nhat Hanh calls “inter-being.”

We notice unanimous agreement among the assisting-counseling forces in all times and traditions: We can be allies to one another only if there are no boundaries to our assisting outreach. Indeed, such boundaries are really barriers. The wise, courageous assisting forces in all traditions have made the journey from insulation to universal embrace. They see diversity as a path to unity. Their caring, committed connection has no frontier.

—Christopher Smart, “On a Bed of Guernsey Lilies”

Adapted from By Your Side: How to Find Soulful Allies and Become One to Others (Shambhala, 2024)

#4928. France budget rejection sidelines stablecoin tax, wallet reporting

Three adopted amendments, including stablecoin taxation and self-custody reporting, fall away but could be reintroduced in the Senate.

The French National Assembly rejected the 2027 state budget’s revenue section late Wednesday, removing crypto-related amendments from the version of the bill sent to the Senate.

The Assembly rejected the revenue section by a vote of 458–0 at first reading, with two abstentions, according to the official voting record. The revenue section covers government income, such as taxes.

After the rejection, the government will send the Senate the original budget text, without the crypto-related amendments adopted by lawmakers.

The proposals could still be reintroduced in the Senate and would need to survive the remaining budget process to become law.

Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph before the vote that rejection of the revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments.”

The Assembly adopted three crypto-related amendments before rejecting the revenue section.

On Oct. 16, lawmakers adopted an amendment that would make gains from crypto-to-stablecoin conversions taxable.

On Tuesday, lawmakers adopted another amendment that would require taxpayers to disclose self-custodied crypto holdings totaling at least 100,000 euros ($112,000), with the threshold assessed on Dec. 31 of the tax year.

Another amendment would increase penalties from 10,000 euros to 50,000 euros for crypto platforms failing to provide information requested by tax authorities.

The Senate is scheduled to begin debating the finance bill on Nov. 20, followed by an examination of the revenue section’s articles on Nov. 23, according to its schedule.

#4926. Surviving Holiday Family Gatherings

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Updated November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

Adult children of emotionally abusive families may dread spending time with their family, especially during the holidays. If this applies to you, instead of feeling constantly victimized by family members during holiday gatherings, try the following suggestions that may help you feel more in control of what occurs.

Hopefully, the above suggestions will help you take care of yourself when you are in your parent’s, sibling’s, or other abusive family member’s presence, but if you find that you aren’t able to follow these recommendations, please don’t chastise yourself or think of yourself as weak. Instead, treat yourself with self-compassion and remember that you sustained a great deal of damage growing up in your emotionally abusive household, and this damage may still be preventing you from acting on your own behalf. Give yourself credit for the changes you have been able to make and trust that by continuing in your efforts to heal, you will eventually feel empowered enough to stand up for yourself and take care of yourself in the ways you deserve.

And remember: You may feel obligated to spend the holidays with your family, but you have an even more important obligation to protect yourself and your children from dangerous and abusive family members.

#4924. South Korea seeks 4th crypto tax delay, targets 2029 start

South Korea is seeking a fourth delay to its 22% crypto tax, months after reaffirming plans to begin taxing digital asset gains in January 2027.

South Korea’s government has backed postponing tax on cryptocurrency gains by another two years to 2029, in what would be the fourth delay to its implementation.

Finance Minister Lee Hyoung-il announced the proposal Thursday, saying the government would consult lawmakers on postponing the January 2027 implementation date.

The tax would impose a 22% levy, comprising a 20% income tax and 2% local tax, on annual crypto gains exceeding 2.5 million won ($1,860).

The government cited 2029 as the start of information exchange with the US under the Crypto-Asset Reporting Framework (CARF). It said imposing the tax before those exchanges begin could put the domestic market at a disadvantage relative to overseas markets. It also wants to account for changes under planned digital asset legislation.

Officials also said they plan to introduce a three-year loss carryforward provision, allowing investors to offset crypto losses against profits earned over the following three years. The government plans to submit amendments to improve the tax system during parliament’s regular session in 2027, with detailed tax guidelines planned for the first half of 2028.

The tax was originally scheduled to take effect in January 2022 but was subsequently postponed to 2023, 2025 and 2027.

The proposal reverses the government’s previous position. A Finance Ministry official said in May that implementation would proceed in 2027.

South Korean finance officials recently engaged with major crypto exchanges amid growing calls to postpone the tax. Industry representatives cited unresolved tax rules for different transaction types and insufficient time to prepare the necessary infrastructure.

#4923. 7 Easy Upgrades for a More Efficient Home Office

Smart storage solutions and clever products maximize your workspace.

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

You don’t need much to outfit a home office; a solid desk and an ergonomic chair and you’ve got yourself a workstation. But it’s the little things that can take your workspace from functional to fully dialed in: monitors in optimal positions, easy access to frequently used gear, a desktop clear of cables and clutter. IKEA offers a wide range of storage and organization solutions to maximize your space and eliminate the friction between you and getting things done. Here are seven of our top picks that will instantly streamline your setup.

Turn dead wall space into storage with the SKÅDIS pegboard, a particularly useful hack if your desk lacks drawers. Combine an accessories set to create the system that suits your needs: Clips corral important receipts, hooks give headphones a home, and bins keep pens in order.

If you do one thing to improve your WFH setup, make it this: Elevate your monitor so it sits at eye level. This reduces strain on your neck and helps keep you from hunching. The ELLOVEN stand also offers storage, both in the bamboo drawer with built-in organizers and the compartment below, which easily fits a keyboard or laptop.

Rid yourself of an unsightly cable nest—and the annoyance of crawling under your desk to reach your plugs—with this easy-to-install deskside tray that fits a standard size power strip. The no-drill clamps allow you to move it as needed.

Make your desk light do more for you: This slim task lamp features wireless charging and a USB port, a welcome duo for your roster of devices.

It’s a digital world, and yet, piles of paper still tend to accumulate; an old-school letter tray is the organizational answer. This one comes with four cork-lined drawers to keep paperwork prioritized, and the powder-coated steel box construction means you’re not losing desktop surface area.

Yes, storing your over-ear headphones on a stand keeps them close at hand, but more importantly, it protects the ear pads and maintains the headband shape. This one pulls double duty as a tablet stand, giving you twice the functionality for the low price of only $5.

Between speakers, a printer, a paper shredder, maybe a fan or air purifier, it’s inevitable that you will need more outlets than your home office walls provide. An extension cord is essential, and the four-pronged shape of this one gives each plug breathing room, so large adapters and power bricks won’t block one another.

Ready to upgrade? Head to IKEA for more smart storage and home office organization solutions.

#4922. The Science and Lived Experience of Fatherhood

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Updated November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

One possesses nothing unless one has experienced it in reality. A purely intellectual insight is not enough, because one knows only the words and not the substance of the thing from inside. —Carl Jung

Three and a half years ago, when I was 58, my daughter Elli was born on Father’s Day, 2023. There is, of course, only a 1 in 365 chance of a child being born on Father’s Day, so the timing of Elli’s arrival was something special.

Up to that point in life I’d never been superstitious. We all experience rare events, like the crash of United Flight 173 in my Portland neighborhood when I was a boy. Such happenings, however unusual, have to be witnessed by someone, so why not me?

Still, I couldn’t shake the notion that Elli’s Father’s Day birth carried meaning beyond coincidence. As the days passed, I came to see it as a call for transformative action.

I’d faced a disturbing dilemma for years. Though I was at the height of a successful academic career, addiction, marital strife, and decades of misguided ambition were taking more from me than a career could deliver. Without reprioritizing, I knew Elli, sooner or later, would be raised without her father. I couldn’t imagine her experiences of future birthdays or Father’s Days.

We’ve all heard the statistics. One in four children are raised without their father—a rate that’s doubled in my lifetime. Both boys and girls are affected, sometimes in similar and sometimes in disparate ways. Boys show more aggression and delinquent behavior, and report wanting more guidance as youth.

Girls engage in more self-harm and suicidal behavior, and experience teen pregnancy at almost eight times the national average. Both boys and girls are prone to early drinking and drug use, and many suffer from depression. Three-quarters of adolescents who don’t finish high school are raised in fatherless homes.

As a child clinical psychologist, I’d known these facts and more for decades. I’d trained with Everett Waters, a prominent attachment theorist. I’d learned how healthy emotional development depends on children’s secure attachments with their mothers and fathers alike. I understood the effects of paternal abandonment on girls’ capacities to form trusting, respectful relationships with their partners later in life.

My knowledge of social science convinced me to abandon my career to be a healthy and committed father. Although I knew intellectually I’d made the right decision, my training couldn’t prepare me for the wellspring of emotion fatherhood can deliver. Neither science nor English language capture deeply embodied human connections.

A few nights ago I put Elli to bed through our normal routine of toothbrushing, reading, and song-singing. After finishing our last verse, Elli gazed, head on her pillow and without words, into my eyes, and I gazed back into hers. This lasted for probably 8 to 10 seconds—nothing peculiar yet longer than typical in U.S. culture.

The experience was inexplicably profound—something I’d never encountered in all my decades of living. I felt small yet right-sized, as if standing on the edge of an expansive still lake on a cool, moonless night, peering into a star-filled sky. I felt connected, not only with Elli but with the world around us and the universe beyond. I felt no ambition, no urge to change, no disappointments, and no uncertainties. There was nothing to want or regret.

I pushed back tears, which Elli couldn’t possibly understand, overwhelmed with deep affection and gratitude. In mere seconds, a little girl, 3 years old, taught me something more profound than anything I’d learned in two and a half decades as a psychological scientist.

Eye contact may be the most powerful route to embodied human connection. When we’re open, something happens in an instant, usually outside our awareness. We gauge, even in strangers, their emotional state, and our nervous systems, whether subtly or strongly, respond. English language provides no words for this kind of human connection, but other languages do.

Mitákuye Oyás’iŋ, a Lakota phrase, captures embodied human connection and our interconnections with all around us. According to Lakota wisdom, Mitákuye Oyás’iŋ—sometimes described as oneness—should be sensed before we engage further with others, even others we might disagree with. When cultivated, Mitákuye Oyás’iŋ is accessible to all of us once we know where to find it. I’d read about Mitákuye Oyás’iŋ and thought I understood it, yet I didn’t until Elli was born.

Science, despite its many benefits, can’t fully capture embodied human connection. Its objective measurement and declarations of winners and losers subordinate subjective experience, even when social scientists claim to value it.

Social science borrowed the scientific method from the hard sciences, where declaring single winners and rejecting subjective experience hold value. Einstein was right: Newton’s theory of gravity was incomplete, and no amount of lived experience is needed to understand that. In fact, it can only get in the way.

Psychology is different. Human needs, motivations, and understandings of the world around us are deeply subjective and varied. As a result, few if any psychological theories are always correct at the expense of all others. There are few zero-sum games in social science.

Instead, the ultimate utility of psychological theories depends on context, culture, and the subjective reactions, motivations, and apprehensions of the very people we seek to understand. These and all parts of the human psyche aren’t amenable to direct observation or precise measurement.

I was taught as a student that anything unmeasurable, even in social science, is inconsequential. If we can’t measure something, no matter how central it is to human experience, it falls outside the purview of science and isn’t necessary to understand. I recall, as if it were yesterday, my psychological methods professor’s disdain for anything subjective.

Social science has, by and large, failed to ameliorate humanity’s greatest dilemmas. Our most pressing personal ailments and social ills, including mental illness, addiction, violence, racism, and war, are just as pernicious today as they were when we embraced the scientific method and marginalized subjective experience a century ago. Maybe it’s time to rethink that decision. Maybe it’s time for transformative change.

Beauchaine, T. (2026). Elvis and the Northern Lights: A psychology professor’s struggles with mental illness and addiction. Amazon.

U.S. Census Bureau. (2025). Historical living arrangements of children. Retrieved on 10/31/2026 from https://www.census.gov/data/tables/time-series/demo/families/children.h…

Popenoe, D. (1996). Life without father: Compelling new evidence that fatherhood and marriage are indispensable for the good of children and society. Simon and Schuster.

Ellis, B. J., Bates, J. E., Dodge, K. A., et al. (2003). Does father absence place daughters at special risk for early sexual activity and teenage pregnancy? Child Development, 74, 801-821.

Culpin, I., Heuvelman, H., Rai, D., et al. (2022). Father absence and trajectories of offspring mental health across adolescence and young adulthood: Findings from a UK-birth cohort. Journal of Affective Disorders, 314, 150-159.

Markowitz, A. J., & Ryan, R. M. (2016). Father absence and adolescent depression and delinquency: A comparison of siblings approach. Journal of Marriage and the Family, 78, 1300-1314.

#4919. Bitcoin win rate vs. S&P 500 recovers as post-midterm history favors stocks

Bitcoin outperformed the S&P 500 in half of the past 90 US trading sessions, while historical midterm data highlights gains for stocks.

Bitcoin (BTC) has outperformed the S&P 500 in half of US trading sessions over Glassnode’s latest measurement period, ending a prolonged stretch of losing more sessions than it won.

Data from onchain analytics platform Glassnode shows that Bitcoin outperformed the S&P 500 in 50% of Wall Street trading sessions over a rolling 90-session period.

The reading, covering the 90 sessions through Wednesday, marks an improvement after an extended period in which Bitcoin lost more sessions than it won.

“The win rate has not been above 50% since March 2025; for most of the past year, Bitcoin lost more sessions than it won,” Glassnode wrote in the latest edition of its regular newsletter, The Week Onchain.

BTC/USD vs. S&P 500 per-session returns. Source: Glassnode

Glassnode added that Bitcoin’s average per-session gains were also “slightly higher” than those of the S&P 500.

Democrats’ wins in the US midterm elections have also drawn attention to historical stock market gains following similar results.

Data from crypto asset manager Capriole Investments shows that Democratic sweeps of Congress under Republican presidents have historically been followed by strong S&P 500 returns.

Related: Bitcoin hits $80.5K as US stocks rally on midterm election results

Of the 26 midterm elections since 1922 (excluding this year’s vote), Democrats won both the House and Senate under a Republican president seven times. The S&P 500 gained an average of 10.7% over the following three months.

Average gains were 17.5% after six months and 15.5% after a year.

“There is simply no better election result for markets than we just had,” Capriole founder Charles Edwards commented on the data in a post on X.

S&P 500 performance based on Midterm Election outcome. Source: Charles Edwards on X.com

Data from TradingView puts Bitcoin’s rolling 20-day correlation with the S&P 500 at 0.63. The measure has become markedly positive since August, when both crypto and stocks gained as the US Treasury expanded its debt buybacks.

BTC/USD one-day chart with S&P 500 correlation data. Source: Cointelegraph/TradingView

#4918. South Korea Faces Consequences of Tough Anti-Bullying Policy

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Tyler Woods

It’s been 27 years—over a quarter century--since the Columbine High School massacre ignited the worldwide war against bullying. Yet many nations that enthusiastically embrace this war discover that bullying becomes a more serious problem.

South Korea is one such country. It has made bullying a major cultural issue, producing a large number of popular “K-dramas” (Korean TV series) depicting the brutality of bullying, including the recent Netflix hit, Teach You a Lesson. Despite this massive anti-bullying activism (or perhaps because of it?), a 2025 survey found that South Korea's violence victimization rate in elementary school hit a record high.

A basic tenet of antibullyism is that bullying must not be tolerated and needs to be met with tough and consistent punishments, or “consequences.” Make the price of bullying too high, it is assumed, and any bully’s appetite to cause people pain will evaporate. Although it has become widely recognized that punishment is not an effective response to bullying,1 the belief in punishment continues unimpeded, probably because it seems intuitively correct to the average person, and perhaps because nothing else bullying researchers have presented seems to work reliably.

Thus, South Korea has adopted what sounds like a tough but logical consequence for bullying. Students with bullying on their records are disqualified from acceptance into public universities. This punishment causes a huge financial loss for students and can seriously impact their future. Given the importance Koreans place on higher education, certainly such a policy must be an effective deterrence to bullying, right?

Not if we consider human nature. Let’s say you get a traffic ticket. When will you fight it harder? When the fine is $50, with no points against your driver's license, or $50,000, and your license gets suspended? Well, the same thing is true if the huge punishments are for bullying violations, as recent news stories from South Korea confirm.

Before we continue, it's essential to recognize that the bullying that typically occurs in Korean schools does not resemble the bloodcurdling cruelty that is depicted in the K-dramas. Such behavior should result in incarceration. As in many other advanced nations, bullying in reality consists of any behaviors that can cause someone to get upset.

Students don’t suddenly become nicer because anti-bullying policies threaten them with severe educational penalties. Instead, they—their parents, really—fight back. An increasingly popular way is to countersue the educational system. As the title of one article in the Korean JoongAng Daily informs us, As consequences of bullying records grow, perpetrators turn to countersuits for leverage. It reveals the nightmare unleashed by these Korean laws. These lawsuits typically last years and cost tens of thousands of dollars.

To help the cover the expense, a growing number of parents in Korea are purchasing bullying insurance policies, as described in another article titled, As school violence becomes legal issue, insurers cash in. :

The number of insurance payouts related to school violence among five major nonlife insurers… surged from 231 cases in 2021 to 1,297 in 2022, 3,388 in 2023 and 3,443 in 2025. This represents a nearly fifteenfold rise in just five years.... The increasing pace has accelerated further this year, with 2,056 cases already recorded in January and February alone.

Furthermore, these lawsuits are being filed even for the most minor of bullying complaints. The Korea JoongAng article presents one parent’s experience with a situation that hardly meets the criteria of bullying:

One parent…started looking into a school violence insurance program after their fifth-grader was mired in a 25 million won ($16,580) lawsuit for repeatedly asking to borrow items and was accused of “having a look” at another student in their school.

The local education office concluded that no action was necessary, but the other student’s parent did not accept the decision and filed a damage lawsuit. The court dismissed the case in full last month.

After going through both the committee process and a civil lawsuit, the parent faced significant psychological and financial burdens, leading them to seek insurance coverage.

The purpose of anti-bullying laws is to reduce the misery caused by bullying. In South Korea, however, the misery of bullying is being dwarfed by the financial expense and psychological pain inflicted by prolonged, expensive lawsuits.

1. American Psychological Association. (2006, August 9). Zero tolerance policies are not as effective as thought, APA task force finds. https://www.apa.org/news/press/releases/2006/08/zero-tolerance

#4917. Thailand to block Bitget in December over unlicensed crypto trading

Thailand’s SEC filed a criminal complaint against Bitget’s operator, with access to its website and app set to be blocked from Dec. 5.

Thailand’s securities regulator has filed a criminal complaint against BG Limited, the operator of cryptocurrency exchange Bitget, for allegedly operating without a license.

Thailand’s Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) said Thursday that it had notified the Ministry of Digital Economy and Society to block access to Bitget from Dec. 5. The restriction will cover Bitget’s website, other associated domains and its mobile app.

The regulator advised users to take action regarding assets held on the platform before the deadline.

The complaint follows Bitget’s Oct. 20 announcement of plans to acquire a licensed Thai crypto exchange.

According to the SEC, Bitget offered crypto trading services between 2022 and 2025, charged trading fees and promoted its services in Thai through its websites and social media channels. The regulator said these activities violated Thailand’s digital asset business licensing requirements.

The complaint also names Kritsana Kritsananuwat, whom the SEC accused of helping promote Bitget through Facebook and YouTube. The watchdog previously filed a criminal complaint against him in March 2025 over allegations that he helped promote OKX’s unlicensed exchange activities.

Cointelegraph attempted to contact Kritsananuwat through a phone number listed for advertising inquiries in a video on his YouTube channel but was unable to reach him.

Thailand introduced licensing requirements for digital asset businesses in 2018 and tightened the rules in April 2025 to cover foreign platforms targeting Thai users. Changes to a separate cybercrime law also made it easier for the ministry to block access to unlicensed platforms.

Related: Thailand SEC says several companies eye crypto ETFs, none has applied

The regulator filed the complaint with Thailand’s Economic Crime Suppression Division for investigation. Prosecutors will decide whether to bring charges, while courts will determine criminal liability.

Bitget did not immediately respond to Cointelegraph’s request for comment.

Bitget announced plans on Oct. 20 to acquire GMO-Z.com Cryptonomics, a locally licensed crypto exchange and brokerage, through BTGT Management (Thailand), a company in which Bitget said it held an interest.

The proposed acquisition remained subject to regulatory approval and other conditions at the time of the disclosure. Bitget said it intended to introduce its trading platform to Thai users after completing the transaction and obtaining approval for the change of control.

“Thailand is an important part of Southeast Asia’s digital asset landscape, and this acquisition gives us a great opportunity to establish local operations,” Bitget CEO Gracy Chen said at the time.

#4916. Trump’s memecoin ventures derailed CLARITY Act, Scaramucci says

The SkyBridge founder said disputes over ethics restrictions also stalled negotiations, with both sides responsible for the setback.

SkyBridge Capital founder Anthony Scaramucci blamed US President Donald Trump’s crypto business interests and memecoin launches for derailing the CLARITY Act, saying they cost the legislation Democratic support.

Speaking with Cointelegraph at SALT London, Scaramucci said the memecoin launches shortly before Trump’s January 2025 inauguration undermined bipartisan momentum for crypto regulation following the 2024 election.

“Singular reason,” Scaramucci told Cointelegraph when asked whether the memecoin activities were the sole reason the bill did not pass. He said Democrats who had become more receptive to crypto regulation withdrew their support following the launches.

The remarks follow the Senate’s failure to advance the CLARITY Act on Sept. 15, when a procedural motion received 49 votes in favor and 50 against, falling short of the 60 votes required. The bill, which sought to establish a federal regulatory framework for digital assets, faced disagreements over ethics provisions restricting government officials’ crypto interests.

Scaramucci also pointed to disagreements over proposed ethics restrictions, saying lawmakers sought stronger provisions that the White House opposed.

“I think it’s the business interests, the memecoins,” Scaramucci said, adding that lawmakers were “trying to put real teeth into the ethics provision” but the White House did not want to sign it.

“There’s culpability on both sides for that deal not going forward,” he said.

Related: Market participants weigh in on SEC’s proposed crypto regulations

In July, five Democratic senators called for hearings into Trump’s crypto holdings and potential conflicts of interest, citing concerns that his financial ties could influence crypto policy. House Financial Services Committee Chair French Hill also acknowledged that Trump’s crypto businesses had complicated efforts to pass legislation.

In August, consumer advocacy group Public Citizen estimated that investors incurred at least $4.7 billion in largely unrealized losses across Trump-related crypto ventures, including $3.2 billion associated with the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin. The group said Trump earned $635 million in memecoin licensing fees in 2025, citing his financial disclosure.

Despite the legislative setback, Scaramucci said the SEC’s approach under Chair Paul Atkins could help tokenization reach “escape velocity,” with enough adoption to make it difficult for a future SEC chair to stop or slow the industry.

He said it was too early to know whether adoption would reach that point before the next administration.
