#4914. Can decentralized phone networks power the AI revolution?

Decentralized AI networks want to turn everyday phones into an alternative to centralized computing infrastructure — but skeptics have their doubts.

The sprawling data centers that underpin the AI revolution have become about as popular as baby toys made from asbestos.

Campaigners around the world highlight the massive amounts of energy and water the infrastructure requires, while markets worry about the revenue required to pay back the $38 billion cost of building a single, one-gigawatt AI data center.

But is there another way to power the AI revolution that’s sitting in your pocket right now?

As of October 2026, there are an estimated 7.68 billion mobile connections worldwide, and billions of smartphones are currently lying unused in pockets, on desks and in drawers.

Each one has a processor, memory and an internet connection, and represent a vast pool of computing power just waiting to be put to work.

So could a network of humble smartphones realistically power AI at scale?

Decentralized AI networks are emerging that aim to turn everyday smartphones into a distributed infrastructure layer, and reduce reliance on costly, centralized data centers.

Acurast says it has onboarded more than 335,000 smartphones to its network. Co-founder Alessandro De Carli says that around 40,000 of those are fully active on an average day, providing computing capacity for tasks ranging from web scraping and cybersecurity to AI inference and agent hosting.

Data center locations across the US. Source: World Resources Institute

Related: DePIN grows into a $10B sector despite token slump, Messari says

He says smartphones are particularly efficient for certain workloads. Their built-in hardware security features “excel” in areas like confidential computing or an “isolated environment” that can install and run executables. They are also well suited to workloads that are “CPU intensive,” or which require wide distribution and “unfiltered access” to the internet,

“Servers are better at multi-threading, but smartphones offer unmatched compute efficiency,” De Carli tells Magazine.

While ordinary smartphones have limited memory, and big servers retain an advantage when handling multiple tasks in parallel, he says many workloads can still fit within the memory available.

Taken as a whole, the active phones on the network “match the compute capacity of about 2,340 high-end data-center servers,” he claims, benchmarked against the ASUS RS720A-E11, a high-density server built for artificial intelligence and high-performance computing (HPC).

Mobile connectivity in 2026. Source: Datareportal

Another project, Phase, launched Phase Proxy in October, which allows smartphone owners to provide internet connectivity and blockchain validation for AI agents. Founder Kirill Eves explains that, “websites routinely block or rate-limit automated traffic originating from data centers,” so the system employs users’ mobile and residential connections as a proxy instead. Phase plans to charge 0.5 USDT per 1,000 requests and 1GB of traffic.

The next step is Phase Router which will direct simpler requests to models running on phones in the network, or route them instead to major external providers like OpenAI and Anthropic, based on “task complexity, model capabilities, performance and cost.”

The network could eventually expand beyond smartphones to desktops, servers and GPUs, he says.

But Markus Levin, co-founder of decentralized infrastructure provider XYO, says decentralized networks may be better suited to collecting and verifying real-world data rather than running AI models.

Phones and sensors can provide localized information that centralized datasets miss, he says, but only if the data is accurate and resistant to manipulation.

“A useful decentralized AI network should be judged by whether it solves a problem customers will pay to fix, not by its token price or node count,” he says.

One of the biggest challenges with mobile phone networks is coordinating the devices to provide predictable hardware, power and connectivity, in the same way data centers do.

A 2025 study tested small groups of smartphones carrying out image recognition tasks and found that clusters of phones could deliver competitive performance in certain conditions, but the results came from a controlled testing environment.

Related: Web3 companies turn to hardware with crypto-powered phones, consoles

A Google-backed project at the University of California, San Diego found that a cluster of 20 retired smartphones could handle the peak computing workload of a class of more than 75 students, with grading times faster than the university’s standard cloud setup. However, those devices were also organized in a controlled environment, not scattered across the world.

In the real world, a distributed network has to contend with phones that disconnect, overheat, run out of battery, change networks, and have varying amounts of processing power and memory.

In a blockchain network like Ethereum for example, the capacity is currently limited by how long it takes to communicate across the network, and the processing abilities of the lowest spec devices.

20 retired smartphones could handle a class of more than 75 students. Source: Google Research

Similarly, when tasks are split between several phones, slow connections or disconnected devices can throw a spanner in the works. Distributing separate, independent tasks across available phones is generally more straightforward.

Even if the technical challenges can be overcome, networks still need to make financial sense for customers and device owners alike.

Using existing smartphone hardware doesn’t incur the high capital costs of building a data center, but costs include factors like connectivity, coordination and incentives for people on the network.

When it comes to compensation, De Carli says there is “no fixed figure” because rewards for Acurast participants vary by “device uptime, hardware specifications (benchmark performance), total ACU staked and active compute demand” from developers. He says operators compete for a pool of inflation-funded rewards, rather than receiving direct payments from customers.

Phase network rewards depend on uptime, participation and customer demand.

“Phase will use a portion of its service revenue to purchase PHASE on the open market and distribute it to node operators based on their contributions to the network,” Eves says.

Running an AI model to run properly on a phone is no longer the main challenge — getting the economics to stack up while coordinating tens of thousands of phones to behave like a reliable cloud service is the real trick.

#4913. How to Transform Anger Into Peace

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 3, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

We may know intellectually that anger can be destructive. We may be aware that it has the potential for deeply negative consequences, and yet we cannot seem to avoid feeling angry. It’s natural to view anger as a huge problem, but what if there is another way of seeing it?

The reality is that we will never get rid of anger. Anger is an emotion, and all emotions are essential to the human experience and can be beneficial when used skillfully.

What if you could learn to reveal the wisdom beneath your anger? What if, by understanding anger’s real meaning, you could gain awareness, inner strength, and true peace?

Think of anger as your own internal GPS. Whenever it arises, it alerts you to a problem and gives you important information about what to do next. When you tune in to anger and use it as an internal guidance system, you can use it to redirect your intentions and actions.

Anger can be the gateway to clarity, equanimity, and even happiness. It’s an opportunity to grow. When you pay attention to the message anger carries, you can transform every aspect of your life. You can become a better parent, spouse, friend, community member, and leader.

Embracing your anger takes courage, but it allows you to tap into your dark side and transform what seems unacceptable into something positive. Pursuing the darkest side of anger will lead you to the brightest light within you. The key is to work with anger instead of against it, moving toward anger rather than resisting it or pushing it away.

When we get angry, it’s not easy to pay attention to subtleties, analyze a situation objectively, or bear unpleasant or hurtful situations. So how do we transform anger into equanimity? Through mindfulness and compassion. Since the mind gives rise to the experience of anger, it is the mind itself that can work with anger. Both neuroscience and psychology show us that compassion is what transforms the mind and that mindfulness is the instrument that carries this change.

Jon Kabat-Zinn defines mindfulness as "paying attention in a particular way: on purpose, in the present moment, and nonjudgmentally.” The practice of mindfulness is the key tool for dealing with anger. Thich Nhat Hanh advises, “Give your anger, your despair, your fear a bath of mindfulness every day.” Being mindful of anger means opening your consciousness to anger and illuminating it with a soft light to gently, honestly, and compassionately investigate it.

The human mind is designed to automatically evaluate and analyze. We are evolutionarily designed and motivated to place judgments on everything we experience. Being nonjudgmental in our attention means noticing and sensing what is going on within our own mind and body in relationship to reality without preferences, choices, or actions. Nonjudgment puts us on the path toward accepting our experiences, whether they are pleasant or unpleasant.

Let us look at how nonjudgmental mindfulness applies to anger. When we get angry, there is an initial discomfort, shame, pain, fear, or rejection. Because of this reaction, we tend to quickly move to a position of defensive judgment. Judging the events and the other people involved is a way of making ourselves protective and powerful. Anger is a potent defensive response, but it comes at a cost. It narrows our perception, limiting us from seeing more perspectives and possibilities. The judgment that arises from anger tends to be biased and distorted. This is why it is important to practice nonjudgmental mindfulness: It adds a purifying lens that takes away our bias, distortions, and ignorance to bring us back to “clean” reality.

Practicing nonjudgmental mindfulness allows you to see your interior and exterior reality clearly. It reveals valuable pieces of information, thoughts, feelings, and body sensations you might not have noticed before. It allows you to become conscious of how overwhelming the flood of anger is and how quickly and forcefully you may have been captured by it. With practice, you’ll begin to recognize anger more quickly when it arises and to observe it as it builds. This enables you to regain control of your actions before anger erupts in hurtful words or aggressive behavior. You’ll be able to act with wisdom, maintaining balanced thoughts, emotions, and actions that are proportional to the situation.

True anger transformation requires compassion. Cultivating compassion involves engaging both your attitude and your actions. It’s a matter of simultaneously strengthening your mental capacities and developing the courage to care for others.

Compassion is the quality of being attuned to people and attending to their needs. It involves the desire to be moved by suffering while motivating us to alleviate it, and even prevent it. Genuine compassion is a deep, heartfelt concern for the well-being of others.

Compassion invites you to break down the walls that have been constructed by fear and mistrust, to cross the borders and unite with someone you once considered an adversary. A compassionate heart allows you to accept others as they are, while self-compassion allows you to tolerate all of your feelings, even the difficult ones such as anger, fear, jealousy, and resentment.

When you are angry, remind yourself that others usually are doing their best—however mediocre it may seem—given their own ability, skills, situation, and limitations. When you avoid judging others who have done something “wrong” and instead try to understand why they did what they did in the first place, you develop compassion toward that person. When you adopt this attitude, you move from criticism to understanding, from separation to unity, and from anger to grace.

Ratson, M. (2023). Anger Is Your Compass.

#4908. Blockchain is ‘perfect solution’ for AI agent trust, VeChain CEO says

Sunny Lu said AI agents would need identity and reputation systems, making the case while promoting agent-building platform VeWorld.ai.

AI agents could drive blockchain’s next adoption cycle by creating demand for systems to verify their identities and reputations, according to VeChain co-founder and CEO Sunny Lu.

Autonomous AI agents operating around the clock would require a “new accountability system” to build public trust, Lu said during Cointelegraph’s Chain Reaction show on Wednesday.

“The perfect solution is a blockchain,” he said.

Lu made the case while discussing VeWorld.ai, an agent-building platform that uses VeChain’s blockchain.

In his vision, agents would have digital “passports” and operate within a “decentralized credibility system” that tracks their reputations and those of their creators. Smart contracts could help settle transactions between agents or between agents and people.

VeChain has also pursued blockchain adoption through supply-chain tracking, including food traceability projects.

AI agents could also help people turn their expertise into businesses or services, extending software-building opportunities to non-developers, according to Lu.

He compared the opportunity to mobile app stores, which allowed individual developers to distribute and monetize their work. Agent-building tools could open that market to people whose skills lie outside programming, including those with specialist professional experience, local knowledge or hobbies.

His own experiments included an educational agent for his daughter, an agent to support his mother’s daily routine and recovery, and a virtual employee to manage purchasing, inventory, sales and operations for an acquaintance’s shop in Milan.

Other industry participants have highlighted the risks of relying on agent providers.

In an October interview, Animoca Brands chairman Yat Siu told Cointelegraph that crypto traders could put AI agents to work around the clock but warned that users risked building their trading strategies around services they did not control.

#4907. Here’s what happened in crypto today

Need to know what happened in crypto today? Here is the latest news on daily trends and events impacting Bitcoin price, blockchain, DeFi, Web3 and crypto regulation.

Today in crypto, France’s National Assembly sidelined stablecoin swap taxes by rejecting the budget’s revenue section, Democrats wasted little time threatening investigations into President Donald Trump’s business circle and Big Tech executives after sweeping the US midterm elections and Bitcoin rebounded above $80,000 as US stocks rallied on the results.

The French National Assembly rejected the 2027 state budget’s revenue section late Wednesday, removing crypto-related amendments from the version of the bill sent to the Senate.

The Assembly rejected the revenue section by a vote of 458–0 at first reading, with two abstentions, according to the official voting record. The revenue section covers government income, such as taxes.

After the rejection, the government will send the Senate the original budget text, without the crypto-related amendments adopted by lawmakers.

The proposals could still be reintroduced in the Senate and would need to survive the remaining budget process to become law.

Faustine Fleuret, global head of public affairs at lending protocol Morpho, told Cointelegraph before the vote that rejection of the revenue section could “result in the rejection of the entire bill in the National Assembly, including provisions adopted by way of amendments.”

Democratic lawmakers said Big Tech executives should expect a wave of subpoenas, along with investigations into US President Donald Trump’s business circle when the new Congress takes office in January.

As Democrats secured control of Congress in a sweeping victory on Tuesday, Democratic lawmakers were already firing warning shots at the Trump family and tech executives close to the US president.

“They are going to need to plant new forests for all of the subpoenas we’ll be printing,” said US Senator Ed Markey, who sits on the Senate Commerce Committee, referring to Big Tech executives.

US Representative Robert Garcia, the expected incoming House Oversight Committee chairman, said he plans to take on “the Trump Crime Family, corporate corruption, DHS and ICE abuses, and the cover-up of the Epstein files,” adding a two-word warning: “Lawyer up.”

The warnings follow a mid-term election campaign in which Democrats challenged Big Tech’s growing political influence and the rapid expansion of AI, raising concerns about data centers’ demands on electricity, safeguards for AI products and tech executives’ ties to Trump.

Bitcoin (BTC) returned to $80,000 at Wednesday’s Wall Street open as US stocks surged following a Democratic sweep in the midterm elections.

Data from TradingView showed BTC/USD up by over 4%, having rebounded from its lowest levels in seven weeks.

Data from CoinGlass showed the price dip liquidating late BTC long positions below $76,000, while a wall of asks kept upside contained below $78,000. Total crypto long liquidations over the past 24 hours stood at $288 million.

US stock futures made swift gains on Wednesday after initially dipping overnight. S&P 500 futures fell as low as 7,490 points, their lowest level since July 31, before reaching 7,690 points, approaching new month-to-date highs.

In comments sent to Cointelegraph, James Butterfill, head of research at crypto asset manager CoinShares, saw the potential for a wider Bitcoin “relief rally” as election uncertainty faded.

#4906. 3 Things to Know Before Entering an Age-Gap Relationship

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

Age-gap relationships are by no means a new phenomenon, but they are something that modern society still struggles to make sense of—often denigrated and dismissed as a fleeting “phase.” Prejudicial terms such as “gold digger,” “cougar,” and “manther” are used to describe some of the forms age-differentiated relationships can take, with the burden of shame shouldered primarily by women.

At the same time, there is a growing movement demanding more respect and inclusivity of relationships that don’t fit the traditional mold, as how we imagine and define the “ideal relationship” is expanding at a rapid pace.

What can we learn from scientific research to help us understand the nuances of age-gap relationships? Here are three insights to guide your thinking.

When it comes to any scientific conversation on sex and relationships, it is important to first consider their evolutionary function: perpetuating the species. From this standpoint, it is not surprising that men have a preference for women in their child-bearing prime.

In a 2012 paper published in Human Nature, evolutionary psychologists Sascha Schwarz and Manfred Hassebrauck write, “The ability to reproduce is not equal across the lifespan. Women cannot give birth to their own children after the onset of menopause. Unlike women, men are not directly restricted biologically in their reproductive abilities.”

It’s also not surprising that women have a preference for men who are able to invest resources into child-rearing. Schwarz and Hassebrauck state, “Women invest more in their offspring (e.g., gestation) than men. Therefore, women prefer committed, long-lasting relationships and seek partners who are able and willing to invest in them and their potential offspring.”

Putting these two facts together, we can see why age-differentiated relationships, when they occur, favor a scenario where the male partner is older than the female partner. Younger women are more reproductively fit, and older men have more resources to invest in their family and children.

This conclusion is borne out by research. A classic 1945 study found that men prefer partners about 2.5 years younger than they are, while women prefer partners about 3.5 years older. Recent research has replicated this pattern.

When we look beyond an individual’s “ideal” partner age and instead ask what they deem acceptable, things get more interesting. For instance, one study found that men, on average, are accepting of relationships with women up to approximately 10 years younger and 4.5 years older. Women, on the other hand, are accepting of relationships with men up to 8 years older and five years younger.

But there’s an important caveat, and it has to do with how these “acceptable” limits change as we age.

“As men grow older, they accept even younger women, but their tolerated age span regarding the oldest partner they would accept is unrelated to their own age,” state the researchers. “On the other hand, women tend to accept younger men as they grow older, but the oldest partner they will accept decreases as they age.”

According to the authors, women are less likely to enter relationships with older men as they get older because longevity favors women, not men. In Germany, for instance, the average lifespan for women is five years longer than it is for men.

Perhaps the most important question revolves around the happiness people experience in age-gap relationships. The first and most compelling insight is that relationships of any make or model can be happy relationships under the right circumstances. However, science offers clues on the combinations that seem to work best.

One study published in the Journal of Population Economics found that both men and women seem to be happier with younger, not older, spouses. However, this happiness advantage may be short-lived.

The authors write, “Marital satisfaction declines with marital duration for both men and women in differently aged couples relative to those in similarly aged couples. These relative declines erase the initial higher levels of marital satisfaction experienced by men married to younger wives and women married to younger husbands.”

To explain this, the researchers suggest that differently aged couples may be less resilient to relationship obstacles than similarly aged couples. However, other research suggests that age-gap couples who stay together experience less jealousy and exhibit a more unselfish form of love than age-similar couples.

Science reveals a strong evolutionary basis for the existence of age-gap relationships. Science also suggests that these sometimes socially taboo relationships are not without their unique set of challenges. But when done right, they can be just as fulfilling and exhilarating as any other type of relationship.

A version of this post also appears on Forbes.com.

#4900. New Ways to Discover Hope and Possibility

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

These days, some of us are experiencing less hope than we would like. While hope is an important positive emotion, it may be the least studied component of well-being (Edwards et al., 2025; Graham & Pozuelo, 2023). Yet it is vital for resilience in many areas of our lives and can help to buffer us during difficult times.

A large-scale 2026 study of 25,000 adults over a 14-year period shows important links between hope and people’s contemporary and future outcomes in an array of life dimensions (Graham & Mujcic, 2026). According to the authors, this key study is the first large-scale research that shows a linkage between hope and several long-term outcomes. The researchers found that people with high hope levels show improvements in education, economic outcomes, resilience, and the ability to adapt. Their research shows less likelihood for loneliness, along with improvements in perceived wellbeing and objective measures of health. In addition, according to the study, hope can function as a psychological buffer during challenging times.

According to the researchers, “We find a strong link between high levels of hope and better contemporary and future outcomes in the wellbeing, economic, health and social arenas…” (Graham & Mujcic, 2026).

Hope is not simply wishing things will get better. Instead, hope involves determination and taking action to make things better (Feldman et al., 2026; Snyder, 1994). Snyder’s framework of hope defines it as setting a goal, devising strategies to achieve it (pathways), and developing motivation to reach that goal (agency). According to Feldman et al. (2026), mindfulness supplements the hope process by grounding us in awareness of the present. This can help stabilize our ability to pay attention to our goals, which can enhance intentionality.

Cognitive psychologist Michelle Chappel, in her book, The Power of Authenticity, reminds readers to “dwell in possibilities” and to “try on something new,” encouraging us to continue learning and growing throughout our lives (2026, p. 131 & 134).

Our attention is a vital component of hope, as Feldman, Shapiro, and Dreher (2026) explain in a new study on the convergence of hope and mindfulness. The researchers find that although mindfulness and hope have distinct roles and mechanisms, both are positively associated with psychological well-being. Moreover, mindfulness may support inner resources and skills that can help us set goals and navigate pathways toward reaching our goals, which are key aspects of hope.

Opening to the realm of hope and possibilities can be useful in any season of life, from young adulthood to mid-adulthood to older adulthood. Many of us are interested in gaining fuller understandings about how to live the best lives we can at any stage – learning strategies to thrive in the face of our vulnerabilities and whatever life throws at us.

Engaging in mindfulness and harnessing actionable hope can be important strategies contributing to the ability to adapt, live with well-being, and access life’s possibilities.

#4892. The Child's Self-Concept: A Social Construct

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

Scientists haven’t found, nor are they likely to find, specific genes that turn on or off and, by doing so, construct the child’s self-concept in a predetermined manner. Absent a purely genetic cause, how does the child develop their self-concept, this crucial entity that delineates the parameters around the child’s needs, their choices, a ceiling on accomplishments, and gauges the child’s interactions with others and life’s smorgasbord of circumstances?

Decades ago, developmental psychologists Alexander, Thomas, and Chess observed that at birth, children come biologically furnished with three innate behavioral predispositions or “temperamental styles”: The “flexible,” adaptive child, the reactive, “feisty,” or not-so-adaptive child, and the “easy,” calm, unhurried child.

Notwithstanding these innate predispositions, how parents welcome, don’t welcome, or otherwise interpret their child’s basic temperament is critical to building the child’s self-concept. For example, a “feisty” child can be seen as a nuisance, irritating, as demanding discipline, and thus reaps parental discouragement or punishment. Or feistiness might be seen as a zest for life, a driving curiosity for novel, stimulating experience, or a precursor to later-life ambition, which would garner parental approval and encouragement—likely accompanied by a few guardrails.

Moreover, the meaning parents impose upon the child’s innate predispositions, and the behavior emanating from them, molds parental attitudes and actions, which, in turn, shapes how the child conceives of themself.

Respected attachment theorist John Bowlby posited that human infants use primitive, inborn cues, like smiling, reaching, cooing, and crying, to elicit nurturing from caregivers, who reciprocate by feeding, comforting, and soothing. These “interactional circuitries” create the fundamentals for a mother-infant attachment bond within the first 18 months of the infant’s life, a time Bowlby called “the sensitive phase.”

When this dyadic bond remains relatively constant and strong over the course of development, the infant or child internalizes a cognitive schema or “working model” of what to expect from subsequent relationships. This bond is the cornerstone of the child’s sense of their worth, the gold standard of their personal standing vis-à-vis others.

For instance, imagine a small child falling, scraping their knee, and crying out in pain, which summons the ministrations of an emotionally attuned mother, who tends to the wound and then follows up with a warm, consoling hug. When reasonably consistent, this type of empathic attunement can be counted upon to nurture maternal trust and the child’s sense of themself as worthy of care. The child’s capacity for interpersonal trust is thus assembled by these early, prototypical interactions, which encourage the child’s prosocial behavior.

The term “person-o-typic affect” (PTA) sounds like a mini-word circus, but a parent’s understanding of the concept can bring significant insight to the relationship they have with their child. Defined literally, PTA refers to the strongest feeling that characteristically surfaces within a parent when in the presence of their child. Despite their strength, parents are often unaware of these feelings; they may be subtle or subconscious, lying outside their immediate awareness. They can also be impossible to ignore, where parents are keenly aware of their presence and the strong influence they wield over their child. But aware or not, these feelings can significantly alter how the parent relates to their child and, consequently, how the child relates to themselves.

For example, a father feels guilty about divorcing his ex-wife because it seemed to shatter his son’s sense of emotional stability. During visitations with his son, guilt warps the father’s behavior through overcompensation, such as granting unearned privileges or turning a blind eye to inappropriate behavior, which are inconsistent with his son’s concept of himself as capable and responsible.

Person-o-typic affect can be an insightful, useful tool for understanding the psychological forces behind parent-child behavior, especially where parental feelings are intense and beg examination. Pertinent questions for parents include: Where do my feelings come from? Do they need modification? How do they influence my behavior and my child’s self-concept?

Need management therapy (NMT) confidently proposes a simple but effective blueprint for building the child’s self-concept. In outline form, it consists of three concrete, doable steps:

1. Need Identification: From the early, seminal moments of life, as loving parents identify their child’s needs, construction of the child’s self-concept begins. And because their needs have been seen by their parents and significant others, the child learns they, too, can identify their own needs and therefore themselves, who they are.

2. Need Legitimization: NMT posits that at their most basic, irreducible level, the child’s needs are valid. For example, the child’s need to be sensitively, respectfully understood is a fundamentally valid need. When the child’s needs are thus “crowned” with validity, a foundation is laid for a positive self-concept. And the child is likely to confidently represent their needs and who they are.

3. Need Representation: Because the child’s basic needs are legitimate, they “demand” representation. Poor or non-management of a legitimate need is inconsistent with building and preserving a complete, healthy self-concept. Under parental mentorship, the child learns to manage their needs by balancing respect for themselves and others, expressing their needs and associated feelings, and measuring the success of these efforts not by how others respond but by how well they represent their needs—difficult but growth-spurring.

By a close adherence to this blueprint, parents can help their child construct a robust self-concept.

Bowlby, J. (`980). Attachment and Loss. Vol. 3, Loss, Sadness and Depression. London: Hogarth Press.
